Executive Summary
i
Preface

The title of the 2022 K -Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR is the “National
Master Plan for STI R&D and Application in Lao PDR.” As a three -year project (2020 -
2022), the project aims to support the development of the National STI Master Plan of
Lao PDR. For the last two years, our STEPI -MTC(Ministry of Technology and ICT in Lao
PDR) team has focused on analyzing the national innovation system in Lao PDR and
sharing STI development experiences and current issues of both countries. In 2021, our
team particularly explored public STI research institutes, higher education and university
systems, and the STI roles in the agriculture and biotech sectors of both Korea and Lao
PDR, and published the report, which was presented to the national government of Lao
PDR.
In order to develop a framework and drafts of the National STI Master Plan of Lao PDR
by the end of the final year of the project, STEPI and MTC collaboration have placed the
top two priorities in 2022 in 1) policy consultation in three strategic areas —technology
commercialization, SME innovation, and international STI collaboration —, 2) capacity
building of Laotian government officials in STI policy planning through a series of
workshops and seminars held throughout the year of 2022 in Korea and Lao PDR.
This report is the output of a year -long collaboration between STEPI and MTC on STI
policy consultation and a series of capacity -building workshops. Starting with an
extensive introduction of the National S&T Basic Plan in Korea in Chapter 1, the report
provides policy analyses in three strategic areas of technology commercialization, SME
innovation, and international STI collaboration of both Lao PDR and Korea from Chapter
2 to Chapter 7 . Each chapter explores the international and domestic environments of
the designated strategic area and identifies challenges and opportunities in the area by
examining statistical data, governance, stakeholders and required resources and current

===== PDF_PAGE 4 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
ii
policy issues of both countries. In particular, those chapters about each policy area in
Lao PDR provide drafts of each policy area of the National Master Plan for STI R&D and
Application. The Draft of the National Master Plan is summarized in Chapter 9. Given the
significance of highly skilled STI human resources in the context of national development,
in addition, it is addressed how the Korean government dealt with the problem of STI
brain drain through the advanced S&T institution building in Chapter 8 . All these
chapters on Korea and Lao PDR deliver valuable information for mutual learning and
insightful messages for the implementation of the National STI Master Plan presented
in this report.
As highly recognized several times already by local media for its work on identifying
strategic areas of the STI master plan and developing a comprehensive framework of STI
development, the K -Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR has contributed to
defining the STI roles aligned with the national socio-economic development framework.
Despite the end of our joint project in December 2022, the STEPI is going to support
further developments and implementation of the STI master plan of Lao PDR through
continuous communications with its partner MTC. Finally, I would like to express my
deepest gratidue to all the experts who participated in our bilateral project for hard
work and enhthusiasm.

Dr. Inkyoung Sun, Research Fellow, STEPI

Chapter 1. National S&T Basic Plan in Korea
3

Dr. Sungchul Chung, CEO, Wonjung Institute

1. Introduction

 Overview
National development plan is a formal document that presents the visions and goals of
development of a nation as well as a systematic and comprehensive set of strategies to
attain the goals, along with the plans for the mobilization of resources required for the
implementation of the plan. A national development plan, in general, comprises three
components: (1) At the aggregate level, t he national planning authority draws up a
macroeconomic plan; (2) The macroeconomic plan p rovides a kind of direction or
guidance for sectoral ministries to draft sectoral plans; and (3) To put the plans into
action, sectoral ministries prepare project -level investment plans, in consultation with
the planning authority. Science, technology and innovation (STI) policy plan is a sectoral
plan that focuses on the issues related to the development and promotion of science,
technology and innovation.
Basically, governments formulate and implement development plans as a means for
government intervention in social and economic development. The need for national
development plans is particularly emphasized in the case of developing economies,
where governm ent intervention is required to make up for the deficiency of private
sectors’ capability. James Wolfensohn, former President of the World Bank stressed that
“All developing countries should have a comprehensive development framework as they
need more integrated approach to development based on a framework articulated and
owned by the country itself” (Mallaby, 2004, pp. 232 -260). In the same vein,

===== PDF_PAGE 16 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
4
governments draw up and carry out STI plans as a tool for systematic intervention in the
processes of STI development and related policy areas.
Korea’s development planning exercise started with the Five -year Economic
Development Plan which was first launched in 1962 and concluded with the completion
of the Seventh Plan in 1996. The plans provided not only clear directions for national
development but also inspired the Korean people to actively engage in the processes of
economic development for more than three decades. Each of the seven plans, from the
first to the seventh, was geared to attaining a self -sustainable economy, even though
the priori ties of the plans varied in response to the changes in internal needs and
external environments. STI has been in the core of the Korean development strategy
from the very beginning. The First Five-year Development Plan was supplemented by a
separate plan o n STI development (the Five -year Technology Promotion Plan, 1962 -
1966), and science and technology has since been on the top of the development agenda
throughout the entire development period.
This report is to review and draw policy lessons from Korea’s policy experience in
planning STI development, with a focus on the Science and Technology Framework Plan
which was launched first in 2002. The Science and Technology Framework Plan is
unique in several ways: (1) It is a plan that has its own legal base – the Framework Act
on Science and Technology (2001); (2) Even though it is a sectoral plan, it is not subject
to the directions or guidelines of any higher -level plans, and thus sets its own visi ons
and goals to pursue; (3) It is a government-wide plan designed and formulated through
inter-ministerial cooperation and consultation; (4) It is a plan to prepare Korea for the
new stage of development or ‘post -catchup’ stage, where, unlike at the catch up stage,
creative research becomes more important; and (5) It is a five-year plan that provides a
mid-term visions for STI development and strategies to attain the visions, and therefore,
it is more about providing an overall vison and directions of STI policy rather than setting
specific scientific and/or technological targets.
The Science and Technology Framework Plan (2002 -2006) is Korea’s attempt to design
an S&T policy plan free from the frameworks and/or directions of higher -level plans or

===== PDF_PAGE 17 =====
Chapter 1. National S&T Basic Plan in Korea
5
policy, and therefore offers unique lessons for those seeking to prepare an S&T policy
plan not stringed to other national development plans. This report begins with a
discussion on the ratio nale for national development planning, including STI planning,
structure of STI plan, and what it requires to formulate and implement S&T plans
successfully. Then we review the Korean experience in formulating the S&T Framework
Plan (2001-2006), covering discussions on how STI planning began in Korea and how it
had evolved over time, as well as the planning processes and structure of the plan. Then
finally, we assess the achievements of the first S&T Framework Plan, evaluate the overall
performance of the plan and conclude the report by deriving policy lessons and issues.

===== PDF_PAGE 18 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
6

2. Why STI Plan?
 Development Planning: Pros and Cons
Many newly independent countries in the 1950s and 1960s resorted to development
planning as a major tool for national development. Then, economic theory was
interpreted more in favor of market failure, supporting an interventionist economic
policy. In ideological terms, the post-World War period saw a broad consensus in favor
of state intervention in the econ omy on both sides of the Cold War, largely because of
the weakness or lack of the capacity of the private sectors in developing countries. 1
They also found superiority of interventionist economic policy in the success of the
Marshall Plan in rebuilding the Western European economy.
The effectiveness of the interventionist policies of the newly independent countries
based on development planning, however, was far short of sustaining the supports for
the interventionist arguments. Beginning in the 1970s, disappointment with the
interventionist approach toward development began to spread. Some argue that plan -
based development efforts are bound to fail because plans distort resource allocation
by replacing more efficient private players with failing state agencies (Agarwala, 1983;
Bauer, 1982). The argument was confirmed by the collapse of the Soviet bloc around the
end of the 1980s and the remarkable economic success of China’s adoption of a market-
oriented economic system. Most of the former Soviet republics abandoned their
planning sys tems in the 1990s and early 2000s (Yazlyyev, 2017). Low -income, aid -
dependent countries also took similar actions in the 1980s and 1990s: Many national
planning agencies and economic development ministries were abolished or merged into
other ministries, and their status was marginalized in terms of power, stafﬁng, budget
and relevance to policy. After two or so decades of the plan-based policy exercise, in the
1970s and 1980s, they began to lean more toward the concepts of government failure

1 The success of Soviet industrialization after ﬁve -year plans started in 1928 was proclaimed as evidence of the
superiority of planned over free market economies (Baran, 1962).

===== PDF_PAGE 19 =====
Chapter 1. National S&T Basic Plan in Korea
7
and rent-seeking activities caused by government intervention in the economy than the
market failure arguments.
Despite the increased disillusionment with development planning, many countries still
rely on planning for the promotion of development. Some of the fast -growing
economies, such as China, Malaysia, India and others maintain their national planning
systems, while seeking free trade and the globalization of the economic systems. It
appears that they do so because unlike the Western countries, they find the sources of
the failure of development planning in the lack of policy capacity of the government –
lack o f political will, inadequate information, inability to grasp the actual needs and
capabilities of the economy, weak planning capacity, etc. rather than in government
failure (Faber & Seers, 1972). The proponents of planning argue that government
intervention is inevitable, if and when private sectors are not capable of dealing with
the problems they face in the market (because of the lack of information, improper
institutions, immature systems, and others) and/or market fails to work because of
externality, imperfect information, uncertainty, spillovers, transaction costs, etc.
Entering into the 1990s, economic theory became more in favor of state intervention in
the economic affairs. Many economic theorists point out new flaws in the idea of market
mechanism that makes optimal resource allocation, and argue that government
responsibility goes beyond the “night -watchman state” (Barr, 1992; Kahneman, 2011;
Thaler & Sunstein, 2009; North, 1990). Interestingly, Bates (1995) said that “an older
generation (of scholars and advocates) who had emphasized the importance of market
failures in development economics ﬁnds in the new institutionalism new justiﬁcation for
their interventionist beliefs” (p. 27). However, this is by no means in line with the old
generation theories supporting the old-style central economic planning. They argue that
new forms of state intervention are in order, which is a new trend in defiance of the
neo-liberalism of the 1980s.
The World Bank which had led the drive toward neo -liberalism for more than 20 years,
came up with a survey result in 2007 that 62 countries had development plans
(Chimhowu et al., 2019). In a 2016 –17 survey that replicated the World Bank survey,
they found that the number of countries that had national development plans more

===== PDF_PAGE 20 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
8
than doubled to 134 (Chimhowu et al., 2019). The spread of national development plans
over the recent decade is a notable policy trend that requires explanation and
exploration. Chimhowu et al. (2019) found three reasons for the re -emergence of
national plans2: (1) to meet the conditions for development aids; (2) to respond to the
SDGs of 2000; and (3) to meet the challenges and reap the opportunities of globalization.
In essence, the re -emergence of national plans suggests that many countries have no
better options than relying on state-initiated planning to initiate and implement national
efforts to enhance the standard of living of the people under the ever -changing global
environments. This is particularly so for the countries, where the private sectors lack
capacity to navigate the fast-changing markets.

 Why and How governments intervene in STI
STI policy refers to government measures designed for the creation, funding, support,
and mobilization of resource for science, technology and innovation. STI policy covers a
very broad range of activities including the production, diffusion, and utilizati on of
knowledge, in both public and private sectors. The scope, aims, subjects, and
mechanisms of STI policies have varied widely over time.
Why should governments intervene in the affairs related to STI? Various schools of STI
policy offer diverse rationales for government intervention in STI processes, but the
most widely accepted one derives from the neo -classical economic theory. The neo -
classical economics shows that market is bound to fail in providing optimal solutions if
market transactions involve the problems of externality, uncertainty, indivisibility,
and/or appropriability. STI policy or government intervention in STI is justified b y the
theoretical prediction that market is bound to fail in making efficient resource allocation
because:
(1) social returns to STI investment exceed private returns, and therefore private players
tend to invest less than social optimality requires, and
(2) STI involves uncertainty, and therefore, private players tend to invest in R&D and
innovation only when expected returns exceed opportunity costs.

2 Chimhowu et al. (2019) suggest four reasons, but in fact two of them are about dealing with the SDGs of 2000

===== PDF_PAGE 21 =====
Chapter 1. National S&T Basic Plan in Korea
9

For optimal solution, therefore, the theory requires government to step in and correct
the market failure by providing subsidies or other forms of assistance.
According to the national innovation system theory, innovation results from interactions
between and among innovation players, say, universities, business enterprises, and
research organizations, and therefore, the theory emphasizes the importance of
systems linkage, such as industry -science interface or industry -university cooperation.
The issue is that in the absence of government intervention, the system may fail, keeping
new knowledge generated by universities from being utilized by industry, due to the
asymmetry of information among the innovation players. To promote the interactions
between and among innovation players, government may provide facilities and/or
programs, such as ‘techno -polis’ that provides physical and institutional conditions for
collaboration between the creators and users of knowledge/technology.
Government interventions in STI, in general, aim at providing the following:
(1) Infrastructure for STI:
• Creation of and support for STI institutions and building STI capacities
• Provision of rules and/or institutions for the promotion of STI: IPR system,
information system, standards, etc.
• Promotion of industry -science interface: promotion of clusters, techno -polis,
etc.

(2) International STI linkages:
• Provision of institutional and/or political base for international STI linkages with
foreign countries
• Diplomatic supports for international STI cooperation
• Promoting and facilitating participation in global STI activities

(3) Provision of environments conducive to innovation:
• Provision of stable social and economic policy framework conducive to long -
term R&D and innovation: economic and social stability, STI awareness of the
society, etc.
• Promotion of market competition and exposure of domestic industries to
international competition.

===== PDF_PAGE 22 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
10
• Provision of financial and non -financial assistance to encourage and facilitate
private investments in R&D and innovation

Policy measures for the promotion of industrial STI can also be categorized into: (1)
policy measures to strengthen STI supply capacity; (2) creation of the demand for
technologies; and (3) actions to strengthen the foundation for STI development.
Government can promote industrial STI activities by providing direct (R&D grants, loans,
etc.) and indirect subsidies (say, tax incentives) for industrial R&D and innovation.
Government can stimulate industrial STI as a consumer of by implementing programs
related to the basic function of the state, such as defense, public health, the
environments, space, and others. In addition, government can facilitate industries’ STI
activities by providing an appropriate IPR services, technology extension programs,
technology and market information system, and building and operating technology
parks for the promotion and facilitation of industry-science interface.

sectors, best leverages the country’s endowments for national development, under the
given (1) resource endowments, the capacity of the society, and (3) social, political and
economic attitude of the members of the society. This suggests that countries cho ose
different strategies for development because they differ in (1) resource endowments
that they can exploit for economic development, (2) the attitude towards development
and well-being, that is, motivation and/or desire for change and development, and ( 3)
in terms of capacity to initiate or deal with changes. The same logic applies also to STI
plan, which means that STI planning process should begin with the following:
(1) Understanding and assessing the STI resources and capabilities of the nation: the
strength and weaknesses
(2) Analyzing the domestic situation – identifying issues and diagnosing the nature of
the problems
(3) Assessing the situations surrounding STI, including S&T trends and developments
and the ways countries respond to the changes and trends
(4) Identifying challenges and opportunities based on the above analyses
(5) Assessment of the nation’s capability to deal with the current issues and future
challenges

The analyses are to assess the situation where the country stands in terms of STI,
evaluate the STI potential of the country, and explore what should be done in terms of
STI development to realize the vision for national development. The key player in this
process (the ministry or agency responsible for STI policy) should consult with the
national planning ministry or authority in order to make sure that the direction of the
process is well aligned with the national development vision, and should collaborat e
with stakeholders and policy experts, and S&T community to make the plan tuned to the
needs and actionable. The logical process of planning can be described as [Figure 1-2].

• How to acquire the key technologies: Who do what?

For the plan to be actionable, strategies should be based on reality -- the resource
availability, STI capability, STI trends, and the techno-economic environments where the
country stands, for the understanding and assessment of which it requires strong policy
capacity. Strong policy capacity is essential not only to the formulation of effective plans
but also to the consistent implementation of the plans.

 Policy Capacity: What it requires to make STI Plans Effective?
Government intervention in STI development is justified because the unique nature of
STI makes it impossible for market to function as a mechanism for optimal resource
allocation, and also because in the case of developing economies, not only they lack STI
capacity, but also private industries are simply incapable to deal with STI issues they face
in the market. As no sectors in developing countries are technologically prepared in the
early stage of development, government has to intervene in almost every sector for STI
capacity building, which requires solid policy capacity.
Policy capacity includes the capacities to systematically diagnose policy problems, to
formulate set of measures to respond to the problems, and to put the policy measures
into action in a consistent and effective manner. To understand the nature of policy
issues, they need information on STI situation inside and outside the country, for which
they need a system that collect and disseminate STI data and information. Then, they
need to analyze the information to diagnose the real nature of the issues, for which they
need to have policy research capability. Then, based on the understanding, we should

===== PDF_PAGE 26 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
14
be able to formulate policy measures to deal with the issues identified, which requires
organizational apparatus to formulate policy measures, plus technical competence of
bureaucrats. Finally, the policy measures should be put into action, for which it requires
not only resources to implement the policy measures – financial, human, and
organizational resources, but also political leadership to ensure long -term policy
commitment to STI, which depends very much on the political competence of STI
leadership of the country.
So, policy capacity is composed of a set of resources and skills that are required to
perform policy functions -- such as analytical competence, operational competence, and
political competence. Each of the competences involves resources and/or capabilities at
individual, organizational and systemic levels. In short, policy capacity is defined here as
an integration of capability to systematically diagnose policy problems; capability to
formulate sets of measures to respond to the problems; and capability t o put policy
measures into action in a consistent and effective manner.

3. Korean Experience: The Science and
Technology Framework Plan

 Emergnece of STI as a Policy Issues and STI Planning in Korea
Science and technology have been in the core of Korea’s development strategy form the
very beginning. In 1961, a draft of the First Five -year Economic Development Plan was
reported to the political leadership. The political leaders were very satisfied with the
draft plan that was very ambitious and tuned to the needs of the nation. Expressing
satisfaction, however, they raised the following question:
I wonder if there isn’t any problem in acquiring technologies required to attain the
development goals. Would it be possible for us to build and operate the new
factories with our own technologies and technical manpower? If not, are there any
additional plans to back up the plan in terms of technology and technical manpower?
(The Innopolis Foundation, 2013, p.28)
No one from the planning ministry expected that such a practical but critically important
question to be raised by the political leaders. They could only evade the moment by
reporting that a separate back -up plan on technology and manpower was being
prepared (Chung, 2016). From that moment on, technology and research have been
placed in the center of Korea’s development policy. It did not take long for the planning
ministry to prepare a supplement to the First Five -year Economic Development Plan
(1962-1966) that provided policy measures to acquire technologies required for the
industrialization and to build STI capacity. As a central agency responsible for
formulating and implementing STI policy, they created the Ministry of Science and
Technology (MOST) in 1967.
Since MOST was created in 1967, there have been three strands of STI policy planning
activities:
(1) S&T sector plans of the Five -year Economic Development Plans (Four plans
covering the period 1962 -1981) and the Fiver -year Social and Economic
Development Plans (Three plans covering the period 1981-1996);

===== PDF_PAGE 28 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
16
(2) As the Korean economy grew, the government began to reduce direct
intervention in economic affairs and put an end to ‘plan -based development
‘policy in 1997 with the completion of 7 th Five-year Social Economic
Development Plan (1992 -1996). But the government deemed that state
intervention is still required in the areas of STI, and thus maintained the plan -
based development strategy, such as the Five -year Science, Technology and
Innovation Plan (1997-2001), and the Science and Technology Framework Plans
(five-year plan that revolves with the five-year term government, 2002-); and
(3) Along with the five -year plans, MOST has also been formulating longer -term
plans that set forth visions for STI development. The first one was the
Comprehensive Long -term Plan for Science and Technology Development
(1967-1986), and since then MOST has been issuing long-term plans revising
mid-long-term development visions for STI development, taking into
consideration the scientific and technological advances inside and outside the
country. For example, in 1980, seven years before the end of the first long-term
plan, MOST issued the Long -term Science and Technology Development Plan
(1980-1991). So, the longer-term plans have been prepared and published when
the government deemed it necessary to reflect the changes in STI and the new
trends and revise visions for STI development.

The above shows that the Korean development has been highly reliant on STI, for the
development of which the government played a key role from capacity building to
technology development, and to the promotion of industry -science linkages. The
strategy Kore a opted for was a “STI -based, government -led” development. Such a
choice was in s sense inevitable, considering the conditions Korea faced in the early
stage of the development, such as:
(1) Korea was (and still is) very poor in natural resources, but was blessed with well -
educated, highly- motivated human resources;
(2) In terms of development capacity, the private sector was extremely weak, while
the government sector was relatively strong in terms of policy capacity; and
(3) The Korean people then had a highly positive attitude toward national
development and had a strong desire for change and development.

===== PDF_PAGE 29 =====
Chapter 1. National S&T Basic Plan in Korea
17
So, Korea opted for a strategy based on STI, which is a very brain -intensive approach.
The government led the development as the leading actor or orchestrator to make up
for the weakness of the private sectors’ capacity. And as a means to mobilize national
resources for development, to communicate with the tax payers on the need for
development, and to engage the society in the development efforts, the government
relied very much on planning which required strong policy capacity. Indeed, the plans
provided the people with a clear picture of the national development and inspired them
to actively participate in the process of economic development for more than three
decades. How much the Korean government stressed the role of STI as a strategic tool
for attaining the national development goals is well reflected in the Five-year Economic
Development Plans. The plans placed STI on the top of the development agenda
throughout the entire development period (1962 -1996). The S&T policies contained in
the plans deserve special attention for the following reasons:
(1) The plans were very specific about the goals and strategies and clearly tuned to
the national development vision, and
(2) The plans were based on clear understanding and assessment of the capacity and
needs of the industries in setting the goals: Starting with promoting technological
learning in the early stage of development and then moving to promoting
domestic R&D for technology acquisition…
(3) In identifying target technologies for development, they monitored and assessed
both domestic and international trends in technology development, through
technology foresight exercise and other analyses.
(4) The plans were fully backed up by specific investment plans.

The above means that the plans were practical and action-oriented, and thus the STI
plans could fulfill the role of facilitating industrial development. The following table lists
the STI plans implemented over the past five decades:

 Economic Development and the Evolutiona of STI Plans:
Transition from the ‘S&T Development Plan’ to the ‘S&T
Framework Plan’
Korea, which was one of the poorest economies in the world as late as in the 1960s,
emerged in the early 2000s as a dynamic, high-technology-based industrial economy. In
the course of the growth, Korea has gone through two important structural
transformations: one from a traditional agrarian economy to an industrial one based
low- and mid-technologies during the of 1960s -1980s, and the other one from a low -
and mid-technology-based industrial economy into a high -technology-based economy
during the period of 1980s -2000s. By the early 2000s, Korea had successfully made a
structural transformation into a high -technology-based economy, entering into a post -
catchup stage of development. 3 Various factors have worked together to make the
transformation possible, of which STI deserves a large share of the credit.
[Table 1-2] Stages of development and STI plans 1962-2001: Korea
Stages of development STI plans
Industrialization based on technological catch-
up (1962-1986)
- The Five-year Technology Promotion Plan,
Supplement to the Five-year Economic
Development Plan (1st-3rd plans)
- The S&T Sector Plan, The Five-year Economic
Development Plan (4th-5th plans)
Structural transformation: Promotion of high-
tech industries (1987-2001)
- The S&T Sector Plan, The Five-year Social
Economic Development Plan (6th-7th plans)
- The S&T Innovation Plan (1997-2001) based
on the S&T Innovation Law
Source: MOST (various years)

While STI plans deserves credits for the development Korea has made, it does not mean
that the validity of the plans remains unchanged in the new stage of development. In
order to move ahead with another transition toward a creative innovation economy, it

3 The transformations raised Korea’s GDP per capita from a mere $94 in 1961 to $35,000 in 2020. Indeed, Korea has
accomplished within half a century what it took almost two centuries for the Western industrial economies to
achieve.

===== PDF_PAGE 32 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
20
was imperative for Korea to attain world top -level competitiveness in the strategic
technology areas. In addition, the government needed a new frame of STI development
because the Five -year national development plan that had provided the visions and
frames for STI planning was completed in the end of the 1990s. STI needed a new frame
for development. It was, therefore, critically important for the government to put
forward a new vision for STI development and design a systematic plan to realize the
vision. It was in response to these needs that the government enacted the Framework
Act on Science and Technology (2001)4 that pursues the following value:
The fundamental value that this Act pursues is to promote science, technology and
innovation as a source of social and economic development, based on the principle
of human dignity and in harmony with the natural environments and the social
ethical value; to promote autonomy and creativity of scientists and engineers; and
to realize balanced development of natural science, humanity and social sciences.
(Article 2 of the Act)

The Framework Act emphasizes science- and information-based policy and requires the
government to take policy actions to promote the formation of an efficient national
innovation system that can usher the nation into a knowledge-based economy. In other
words, the Framework Act mandates the governm ent to make policy efforts to
transform the nation into an innovation -friendly society that provides a fertile land
where creativity blooms and comes to rich fruition – an ecosystem prone to innovation.
In addition, the Act mandates the government to formu late an inter -ministerial mid-
term STI plan every five years (the Science and Technology framework Plan), the
finalization of which is subject to the deliberation and approval of the National Science
and Technology Committee.
The Framework Act finds the need for a new plan (the Framework Plan) in the emerging
new policy issues that could not be dealt effectively with the past frame of policy. The

Innovation Special Law (1997)

2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
22
Therefore, the S&T Framework Plan differs from the past S&T plans in several aspects:
(1) It is a government -wide plan mandated by the Framework Act on Science and
Technology (2001) and formulated through inter -ministerial consultation and
public-private cooperation;
(2) It is a five -year plan that provides a mid -term visions for S&T development and
strategies to attain the visions, which suggests that the Framework Plan is more
about providing an overall vison and directions of policy. Rather than setting
specific targets, the Plan is more indicative and perspective-oriented, suggesting
a mid-term direction for the government and private sectors’ consideration in
making strategic decisions;
(3) The plan is finalized with the deliberation and approval of the National Science
and Technology Committee chaired by the president.

 The Planning Process and Structure of the S&T Framework
Plan
Article 7 of the Framework Act on Science and Technology mandates the government to
formulate and implement the S&T framework Plan every five years. It is a government -
wide plan that synthesizes and systemize S&T policies of diverse ministries and agencies,
setting forth the goals and direction of S&T development along with strategies to attain
the goals, such as S&T investment, focus areas for investment, resource allocation and
directions for sub -sector development plans. The first Framework Plan was put into
action in 2002. This section describes the processes of the planning and structure of the
first S&T Framework Plan.5

3.3.1 Process and Organization of the Planning
The key player in the planning process is the National Science and Technology
Committee. The Committee provides the direction of the planning and organizes the

5 The plan was revised with the change of the government in 2003, but the essence of the plan remained the same,
while the plan period was changed to 2003 -2008 from the original plan period 2002-2006. So, the discussion here
stick to the original one.

===== PDF_PAGE 35 =====
Chapter 1. National S&T Basic Plan in Korea
23
planning apparatus that is composed of (1) Steering Committee for the S&T Framework
Plan; (2) Sectoral Planning Sub-Committee; and (3) Secretariat.
The Steering Committee is responsible for analyzing and assessing the S&T trends,
techno-economic changes, and other social, political and cultural issues, inside and
outside the country that might influence the directions or patterns of development.
They derive from the analysis implications and suggestions for Korean development, on
the basis of which the visions and goals of the plan are set.
The Sectoral Sub-Committees are organized based on the findings of the above analysis
– How many committees for what areas, for example. The Sub -Committees work on
issues related to the specific technology or policy areas assigned to them (1) to support
the overall planning activities, and (2) to formulate plans for the areas assigned. The
Sub-Committees are composed of experts from private and public sectors. For the First
Framework Plan, they organized ten Sub -Committees – three responsible for planning
national strategic technology development, and the remaining seven assigned to work
on how to strengthen S&T capacity. What follows are the Sub-Committees and the scope
of the work assigned to them:
(1) National strategic technology development
• Sub-Committee for Future Technology Research: 6 Ts that include Information
technology, Bio -technology, Nanotechnology, Space technology, Environmental
technology, Cultural technology.
• Sub-Committee for Industrial Technology Development: Ways and means to promote
value creation by linking new technologies to traditional industries; Improving and
strengthening the competitiveness of the key industries by commercializing new
technologies.
• Sub-Committee for Welfare Technology Development: How to strengthen
technological capability to deal with unforeseen disasters – epidemics, typhoon, flood,
etc. Development of health, medical, marine, meteorological, disaster prevention
technologies. Development of food and energy technologies, along with defense
technologies.

===== PDF_PAGE 36 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
24
(2) Strengthening S&T capacity
• Sub-Committee for Manpower Development: Devising incentives to boost the morale
of researchers, improving S&T manpower development system, promotion of inter-
sectoral mobility of researchers.
• Sub-Committee for the Promotion of Basic Science: Designing stable and steady
supports for basic scientific research, Development of a basic science support system,
Inter-regional and inter-disciplinary balance in supports for basic science, Diffusion of
the results of scientific research to promote inter-linkages in basic scientific research.
• Sub-Committee on the Internationalization of S&T: Active utilization of foreign S&T
resources, International cooperation to solve the issues of global challenge, Promotion
of strategic international cooperation at the regional and national levels, Improvement
of institutional and regulatory systems to facilitate international S&T cooperation.
• Sub-Committee on the Promotion of Scientific Culture: Enhancing the sense of
responsibility of science, Promoting citizens’ engagement in S&T, Raising public
awareness of S&T, Developing a institutional base for the promotion of scientific
culture.
• Sub-Committee on Incentives for Private R&D: Promotion of industrial R&D by
strengthening incentives, Promotion of the diffusion of technologies of high
commercial potential, Development a system to strengthen the R&D capacity of the
private sectors.
• Sub-Committee on S&T Infrastructure: Upgrading the system for testing, analysis, and
assessment, Development of an S&T information system, Strengthening overall S&T
infrastructure including those in the regions, Strengthening the role of the National
Science and Technology Committee in coordinating S&T activities.
• Sub-Committee on S&T Investment: Increasing government investment in R&D,
Strategic prioritization of government R&D resource allocation, Strengthening the
analyses and evaluation of the government R&D programs, Systematic planning of the
government R&D based on technology foresight.

Committee members included experts from industries, academia, research community
and the government. About 140 experts participated in the committee activities.
The works of the Sub -Committees are put together into a draft plan by the Steering
Committee and reported to the National S&T Committee. The draft plan is then
reviewed by the ministries and agencies as a process of an inter-ministerial consultation.
Then, the next step is holding a public hearing, an opportunity for stakeholders from the
civil society, media, S&T community and the government to exchange views on the draft
plan. The draft that has taken into account the public opinion is handed over to the
National S&T Committee for final deliberation. If the Committee approves, the plan
becomes official.
In formulating the first Framework Plan, the Prime Minister’s Office (Office of
Government Policy Coordination) and 16 ministries collaborated and they are the official
co-authors and co -publishers of the plan. But de facto key player was the Ministry of
Science and Technology. The ministries that participated in the planning are: Ministry of
Finance and Economy, Ministry of Education and Human Resources, Ministry of National
Unification, Ministry of Foreign Affairs and Trade, Ministry of Defense, Ministry of
Science and Technology, Ministry of Culture and Tourism, Ministry of Agriculture and
Steering Committee
Logistic supports: The Korea Institute of S&T Evaluation and
Planning (KISTEP)

Forestry, Ministry of Planning and Budget, Ministry of Industry and Resources, Ministry
of Information and Communications, Ministry of Health and Welfare, Ministry of
Environments, Ministry of Labor Affairs, Ministry of Construction and Transportation,
Ministry of Fisheries and Marine Affairs, Office of Government Policy Coordination
(Prime Minister’s Office).

3.3.2. Structure of the Plan: How the Content was structured
The Framework Plan followed a logical sequence starting from the analyses of current
tecno-economic situation and predictions on future developments, both internal and
external, from which the basic direction of the plan was derived. They combined the
analyses on the global trends of techno -economic changes, the responses of the major
countries to the changes, and domestic situation – not just science and technology but
also economic and social, on the basis of which they derived the visions and goals for
S&T development and new direction of S&T policy. Then, the plan set forth strategies to
attain the goals. What follows are a very brief skeletal description of the first S&T
Framework Plan (2002-2006).

◼ The global trends of techno-economic changes:
• Rapid transition toward knowledge society
• Globalization and intensifying competition
• Emergence of new technologies and acceleration of technological progress through
inter-disciplinary fusion
• Demand for increased role of technology to meet the increasing desire for higher living
standard
• Increased role of technology in national security – defense, food, energy, etc.
• Increased need to raise public awareness of science and technology and social
responsibility of science and technology

===== PDF_PAGE 39 =====
Chapter 1. National S&T Basic Plan in Korea
27
◼ Policy responses of major countries
• Overall responses:
- Science and technology to meet social needs
- Strategic investments to attain technological leadership in such areas as ICT,
biotechnology, nanotechnology, environmental technology, etc.
- Adoption of comprehensive coordination system to ensure efficiency in public R&D
- Public-private cooperation in R&D and technology

• U.S.A.: Focus on improving the framework condition for innovation – Infrastructure,
S&T education, etc.
• Japan: Focused R&D investment in such areas as ICT, Biotechnology, nanotechnology,
and materials
• EU: Promotion of the mobility of S&T resources, open research system; and increased
investment in such technology areas as ICT, biotechnology, nanotechnology and space
technology
• China: Selection 12 strategic areas for focused R&D

◼ Situation in Korea
• Established a system of R&D within a short period of time (app. 40 years) – Emerged as
major player in global R&D landscape (GERD/GDP, from 0.38% in 1967 to 2.68% in 2000)
• Despite the growth of R&D expenditures, analysis shows that R&D explains only 18%
of Korea’s economic growth (KDI, 1991-2000)6, in contrast to 26% in the USA and 22%
in Japan
• Increasing demand for the role of science and technology in meeting the challenges
that Korea faced, such as quality of life, national defense, and other social issues
• Increasing demand for closer science-society interface

◼ Investment plan
The S&T Framework Plan also included a plan for government S&T budgeting. The plan
estimated that a total of KRW 35,331.6 billion would be required to implement the policy
programs, of which 68.9% or KRW 24,369.4 was for the development of strategic
technologies. Even though the lion share of the financial resources was planned to be
allocated for the development of strategic technologies, the share was actually smaller
than the actual share of 2001 (71.4%), which means that emphasis on strategic
technology development was slightly reduced in the plan. Still, it was planned that the
average annual government S&T budget would increase 1.32 times the actual spending
of 2001 during the plan period.

3.3.3. Implementation and Ex Post Evaluation
What distinguishes the S&T Framework Plan from the past S&T sector plans is that the
Framework Plan is more about providing a frame for actions and overall directions of
S&T development and resource allocation rather than specific programs for action. For
the implementation of the plan, it requires each of the ministries to draw up specific
programs for action based on the frame set in the plan. Each of the ministries is
financially and policy -wise responsible for the drafting and implementing the action
programs under its purview. At the end of the plan period, the performance of the S&T
Framework Plan is assessed based on the criteria set by the planning authority. And the
results are fed in the process of designing the next plan.
The performance of the S&T Framework Plan was to be evaluated at the end of the plan
period based on the following indicators.7
• Inputs: Has the plan been effective in mobilizing financial resources and developing S&T
manpower?
- R&D expenditures/GDP
- R&D manpower: Number of researchers per 10,000 population
- Government R&D expenditures/Government budget
- Government expenditures on basic research/Government R&D expenditures

• Outputs: Scientific and technological outputs
- Scientific publications: number of SCI publications, world rank, citation
- Patents: Domestic patents, foreign patents (USPTO, PCT, etc.)

• Outcome: impacts of the outputs on S&T competitiveness, social and economic
development
- S&T competitiveness: IMD and other international reports on competitiveness
- National competitiveness: IMD and other international reports on social and
economic competitiveness
- GDP per capita

7 Subject to change depending on the decision of the planning authority

===== PDF_PAGE 43 =====
Chapter 1. National S&T Basic Plan in Korea
31

4. Achievements and Policy Issues

 Acheivements
The achievements of the S&T Framework Plan can be assessed using several criteria –
resource mobilization, resource allocation, R&D output, scientific, technological and
economic impacts. In terms of resource mobilization, it is fair to say that the plan w as
successful as [Table 1-5], [Table 1-6] and [Table 1-7] show. Government S&T
expenditures reached KRW 36,450 billion, which exceed the target (KRW 35,331 billion)
set by the plan. The gross expenditures on R&D also increased from KRW 16,110.5 billion
(2.28% of GDP) in 2001 to KRW 27,345.7 billion (2.72% of GDP). The increases in R&D
expenditures were accompanied by remarkable increases in research manpower – from
178,937 in 2001 to 256,598 (1.43 times increase) in 2006.

Regarding resource allocation, the S&T Framework Plan emphasizes (1) focus on the
development of strategic technologies, (2) promotion of basic research, and (3)
strengthening science-society linkages. [Table 1-8] shows that expenditures on strategic
technologies (what the Korean government dubbed 6 T’s, including information
technology, biotechnology, nanotechnology, space technology, environmental
technology, and cultural technology) account for 35 -40%s of the t otal government
R&D expenditures during the plan period, which is consistent with the government’s
commitment to the strategic areas of technology. The promise to increase expenditures
on basic scientific research has also been implemented – As shown in [Table 1-9], the
share of basic research in the government R&D expenditures rose from 18.7% in 2002
to 23.4% in 2006.
Yet, it does not seem that all the promises of the plan had been kept. For example,
Hwang et al. (2017) assesses that the plan to strengthen the science -society linkages in
various ways fell short of the promise made by the goal.

plan period, quantitatively at least. During the period from 2001, the year before the
plan started, to 2006, the last year of the plan period, GERD increased 1.69 times and
the government R&D expenditures 1.55 times, which resulted in the 1.79 times increase
in the number of scientific publications and 1.66 times increase in the number of US
patents registered during the same period. The phenomenal growth of the US paten ts
owes very much to the areas of information and communications technology, the
number of patent applications of which grew from 668 in 2001 to and bio -technology
2,634 in 2006, which in turn is due to the R&D strategy focused on the area.

The scientific and technological outputs are translated into social and economic
development, even though not instantaneously. It is practically impossible to
numerically explain which outputs affect what industries to what extent and the time
required for the impacts to take place. We may guess very roughly the impacts by
looking at the changes of some economic indicators during the plan period. For example,
During the plan period, Korea’s share in the world exports of high -technology products
increased from 4.49% in 2001 to 6.09% in 2005 and then fell slightly to 5.83% in 2006
(NTIS), indicating that Korean high -tech industries’ competitiveness was enhanced
significantly during the plan period. This may be in part due to the focused investments
in the development of strategic technologies during the plan period.
According to the IMD World Competitiveness Report, Korea’s scientific and
technological competitiveness also was visibly improved during the plan period –
Korea’s world rank in scientific competitiveness rose from 14th place in 2001 to 10th place
in 2006, and technological competitiveness from 21 st place in 2001 to 6th place in 2006
(IMD, various years). This means that the plan’s goal of elevating Korea’s S&T
competitiveness to the level of world top ten countries was attained. In addition, the
vision of the plan to attain a GDP per capita of $15,000 by the year 2006 was realized as
Korea’s GDP per capita rose well over the target to $17,690 in 2006. Yet, however, the
overall national competitiveness of Korea fell a little from 29th place in 2001 to 32nd
place in final year of the plan period (IMD, various years), suggesting that the
improvements in S&T capability had not been properly complemented by improvements
in other factors that determine the national competitiveness together with S&T
capability.

 Assessment and Policy Lessons
The plan’s policy goal was achieved to a certain extent – Growth of R&D investments,
enhanced technological capability of innovation players, in particular private industries
made possible the structural transformation of the industries and economic growth. Korea
has been able to attain the development goals based on the S&T Framework Plan, owing
very much to:

(1) Strong policy capacity of the government: Effective intervention in S&T affairs
• Information and analytical capacity
• Operational capacity of the government to translate policy ideas into actionable policy
measures and to put the measures into action
• Political capacity to set the vision for development, to put the society together toward
the set goal, and to mobilize resources required to implement the policy plans.

2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
36
(2) Hard-working, well-educated human resources with strong desire for change for
the better
(3) Heavy investments in R&D and advanced education and concentration of the
limited resources on the development of strategic areas of technology
(4) Consistent linkage of S&T policy to industrial development: R&D priority dictated
by industrial development

Even though Korea was able to realize the vision and goal of the plan, various challenges
remain there. The fundamental problems of the Korean innovation system still remain
uncorrected.
(1) Dominance of the government in S&T and innovation policy process is still there.
Despite the growth of the private sectors’ capability in R&D and innovation, the
influence of the government still remains largely unchanged.
(2) The imbalance in the innovation system has not been corrected – the imbalance
between scientific research and industrial technology development, the
imbalance between large enterprises and SMEs, the regional imbalance in
innovation performances are still there.
(3) To nurture creative researchers, universities’ role in R&D and innovation has yet
to be further strengthened. Korea is one of the few countries in the world where
universities are minor players in R&D.
(4) As far as science, technology and innovation system is concerned, Korea is still one
of the least internationalized countries among OECD members.
(5) Many of the above problems have something to do with the dominance of
government in STI. A paradox -- as long as the government succeeds in planning,
we can hardly expect the government to change. Very often, success is used as an
excuse to stay unchanged.

Dr. Soo J. SOHN, Senior Research Fellow, STEPI

1. Background

A typical growth model is a function based on labor( ) and capital( ). But, a growth
model based on innovation considers technology, , as a main factor. That is, the
growth model changes to . Thus many innovative subjects
invest big R&D resources into R&D activity to obtain qualified technologies. And these
technologies need to have legal property rights to prevent their efforts and investments.
This is the reason why many major advanced coun tries and industrialized countries
started to pay attention to IPRs after the industrial revolution. The legal system provides
holders who create intellectual property, that is, creators who engage in creative
activities and inventors who engage in invention activities the right to creative activities.
It is basic institution to prevent technologies that made through R&D from abusing or
misusing. It will be incentive for innovators to invest their time and effort to create such
IPRs. Therefore, many innovative countries organized their insitutions for granting IPRs
in general to give incentives to engage in more creative activities or invention activities.
Therefore, it can be said that the more advanced science and technology, the more the
importance of inn ovation is emphasized, and thus the more the importance of
intellectual property is emphasized.
Since 1990, Korean government budget for R&D has been radically increased, and IPRs
such as patents resulted from R&D was also extended. Based on this situation, the
Korean government was interested in creation, dissemination, and utilization of IPRs. As
industrialization evolves, the granting of rights, IPRs, to that R&D outputs and IPRs input
for industrial revitalization become issues.

===== PDF_PAGE 54 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
42
[Figure 2-1] Government R&D Budget and PCT patent application of Korea

note: GOVERD(Government expenditure on R&D)

As seen in [Figure 2-1], Korea can be called a country that is currently creating a lot of
R&D investments and therefore a lot of patent results. Korea has been trying to
overcome its resources limitation, such as small nature resources, small markets, and
short history for industrialization, etc. This is a main reason why Korea is more interested
in innovation. That is, it is because Korea is weak in all areas including the labor force,
natural resources, and literally capital power required for growth. So it had to make a
very important effort in technological development and innovation. Therefore, Korea
increased the R&D input has been quite quickly compared to its history of economic
scale and growth, and made remarkable achievements in the end.
And now Korea has faced with another challenge and needs to shift from catch -up
innovation policies to leading innovation policies. Therefore many innovation policies
focus on technology commercialization.

===== PDF_PAGE 55 =====
Chapter 2. Technology Commercializaiton in Korea
43

2. Policies for Technology Commercialization

The law, ‘The Technology Transfer Act’, to promote knowledge transfer was launched in
January 2000. This law provided a legal basis for the country’s technology
commercialization policies. Based on this law, many policies for hardware infrastructure
(organizations, spaces, facilities, etc.), incubation programmes, information networks,
training programmes, technology finance and technology valuation, etc. have been
introduced and extended for encouraging technology commercialization(technology
transfer, technology-based startup, et c.). And the pan -department has been designed
the basic plans to secure national competitiveness in technology commercialization.
Policies such as training experts, establishing information networks, strengthening
technology scale -up, and creating comer-cialization funds were planned. Especially
regulation, demonstration, connection and tech finance are considered as the most
important factors. And policies for establishment of technology -based venture
ecosystem has been increased.
[Figure 2-2] Structure of policy for technology commercialization

Source: author

===== PDF_PAGE 56 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
44
Many support projects are implementing by government ministries including key players
like the Ministry of Trade, Industry, and Energy, the Ministry of Science and ICT, and the
Ministry of SMEs and Startups. Each ministry has designed a representative program.
For example, the Ministry of SMEs and Startups launched TIPs(the Tech Incubator
Program for Start-ups) programmes for incubating startups. It’s a co-incubation program
in which the gov ernment matches start -ups discovered by private accelerators. The
Ministry of Trade, Industry, and Energy carries out technology development projects
linked to commercialization. This is to bridge and incubate the gap between supply
technology and demand t echnology by connecting public research institute and
companies (start-ups, SMEs, etc.). The Ministry of Science and ICT operates the R&D
special zone system, Innopolis. This is a platform for the transfer of public technologies
and start-ups based on public technologies. It supports many useful programmes, such
as reg ulatory exceptions, post -R&D for scale -up, and incubation, etc. As such, many
ministries are operating technology commercialization programmes tailored to their
characteristics.
And the main frame has changed over time. In the 2000s, policies related to technology
commercialization began to be introduced. In particular, manpower training,
establishment and support of dedicated organizations such as TLOs. And the technology
information net work, NTB (National Technology Bank) was established and the
technology evaluation system was developed.
In the 2010s, main policy support was focused on building infrastructures and platforms,
for example incubation centers, Innopolises(special zone), Technoparks, CCEI, etc.
Technoparks have now been established in 19 regions. And Innopolises support
matching tech supply -demand, tech -based start -up companies/products/ services
under the law ‘Innopolis Act’. Innopolises have been established in main 5
regions(Daedeok, Daegu, Busan, Gwangju, Jeonbok) and since 2017, small and high -
density intensive area(Town) have been designated and nurtured as R&D special zone,
Innotown. Now, there are 14 Innotowns nationwide. CCEI(Center for Creative Economy
Innovation) serves as a hub for innovative startups. CCEIs were opened in 19 regions.
They provide programmes for the v italization of local start -ups and the promotion of
entrepreneurship. And it was also focused on bridging gaps or steps. In particular,

===== PDF_PAGE 57 =====

45
consensus for connecting was formed. In addition, local governments also established
their own institutions dedicated to supporting activation of technology -based regional
industries and these institutions are now leading the TC support efforts of the loca l
governments.

For the past two decades, policy has been placed on introducing and maturing the
technology commercialization system, building infrastructure for it, supporting the

===== PDF_PAGE 58 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
46

3. The Next Step in Korea’s Technology
Commercialization

Korea has created a government -led technology commercialization environment.
Support policies for commercialization include direct measures such as investment and
R&D support, and indirect measures, such as support of entrepreneurship education,
mentoring and consulting, workspace supply, and networking events, seed funding,
matching investment. But despite these support, there are still big gaps between labs
and markets, suppliers and demanders, technologies and capitals. And there is also the
issue of rent seeking by agents acting in encouraging technology commercialization. As
such, some of the past policies have been successful and some have acted as obstacles.
It is important to understand the environmental changes and limitations surrounding
technology commercialization to be able to design the next steps. All countries are the
same. Accurately diagnosing the current level of each country is a very important
process for designing the next step. External shock is also a big variable. Especially,
digital transformation will have a great impact on technology commercialization. Above
all, we can use a virtual space like the metaverse. And as a demonstration tool, the digital
twin can be used. From this point of view, the two keywords, ‘speed’ and
‘transformation’, will be important triggers. Thus the next step of commercialization
policy will be to build a system that can speed up the flexible transformation and lead
by the private sector.
Korea is in the vortex of change, such as the global pandemic, technological hegemony,
Russia-Ukraine war, and digitalization, convergence, inflation, especially dramatic
demographical changes, etc. Therefore, it is important to obtain technological
competitiveness. In particular, technological prowess is important to be competitive in
the international market and to have a comparative advantage over other countries.
Even with this understanding, an accurate forecasting of the impacts of these changes
is still a challenging task to complete. Therefore, an optimal policy would be to lay down
the framework for technology commercialization, so that the country will be able to
respond to changes quickly and flexibly. Realistically, a feasible next step will be t o

===== PDF_PAGE 59 =====
Chapter 2. Technology Commercializaiton in Korea
47
design an optimal path in areas where government policy intervention is needed and
plan a structure that can ensure policy intervention through efficient intervention
models.
South Korea’s sustainable innovation efforts and related experience have made it an
innovative country in a relatively short period of time. In order for this growth to
continue, a transformative commercialization policy is needed. Above all, it is to crea te
an environment centered on the private sector. And it is to set excellence as an
important policy criterion. In other words, it is not a distributive support, but a reliable
support for an excellent subject. In addition, it is necessary to create a global value chain
for commercialization. There are many fields that require global cooperation, such as
demonstration or technology packages.
Commercialization requires a long cycle from securing good technology to creating
added value. Above all, the uncertainty of technology and market is high. Therefore,
there are areas of market failure that cannot be achieved only by the private sector, and
this is the reason for government intervention. Appropriate and efficient government
intervention is required to strengthen technology commercialization capabilities.

Dr. Sengpasith HOUNGALOUNE, National University of Lao PDR

1. Introduction

 Backgrounds
In Lao PDR, it is essential to develop the National Master Plan for Science, Technology
and Innovation Development and Application as well as the National Policy on Science
and Technology Research. The plans should focus on survey and study development
indicator of relevant sectors, human resource, science and technology research
innovation networking and infrastructure development. It is specially to increase
competitiveness of trade, capability of research, and industrial development to be more
innovative.
Recently, there are several areas that are important for developing of science and
technology such as technology commercialization, small and medium -sized enterprises
(SME) innovation, international science, technology and innovation (STI) Collaboration,
etc.
Technology commercialization seems a complicated term at first sight, but its meaning
is an easy one; it is the process of transforming knowledge into services or products.
Simple, exceptional ideas are analyzed in labs, and then converted into services an d
products to improve our everyday life (Ghazinoori, 2005). They are then launched to the
wider international community.

===== PDF_PAGE 64 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
52
The main purpose of technology commercialization is to add more value to new
products and services. Technology commercialization is the most effective and
efficient way to turn ideas into real services and products, that will eventually satisfy
market’s needs. The process also looks at whether the inherent strategies have a long -
term economic advantage (Bahreini and Shadnam, 2007).
Nowadays, technology commercialization is attracting worldwide attention and plays an
important role in the integrated national approach for innovation and research. Likewise,
businesses are always looking for new strategies to improve their market status,
increase their business awareness and develop successful products and services. Thus,
by implementing the right ideas and converting them into products and services, they
are successful (Dehghani, 2015).
However, technology commercialization is not an easy process. It requires the
understanding of some basic concepts which all institutions, universities and
corporations should be aware of in order to successfully achieve this process. Similarly,
the fundam ental concepts of intellectual property (IP) protection represent an
important issue related to technology commercialization, and it is helpful to be informed
regarding the ways through which potential intellectual property conflicts can be
avoided (Kim and Cho, 2022; Dehghani, 2015).
Therefore, it is necessary to include technology commercialization in a national master
plan science and technology (S&T) development to compete with the growth of S&T,
and commercialization system with the needs of the market and society. It is critical that
research and development (R&D) capacity needs to be aligned with the market needs,
a system with developed S&T and R&D capacity and process for the practical policy
decision-making need to be established by applying available opportunities and
potentials in line with the growth of economic and social needs.

 International environment
In today's world, technology commercialization is one of the most controversial issues
(Lee and Lee, 2015). A number of companies and countries give first priority to this issue

===== PDF_PAGE 65 =====
Chapter 3. Technology Commercialization in Lao PDR

53
and allocate their attention among a number of activities (Dehghani, 2015).
Nevertheless, there are some obstacles to technology commercialization even within
most of the industrial countries i.e., financial issues, inefficiency of organizational
government and administration, lack of interaction between research teams, lack of
mass production, evaluation of research findings, implementation of reforms, and
product optimization which are being gradually eliminated (Kim and Cho, 2022;
Dehghani, 2015). Park a nd Glenn (2020) have warned that companies failing to adopt
accelerating technological changes might go out of business, leading to personal
bankruptcy and even a national crisis.
Developed countries have a long history in commercialization of ideas and research
outputs. For instance, university-industry collaboration in the U.S., concerning linkages
between universities and industries in commercialization of innovations and researc h
findings, dates back to decades ago, to the Morril Act of 1862, also known as the Morril
Land-Grant acts, defense R&D during World War II, and competition between two Cold
War rivals, the Soviet Union and the U.S. (Dehghani, 2015). These are U.S. statutes that
allowed for creation of land -grant colleges. In addition, the Bayh -Dole Act, enacted in
law in 1980, encouraged universities to acquire patent government -funded research
inventions and to engage with industries in technology transfer and research
commercialization (Wu et al., 2015).
The Japanese government has been formulating various measures to strengthen
industry-university collaboration. For example, the Japanese government has been
sending professional advisors on industry -university collaborative intellectual property
to univer sities since 2016 and intellectual property strategy designers to universities
since 2019. They have also implemented measures to reduce and exempt patent fees
and examination application fees for universities. These measures are expected to
improve the weakness of the patent strategy at Japanese universities and the situation
where there is a lack of expertise in intellectual property licensing strategies. It is also
necessary to focus on cultivation of human resources who are responsible for consistent
practice of technology transfer from acquisition of IP rights, financing arrangements,
and development of the new markets (Masato, 2020).

===== PDF_PAGE 66 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
54
In Germany, the transfer of knowledge and technology from higher education
institutions and higher education-related institutions to industry and vice versa is based
on a highly differentiated system of different stakeholders. In addition to the universities
themselves, there are three large clusters of non -university research institutions:
Helmholtz Association, the Max Planck Society and the Leibniz Association with their
respective institutes (Czychowski, 2019). Germany has also made strategic efforts to
strengthen the competitiveness of SMEs through public technology transfer (Kim and
Cho, 2022). The government-funded research institutes (GRI) of Germany is operated by
four large research groups. Among them, the Fraunhofer Society was established in
1949 to support the research needs of industries (Kim and Cho, 2022). The model
agreements for R&D cooperation at the Federal Ministry of Economics and Technology
prevailed in the 2010s. These model agreements, which are now in their third edition,
attempt to reconcile the interests of all parties involved, universities and research
institutions as well as SMEs and large industry, which were all involved in the
negotiations on these models at the Federal Ministry of Economics and Technology. At
this point, only the essential contents of these model agreements are reproduced in
order to give a feeling of which topics in Germany play a role again and again in the
transfer of technology under contract law (Czychowski, 2019).
Meanwhile, the Singaporean government attaches great importance to the linkage
between R&D and business. Therefore, Singapore universities have a strong link with
industry to commercialize research results or contribute to society (Kim and Cho, 2022).
South Korea enacted the Technology Transfer Promotion Act in 2000 (Kim and Cho,
2022). Currently, various policies are being implemented, such as the seventh
Technology Transfer and Commercialization Promotion Plan and the fourth Basic Plan
for Research Results Management and Utilization in Korea. Accordingly, the quantitative
growth of technology transfer is increasing at a rate of 7% per year (KISTEP, 2020).
However, the success rate of public technology commercialization remains less than 15%
(Kim and Cho, 2022). Thus, qualitative growth is disappointing when compared to
quantitative growth. Small- and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) in Korea account for
99.9% of all enterprises and 83.1% of total employment and are closely related to the

===== PDF_PAGE 67 =====
Chapter 3. Technology Commercialization in Lao PDR

55
national economy (Kim and Cho, 2022). However, these enterprises find it challenging
to develop core technologies independently. Therefore, SMEs must find other ways to
reinforce their technological capabilities within a short period of time and escape the
crisis (Kim and Cho, 2022).
It has been reported that almost all research universities in the world established
technology transfer offices (TTOs) to manage new technology commercialization
(Wahyudi et al., 2019). Generally, a technology transfer office (TTO) is established to
support the research organizations to manage their intellectual assets that provides a
benefit for society with their transformation. The general roles of TTO include
establishing relationship with firms and community actors, generating new funding
support from sponsored research or consulting opportunities, providing assistance on
all areas related to entrepreneurship and intellectual property, facilitating the formation
of university -connected companies utilizing university’s technology (start -up) and/or
university resource (spin -off) to enhance prospect or further development and
generating net royalties for the university’s technology and collaborating partner
(Wahyudi et al., 2019).

commercialization based on analyses of several studies as summarized in [Table 3-1]

 Domestic environment
The Lao government established the Ministry of Science and Technology (MOST) in 2011,
in regard with the decree number 309/PM, officially issued on September 28, 2011. It
indicated the government’s interest in fostering R&D. However, Lao PDR sought to
reduce the overlap of R&D policies. In February 2021, the government settled the MST
and transferred its roles and responsibilities to Ministry of Education and Sports (MOES),
Ministry of Post and Telecommunications (MPT), and other relevant line ministries. In
October 2021, the government established the Ministry of Technology and
Communications (MTC) under the Decree on Organization and Activities of Ministry of
Technology and Communications, No.625/PM, officially issued on October 22, 2021. It
is responsible mostly for MOST’s responsibilities and roles in terms of governance and
management of technology, innova tion, telecommunication, internet, post, and cyber
security.
Lao PDR’s 7th Socio-Economic Development Plan (2011-2015) included the development
technology and promotion of technology transfer was one of the significant elements in
such development plan. The technology and technology development for SMEs was
supported under the D evelopment Strategy of Science and Technology (2013 -2020) of
the Ministry of Science and Technology (MOST).
• A legal and policy framework supporting development of technology in universities,
R&D labs and support centers on IP rights exist has also been established by the
Government (ERIA and OECD, 2014).

There is limitation on the financial support schemes funded by the government, donors,
and other organizations. The pilot public funds supporting R&D activities are also
provided with a commercial orientation and limited allocation.
