2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
58

2. Policy Analysis
 Institutions related technology commercialization in Lao PDR
2.1.1 Technology Transfer and commercialization in Lao Context
The concepts of “technology transfer” and “technology commercialization” have been
used interchangeably depending on the subject and research topic (Kim and Cho, 2022).
However, some studies suggest the definition of technology transfer as “the transfer of
intellectual property such as accumulated technology, knowledge, and know -how
developed for the achievement of a specific purpose through contract and negotiation
between the parties to the techn ology transfer”. While technology commercialization
was defined as “developing, producing, and selling products using technology, or
improving related technology in the process” (Kim and Cho, 2022; Roh et al., 2017).
With respect to Lao context, since the technology transfer and technology
commercialization may be used interchangeably as there is no specific law and
regulations on technology commercialization. Regarding to the law on Technology
Transfer No. 33/NA dated November 8, 2017, the technology transfer defines as
“transferring of right and ownership partly and wholly on the technology such as know-
hows, experiences, processes, methods, equipment, tools, and vehicle from a transfer
person to a receive person”.

2.1.2 Laws Related to technology commercialization in Lao PDR
The law that directly related to technology commercialization in Lao PDR includes the
law on Technology Transfer No. 33/NA dated November 8, 2017 (consists of 11 Chapters
and a total of 58 Articles). The law defines the principle, regulations, practices, measures,
restrictions, and standards for management and monitoring technology transferring to
promote the research, development, creativity, trade, investment, production, service,
and industry to ensure that the technology applied is effectively and contr ibute to
sustainable growth of the national economy.

===== PDF_PAGE 71 =====
Chapter 3. Technology Commercialization in Lao PDR

59
Based on the laws, it emphasized directions and policies for technology transfer
development as follows:
• The Government of Lao PDR (GoL) gives priority for the technology transfer by
facilitating the R&D, innovation, local wisdom, production, management of technology
transfer and innovation application to ensure for raising of living standards of people,
and to set the foundations for steady industrialization and modernization supporting
to the national economy growth sustainably;
• GoL supports and promotes activities and investments for the technology transfer by
providing infrastructures, human resources development, budgets, equipment,
accessible to development funds, and provide information related to science and
technology; and
• GoL supports and promotes individuals, legal entities, and organizations domestically
and internationally to invest in the development of advance technology, modern,
suitable, and environmental friendly by providing special credit, exemption or
reduction of taxes according to the law.

Promotion of the technology transfer include:
• Promotion on the policy related to the technology transfer;
• Promotion on human resources development; and
• Promotion on marketing related the technology transfer.

Besides, the Law on Intellectual Property, No. 38/NA dated November 15, 2017 is
another law that could be related to technology commercialization in Lao PDR (consists
of 10 Chapters and a total of 170 Articles). This law determines principles, regulations
and measures relating to the management and protection of intellectual property rights
[in order] to support and promote inventions, creativities, local knowledge -based
economy, development of science and technology, the transfer of technology within the
country and from abroad, to ensure legitimate interests of the owner of the intellectual
property and the interests of state, society aiming to promote trade, investment and the
competitiveness according to market based economy mechanism effectively and
efficiently and integrate into regional and international levels and contribute in socio -

===== PDF_PAGE 72 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
60
economic development of the nation and industrialization and modernization of the
country.

 Analysis of Lao PDR current situations related technology
commercialization
2.2.1 Descriptive Statistics
2.2.1.1 Intellectual property Performance
The National Assembly passed a revision of the Law on Intellectual Property
comprehensively in 2011, and an amended version in 2017 (though changes are not
considered significant). After the dissolution of the Ministry of Science and Technology
(MOST), the Ministry of Industry and Commerce (MOIC) now controls the issuance of
patents, copyrights, and trademarks. The consolidation of responsibility for intellectual
property rights (IPRs) is a positive development, but the enforcement capacity is
insufficient and lacks qualified patent examiners.
In Lao PDR, the intellectual property protection is weak, but steadily improving. The
country has been a member of several regional and international organizations as
follows:
• Member of the World Intellectual Property Organization (WIPO) in 1995;
• Joined Paris Convention for the projection of industrial property in 1998;
• Joined Patent Cooperation Treaty (PCT) in 2006;
• Protocol Relating to the Madrid Agreement Concerning the International Registration
of Marks in 2016; and
• Joined the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN) and ASEAN working group
on IP cooperation in 1997.

◼ Patent
A “Patent” means the official certificate issued by the state organization to protect
inventions that they are novel, include an inventive step and are practicable for

===== PDF_PAGE 73 =====
Chapter 3. Technology Commercialization in Lao PDR

61
industrial application. Whereas “Invention” means the technical solution to create new
product or process of production to resolve a specific problem.
Measuring the link between publications and patents has been a subject of academic
research in recent decades. It helps us to understand the intensity and orientation of
research as well as the relation between science and technology (UNESCO, 2018). A
measurable relation lets us to examine knowledge transfers and potential spillovers; to
describe the knowledge base of particular technologies; to disclose the technological
neighborhood of scientific themes or research fronts; to reveal an ongoing innovation
process (from research to technology then on to commercialization).
Patents include a unique source for technological knowledge, which they are regarded
a good proxy for R&D activities, invention skills, and for the scope of technological
innovation of sectors and firms, countries, and regions. The use of patent statistics
makes it possible to track technological changes and to examine knowledge transfer and
R&D cooperation between various sectors and countries (UNESCO, 2018).
According to requirement of the Government represented by Department of Intellectual
Property (MOIC), in order to obtain a patent (with a Term of Protection of Patents 20
years), an invention shall meet all the following requirements:
(1) shall be new: meaning such invention has not existed, not been disclosed to the
public by publication, or by use or in any other means in the Lao PDR or any place
in the world prior to the date of filing the application for registration or where
priority is claimed prior to the priority date of the application for a patent;
(2) shall involve an inventive step: meaning that would not be obvious to a person
having ordinary knowledge in that field of technology;
(3) shall be industrially applicable in industry, handicraft, agriculture, fishery, services,
etc.
Exclusion from patent protections include discoveries, scientific theories, mathematical
methods, schemes, rules or methods for performing mental acts, methods for doing
business, methods for treatment of human and animal body and inventions which are
contrary to the public order or the national cultural morality. [Figure 3-1] presents the
number of patent applications filed in the Lao PDR by origin of the applicant (residents

===== PDF_PAGE 74 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
62
and non -residents) between 2011 and 2020. The number of applications ranged
between 43 -97 patents annually. Most of the patent applied are from non -resident
applicants. The highest applications of 97 patents were recorded in 2017. The number
of patent appl ications were significantly decreased in 2019 and 2020, which this may
due to the Covid-19 pandemic that affected all socio -economic system including the IP
sector.

◼ Trademark
A trademark is defined as a sign capable of differentiating the goods or services of one
enterprise from those of other enterprises, which it is protected by IPRs. Principally, an
exclusive right on the use of the registered trademark is conferred by a tra demark
registration. It is implied that the trademark can be used entirely by its owner or licensed
to another party for use in return for payment.
Recently trademark data have also been used to convey information on two key aspects
of innovation, which are not usually covered by traditional indicators: marketing
innovation and innovation in the services sector (Millot, 2009).
The relation among trademark deposits and product innovation is fairly straightforward:
the commercialization of novel products is occasionally linked with the creation of a new
trademark to connect about the innovation and subsequent possibly become the
reference on the market for the product, which in turn enables companies to suitable
the benefits of their innovation. When it comes to marketing innovation, the link with
trademark deposits is more complex (WIPO, 2021b).

and aboard between 2011 and 2020 in Lao PDR.

copyright, industrial designs, geographical indicators, new plant variety, layout -design
of integrated circuits, and IP disputes resolution.

The results from ERIA and OECD, 2014 study recommended that the main gap is in the

strategic approach to SMEs innovation policy, limited access to standard certification
services, lack of technology provision in universities, poor provision of information on

2.2.2 Governance
With respect to technology transfer and commercialization in Lao PDR, the Technology
Promotion and Transfer Center (TPTC) and DTI of MTC play important roles for
technology and technology transfer. The DTI comprises of 6 divisions: (1) Administration
Division, (2) Policy and Strategy Division, (3) Technology Management Division, (4)
Innovation Division, (5) Advanced Technology Division, and (6) Satellite Imagery
Information Technology Division. The TPTC also comprises of 6 divisions: (1) General
Affair Divi sion, (2) Star -up Division, (3) Technology Promotion Division, (4) Digital
Transfer Division, (5) Technology Transfer Division, and (6) Technology Zone Division.
Besides, Department of Intellectual Property (DIP), MOIC responsible for intellectual
property promotion and development that includes facilitating for applications and
grants of patent, trademark, copyright, industrial designs, geographical indicators, ne w

===== PDF_PAGE 81 =====
Chapter 3. Technology Commercialization in Lao PDR

69
plant variety, layout-design of integrated circuits, and IP disputes resolution, which can
support technology and technology transfer. The MOIC was reformed and restructured
since its establishment. It was recently legislated as stipulated in the Prime Min ister’s
Decree on Organization and Activities of Ministry of Industry and Commerce, No.
604/PM, issued on October 15, 2021. The MOIC acts as a secretariat to the government
in responding to governance and management at the macro level. Its responsibilities
cover various fields, such as industry, handicrafts, industrial zones, chemical
management, internal and international trade, and electronic commerce. The MOIC is
also responsible for protecting consumers, adjusting prices, managing the market, and
enhancing businesses’ competition. The DIP comprises of 9 divisions:
1) Copyright Division, 2) IP Promotion and Development Division, 3) Plant Variety
Protection Division, 4) General Affair Division, 5) Policy and International Affair Division,
6) IP Service Center, 7) Patent Division, 8) Trademark and Geographical Indication
Division, and 9) IP Dispute Resolution Division.

2.2.3 Stakeholders
Regard to stakeholders for technology and technology transfer in Lao PDR, Technology
Promotion and Transfer Center (TPTC) and Department of Technology and Innovation
(DTI) of MTC play important roles in promoting, development, implementation and
management for technology and technology transfer. These two departments also work
together with other line ministries related to technology transfer such as: Department
of Intellectual Property (DIP) of MOIC, Research Institute for Technology and Innovation
of MOES, etc.

2.2.4 Resources
2.2.4.1 Human resource
The Human Development Index (HDI) has been measuring by the United Nations
Development Programme (UNDP) since 1989. It describes in a summary way the
achievements of a country in relation to the three -dimensions of human development
including access to knowledge, a long and healthy life, and a decent standard of living.

===== PDF_PAGE 82 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
70
The global rank of countries is obtained by representing each individual HDI value in
descending order. Countries are classified as achieving very high (>0.800), high (0.700 -
0.799), medium (0.550-0.699) or low (<0.550) human development, according to their
ranking (UNESCO, 2018).
In 2015, Lao PDR got a HDI of 0.586, which corresponds to a medium human
development category positioning it at 138 out of the 188 countries and territories
covered by the last report (UNDP, 2016). In 2019, Lao PDR got a HDI of 0.613, which still
corresponds to a medium human development category (UNDP, 2019).
Lack of qualified academic staffs is one of the main reasons for the low quality of
education and R&D in Lao PDR. The education institutes have difficulty retaining skilled
and experienced academic personnel due to low salaries paid and unattractive benefits
to academic staffs, so it is leaving institutes with only many young, underqualified, and
inexperienced academic staffs (Onphanhdala & Philavong, 2022). The key constraint on
undertaking R&D in innovation is lack of academic staff with advanced degrees.

2.2.5 Issues
Generally, transfer of technologies and innovations is affected by numerous barriers
understood as “any kind of limitations and features that hamper the effective
functioning of a technology transfer and research commercialization system, and, as a
result, block interactions between the R&D sector and enterprises, therefore impeding
the de velopment of innovative entrepreneurship” (Matusiak and Guli ński, 2010; and
Mazurkiewicz and Poteralska, 2017). The application of technological innovations is
acknowledged as a driver for economic and social development considering the
significance of the issue from the scientific and practical points of view.
With respect to technology transfer (TT) and technology commercialization (TC) in Lao
PDR, there are numerous issues facing for the country as follows:
• Institutions responsible for technology transfer and innovation management are lack
of experiences and skills;
• Limited financial supports for innovation and technology development at universities,
research institutes, and lack of private funds at enterprises;
• Lack of infrastructure and facilities to support innovation and technology including
technology transfer and commercialization;
• Lack of human capacities for STI at central and local government levels as well as at
private companies;
• Lack of disseminate the knowledge experiences and best practice on TT;
• Technologies of universities are embryonic and not mature, therefore highly risky to
develop and invest. TT from universities to industries in Lao PDR is still limited,
desynchronized, and weak;
• Practical utility is not proven: Technology is not developed in response to market need
(i.e., only at Lab.-level, lack of collaboration with businesses and lack of networking or
linkage among public and private sectors);
• Weak protection of IPRs at universities, which affected University Technology Transfer
(UTT) and creativity within universities;

===== PDF_PAGE 84 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
72
• Poor utilization of patent information for research, and IP promotion policy and act
need developments;
• Lack of innovation promotion and technology commercialization for technology
makers and transfers;
• Lack of offices or bodies which specialize in the commercialization of research results
or patents like models of Technology Licensing Organization (TLO);
• The size of Lao PDR enterprises is still small with a proportion of MSMEs covers more
than 99% of total enterprises. A proportion of micro, small, and medium enterprises
are 94%, 5%, and 0.7%, respectively according to the 3rd national economic census
(2019-2020).

 Challenges and Opportunities
SWOT analysis of the technology transfer and technology commercialization for Lao PDR
is provided as follows:

===== PDF_PAGE 85 =====

 Policy Recommendations
(1) Firstly, the legislative framework needs to be improved, where the main role
should be assigned to intellectual property rights (IPRs) protection. Strengthened
IPRs facilitate open innovation adoption. Thus, robust and certain IP protection
and collaboration are complements in supporting the innovation process.
(2) A strategy is developed from each side of technology transfer, and to achieve a
successful result of innovation, a significant exchange of knowledge is required,
on the one hand, and some ways and mechanisms to limit the exchange of other
knowledge, on the other hand. Intellectual property rights help to deal with this
controversy.
(3) A specialized program appears necessary to be developed to support the
technology transfer network to bring market demand closer to the capabilities of
domestic scientific and industrial complex, and create an active circulation of
intellectual rights and innovative developments.
(4) It is necessary to stimulate and accelerate the development of technology markets.
It is necessary to develop regional and sectoral technology transaction markets
connected to the nationwide network of technology transactions, to promote
linkages and merge rs between technology and capital markets, and to expand
investment channels.
(5) It is necessary to improve the quality of technology transfer services by developing
standards for technology transfer services, improving market pricing mechanisms,
creating a reliable specialized statistical system for the technology transfer
services se ctor, improving the rules for identifying technology contracts and
registration rules. Obviously, widespread use of common standards allows
connectivity among diverse technology transfer participants.
(6) Lastly, there is a need to improve the infrastructure in the field of telecommuni -
cations with the outside world and within the country. Scientific centers will be
able to gain access to global knowledge, interact with foreign scientific centers and
carry out joint work in real time. The exchange of data in an international format
will greatly speed up the process of innovation generation, and consequently, it
will lead to an improvement in the state of Lao PDR technology transfer.

3. National STI Master Plan

 Direction of the Plan
3.1.1 Vision
“Transforming Lao PDR to a Sustainable Lower Income Economy by 2030 through
Strengthening Science Technology and Innovation Capability”.

3.1.2 Strategy
To achieve the proposed vision, several strategies are proposed as follows:
• Strategy 1: Technology Commercialization – Promote the linkage of the supply and
demand for technologies.
• Strategy 2: MSME Innovation – Promote the development MSMEs to generate
demands for technologies.
• Strategy 3: International STI Cooperation – Take advantages for the strengthening of
S&T capacity.

 Objectives for “Stagey 1: Technology Commercialization”
Several objectives as below are proposed in order to achieve the Strategy 1: Technology
Commercialization – Promote the linkage of the supply and demand for technologies.
• Objective 1-1: (Infrastructure Creation) Development of human capacity and facilities
related to technology commercialization and technology transfer.
• Objective 1 -2: (Consensus Creation) Promotion the awareness on technology
commercialization, technology transfer, and IPRs knowledge for both public and
private sectors.
• Objective 1-3: (Relationship Creation) Establishment of the cooperation mechanisms
between universities or research institutions with industries.

Prof. Soogwan DOH, University of Ulsan

1. Introduction

Sustainable economic development and growth for many countries have become an
important policy agenda, with innovation from each sector of society serving a key role.
This is especially true in today’s knowledge -based society where knowledge and
technology-based innovations are critical for promoting economic development
(Collinson, 2000; Doh and Acs, 2010; Doh and Kim, 2014). Not only is innovation one of
the most important aspects of knowledge creation (Collinson, 2000), but the future of
prosperity depen ds to a large extent on successfully promoting innovation (Baumol,
2004). Therefore, much research has aimed to understand the drivers of innovation as
well as how innovation promotes sustainable economic development and growth. These
studies mostly emphas ize innovation spurred by high levels of human capital, R&D
investment and expansion of entrepreneurship (Grossman and Helpman, 1994; Kirzner,
1997; Baumol, 2002; Audretsh and Keilbach, 2004; Acs, 2006; Doh and Kim, 2014).
Specifically, investment in human capital and R&D will lead to entrepreneurship based
on new knowledge and technology, generating various Small and Medium -sized
enterprises (SMEs). These innovative SMEs, according to prior studies, significantly
contribute towards economic development and growth (OECD, 2004a, 2004b; Kramer
et al., 2007).
However, SMEs, due to comparative disadvantage in various aspects such as labor,
capital, information, management skills, and government regulations, face a steep
challenge competing with large sized enterprises. Nevertheless, SMEs have positively
contributed to sustainable economic development and growth through notable

===== PDF_PAGE 96 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
84
achievements such as innovation, job creation and export expansion. Thus,
governments from various countries, including South Korea, have supported SMEs in
various ways. In 1960s, South Korea, a poor agrarian society based on agriculture and
light industry, achieved rapid economic growth in 1970s by transitioning to an industrial
society based on heavy and chemical industries (Doh and Kim, 2014). South Korea
successfully transitioned by investing in capital-intensive heavy chemical industries. The
needed capital came from exporting goods from labor-intensive manufacturing industry.
Export expansion coupled with development of chemical industries – shipbuilding, cars,
and chemicals – allowed South Korea opportunities to transition from an
underdeveloped to a developing country.
Although South Korea’s economic development and growth continued until ICT industry
developed in early and middle of 1990s, South Korea faced Asian financial crisis in 1997.
At that time, President Kim Dae Jung, who had just been elected as a president, inherited
a nationally bankrupted economy. In addition, he also inherited a host of other problems
caused by rapid economic growth such as imbalanced relations between central and
local governments and economic and social inequality. These problems raised the need
for Kim Dae Jung administration to develop a balanced economic development plan for
regions and promote a new industry that can serve as an engine of growth and enhance
labor flexibility.
To resolve these problems, President Kim’s administration first reformed each economic
sector’s vulnerability by implementing restructuring policies to enhance their
competitiveness. These measures included big business (Chaebol) reform, banks sector
reform, and corporate restructuring based on ‘industrial development act’ (February
1999). To expedite the restructuring process and maximize its effect, various
restructuring policies were promoted, including financial support for finance sector,
protection and tax breaks for the unemployed and depositor and monetary and credit
policy (total loan limit control policy, interest rate control policy, foreign exchange policy,
maintenance of foreign exchange management system, currency cooperation with

===== PDF_PAGE 97 =====
Chapter 4. SME Innovation Support in Korea
85
foreign countries, foreign investment attraction policy, SME financial support policy,
fostering venture business, e-commerce activation, etc.).8
One of the economic policies Kim Dae Jung administration prioritized in the midst of the
crisis was the policy measures supporting SMEs. Because President Kim Dae Jung
perceived SME reform as critical, SME policy measures were highly prioritized among
Kim Dae Jung administration’s various economic policies. Most of these policies were
introduced during the first year of the administration, as President Kim aggressively
promoted venture capital initiatives to overcome economic difficulties through job
creation. Such policy effort was part of a larger plan by the administration to enhance
competitiveness of enterprises and create new jobs. These efforts paid off, contributing
to overcoming financial crisis and improving sovereign ratings. Of course, some policies
adopted by the administration justified wrongdoings of big businesses (Chaebols),
collapsed middle class through large scale restructuring and escalated labor repression
and social conflict. Despite these side effects, there is a consensus that the
administration’s policies promoting venture corporations based on technological
innovation and financial support were critical to ending national bankruptcy early and
normalizing economic difficulties.
This research analyzes the historical context that enabled South Korea to promote SME
innovation and employment expansion during the financial crisis and in the larger
scheme of things, achieve sustainable economic development and growth. In this line of
argument, author of this research examines and reviews the functions and the role of
the Korean government in promoting SME innovation, in addition to how relevant
governance and institutions were implemented. To this end, this study seeks to provide
policy implications for promoting their economic development and growth and
supporting SMEs for underdeveloped or developing countries. Although country context
may differ by countries, Korean government’s diverse policies to support particular
industrial base and group of corporations in the midst of the financial crisis can provide
important policy implications.

(https://www.pa.go.kr/research/contents/policy/index020602.jsp).

2. Historical Background of Korea’s SME Policy

 SME Definition
In South Korea, as of December 1st, 2022, SME is defined as enterprises eligible to receive
support according to the Framework Act on Small and Medium Enterprises 9 , which
establishes and stipulates legal basis for fostering and promoting SMEs. According to the
Ministry of SMEs and Startups (2018), the criteria for being SME rests on economic scale
of SME and its degree of independence regards to its subsidiaries. The first criteria,
economic scale of SME, is based on the size and upper limit of each industry. The economic
scale criteria for each industry applies different revenue criteria based on standard
industry classification (main category, middle category for manufacturing). For the upper-
limit criteria, corporations with more than 500 billion won of total assets (total capital +
total liability) is not an SME regardless of industries they belong to. Overall, if a corporation
qualifies the size criteria but not the upper limit criteria, the corporation is not an SME.
Next, in many cases, an individual company can grow or diversify its business by
acquiring a subsidiary. It can be seen as unreasonable to financially support these
companies that combined with their parent company, exceed SME’s economic scale
criteria. Therefore, for companies 1) belonging to business groups subject to restrictions
on cross-shareholding under the Monopoly Regulation and Fair Trade Act, 2) with total
assets of KRW 500 billion or more (including foreign corporations, excluding non -profit
corporations, etc.) with a stake of 30% or more directly and indirectly as the largest
investor, their revenue is calculated proportional to their total investments. Even if their
total revenue qualifies them as SMEs, based on the degree of independence crite ria,
cannot be labeled as SMEs.
SMEs are classified into small and medium-sized enterprises. Businesses are classified as
small enterprises if their average sale falls within the criteria stipulated under the Table
3 Enforcement Ordinance of the Framework Act on Small and Medium Business. Middle
enterprises refer to businesses excluding small businesses in SMEs.

9 See https://elaw.klri.re.kr/kor_service/lawTotalSearchList.do

===== PDF_PAGE 99 =====
Chapter 4. SME Innovation Support in Korea
87
 SME and Innovation
Conventional understanding of the innovation process assumes that innovation lies in
the domain of large enterprises (Acs and Audretsch, 2005). However, in the new
perspective (i.e. entrepreneurship theory) on innovation process, entrepreneurial SMEs
as well as large established enterprises play an important role in innovation (Rothwell,
1989; Link and Bozeman, 1991; Lee et al., 2010; Lausen and Salter, 2004; Acs and
Audretsch, 2005; Doh and Kim, 2014). SMEs play an important role contributing to
regional development and enhancing economic performance by innovating technology
and strengthening their capacity (Jones and Tilley, 2003; Doh and Kim 2014). SMEs rely
on innovative dynamics more so than large enterprises in terms of product innovations,
and critically depend on production flexibility in terms of process innovations (Hall and
Harvie, 2003). For example, prior studies suggest that SMEs do a better job capturing
the benefits of networking for innovation in the knowledge -based environment (i.e.
Rogers, 2004; Edwards et al., 2005). Link and Scott (2005) and Mowery and Sampat
(2005) also maintain that in a more innovative environment, SMEs do a better job
exploiting external economies through their proximity to the R&D centers of large firms
and universities.
All in all, SMEs’ performance implies productivity growth, employment creation and
export growth. These findings suggest that SMEs’ performance is an important driver of
innovation as well as economic growth. For instance, SMEs’ high productivity could
mean SMEs’ high innovation, given that productivity is an important function of
innovation. In terms of economic growth, raising employment and creating jobs within
SMEs imply their competitiveness in the market, whereas export growth implies SMEs’
international competitiveness in global market. Therefore, government should prioritize
its policy orientation on promoting job creation, mitigating unemployment rates and
increase international competitiveness.
Despite SME’s contribution to innovation, productivity, economic development and
growth, SMEs face vulnerability achieving economy growth and development on its own
compared to its counterpart, large enterprises (OECD, 2004b). For this reason,
governments have been promoting various policies to support and foster SMEs. Korean

===== PDF_PAGE 100 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
88
government has also prepared and implemented various projects such as start -up,
funding, management, sales channels, technology development and mutual growth to
strengthen fostering SME’s.

 Status and Development of the Korean SMEs
SMEs play an important role in Korean Economy (Nugent and Yhee, 2002). According to
business statistics, SMEs account for ninety-nine percent of total enterprises, eighty-two
percent of total employment and forty-eight percent of export (Official webpage of the
Korean Ministry of SMEs and Startups).10 Table 1 shows the status and trends of Korean
SMEs from 2016 to 2020. As these data suggests, SMEs play a particularly important role
in the Korean economy due to their large share of the workforce dependent on SMEs
(Nugent and Yhee, 2002).

 Asian Financial Crisis and Transition of SME Innovation
Policies in Korea
As of 2020, South Korea has 7,286,023 SMEs with more than 17 million employees
(17.54 million). South Korea’s successful SME performance can be attributed to various
government policies intended to promote SME innovations during the Asian Financial
Crisis. Until late 1980s, South Korea’s economy mostly relied on heavy and chemical
industries but since early 1990s, various efforts have been made by the government to
transition into information technology economy. However, spread of financial
liberalization i n 1990s coupled with South Korea’s weak financial risk management
system severely weakened South Korean economy’s international competitiveness. As
a result, South Korean economy eventually fell prey to the Asian Financial Crisis 1997,
one of the most severe crises in Asia’s history. To capitalize the crisis into an opportunity,
South Korean government strived to transform South Korea into a market -oriented
economy and knowledge-based economy.
As [Figure 4-1] suggests, South Korea was struck with the Asian Financial Crisis at the
early stage of information society. During the crisis, Kim Dae Jung administration
assessed that promoting ICT industry – a foundation to information society – can
facilitate recovery of Korean economy. Thus, the administration aggressively sought to
promote policies supporting SMEs, expecting such innovative approach would bring job
creation and economic growth.

===== PDF_PAGE 102 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
90
[Figure 4-1] Stage of Social Development and Evolution of S&T Policy in Korea

Source: Park (2019: 390) and revised by author

After the financial crisis 1997 -1998, South Korean government strived to reduce the
influence of big businesses (chaebol) through promotion of technology -based SMEs,
while directly intervening in the venture capital market in 1998 for its revitalization. The
intervention enabled innovative small firms with technology but needing startup funds

===== PDF_PAGE 103 =====
Chapter 4. SME Innovation Support in Korea

to obtain investment from the venture capital firms (VCFs) and limited partnerships
funds (LPFs). To recap, [Figure 4-2] compares South Korean government’s SME support
policies before and after the Asian Financial Crisis.

As shown in the [Figure 4-2], South Korean government enacted SME related laws in
1960s and promoted various policies intended to support, protect and foster SMEs in
1970s and 80s. But in the late 1990s, the Asian Financial Crisis 1997-98 had tremendous
adverse effect on South Korean economy and brought significant changes to various
sectors. Further, not only did the Asian Financial Crisis trigger structural reform to
Korean economy in the late 1990s but brought changes to South Korea’s SME policies.
Specifically, to reduce influence of big businesses (Chaebol) on Korean economy, the
Korean government aggressively promoted policies supporting innovative venture
corporations and technology based SMEs. In this spirit, the government pursued various
supportive policies aimed to promote SME’s innovation such as promoting large scale
policies supporting SMEs. In addition, the government support policies for SMEs also
served as means to resolve unbalanced growth problem between central and local
government.

3. Government Support Policy for SME
Innovation in South Korea

 SME Technology Innovation Policy
To promote SME innovation, the Korean government emphasized policies promoting
technological innovation. These policies include SME Technology Innovation Program,
Industry-University-Research Institute Consortium Program, New Technology
Purchasing Assuranc e Program, and the Korean Small Business Innovation Research
(KOSBIR) Program (Kim, 2007: 135 -137). First, SME Technology Innovation Program
aimed to boost technological innovation of SMEs through R&D and build R&D capability.
To this end, the program was launched in 1997 to strengthen technological
competitiveness by supporting SME’s development of new products and its process.
Second, Industry-University-Research Institute Consortium Program aimed to support
SMEs with inadequate innovative capacity to res olve technological problems and to
strengthen their technological innovation by utilizing capable human capital and
equipment from universities and research institutes. Through the program,
performances of patent, prototype, and process improved. Third, Ne w Technology
Purchasing Assurance Program, to assist SMEs in commercializing their newly developed
technologies, guaranteed that the government agencies and public institutions would
purchase SME’s new technology products. Fourth, the Korean Small Business Innovation
Research (KOSBIR) Program was launched in 1998 to support SME’s activities promoting
technological innovation. The program makes recommendations to 16 public agencies –
10 government agencies and 6 government -funded research institutes with lar gescale
R&D budget – to provide more than 5% of their R&D budget to SMEs. In most SME
technology innovation policies, government supports more than 50% of the budget,
which implies that SME technology innovation requires large -scale government
investment and support.

===== PDF_PAGE 106 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
94
 Government Investment in the ICT Industry
It would not have been an exaggeration to suggest that South Korea’s IT capability
before 1990s lagged behind advanced countries by at least ten years and at most forty
years. Today, scholars generally agree that South Korea’s IT miracle that surprised the
world started with the launch of Ministry of Information and Communication in 1994,
which brought and sustained intensive investment policies on IT industry (Lee, 2008). It
is worth noting that such policy measures took place during the Asian Financial Cr isis
1997-98, one of the most devastating crises in Asia’s history. The crisis raised number of
unemployed to 2 million and Kim Dae Jung administration aggressively promoted IT
policies with hope that ICT industry would serve as a cure. [Table 4-2] summarizes Kim
Dae Jung administration’s ICT policies.

As the [Table 4-2] suggests, to recover from the Asian Financial Crisis 1997-98, Kim Dae
Jung administration invested 400 billion won in the IT, BT, NT, CT and ET sectors and
additionally invested 600 billion won in venture investment funds to promote new
industry developme nt plan. In addition, various policies supporting SMEs were
promoted to actively foster venture companies, such as the establishment of the
Cultural Contents Agency for the promotion of ICT -based cultural and artistic contents.
Among these active governmen t efforts, also included are the 2000 Comprehensive
Unemployment Measure to solve massive unemployment caused by the IMF economic
crisis and the promotion of the ICT industry as a part of this measure for job creation.
Specifically, the government comprehensively supported spaces needed for start -up,
high priced advanced equipment and technology and market information to SMEs with

===== PDF_PAGE 108 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
96
excellent ideas and technologies but without start -up funds. The government also
promoted policies expanding domestic software market through strengthening
crackdowns, supporting domestic software companies to enter overseas markets and
fostering component software industry – an industry that emerged as a core sector of
software industry. Such government efforts brought a rapid increase of venture
companies, localization rate of computer parts, new employment, GDP and IT
environment conducive for IT development and growth.

 Entrepreneurial Venture Business Growth in the ICT Industry
through Korean Government Financial Support
In South Korea, entrepreneurship has been an important driver of SME innovation in the
ICT industry. The notion of entrepreneurship not only includes creation of new firms,
but also the economic activities of newly established corporations. In essence,
entrepreneurship is about recognizing and exploiting opportunities (Kirzner, 1997). Not
surprisingly, entrepreneurship is associated with innovation and other entrepreneurial
features such as risk -taking and proactiveness (Covin and Slevin, 1989; Lumpkin and
Dess, 1996; Hessels, 2008). Further, entrepreneurship also facilitates spread of
knowledge. According to knowledge spillover theorists, entrepreneurship is the
knowledge filter serving as a conduit for knowledge spillovers (Acs, 2008; Don and Kim,
2014). S MEs are critical for entrepreneurship because it can bring technological
innovation and strengthen the capacity to do so. As a result, performance of SMEs is a
significator factor for building a competitive economy in a global market. In sum, SMEs
play an important role enhancing innovation and economic performance.
During the Asian Financial Crisis 1997-98, Kim Dae Jung administration paid attention to
the newly growing ICT industry. To overcome the economic crisis, the administration
tried to expand employment and job creation through fostering venture business that
required new knowledge and technology. As land formation plan for techno park – a
research complex promoted for ICT and venture corporations since February of 1997 –
began to mat erialize, a corporate body responsible for constructing techno parks in
Gongju-si, Asan -si, Cheonan -si, Gwangju -si, Gyeongsan -si, Daegu -si, Ansan -si and

===== PDF_PAGE 109 =====
Chapter 4. SME Innovation Support in Korea
97
Incheon Metropolitan City was established in 1998. Furthermore, various policy efforts
such as Venture Business Financial Support Expansion Plan (September 1998), Venture
Business Startup Fund Support Plan (February 1999), Job Creation and Unemployed
Protection Plan (March 1999), Venture Investment Association Investment
Management Plan (2001) were put forth. Such atmosphere generated numerous venture
corporations and served as an opportunity to attract talented individuals to ICT industry.
However, when venture capital funds became easily available from government’s supportive
policies, it also generated problems for the IT industry. With abundant venture capital funds
available, many of the talented prospective college graduates chose to start their own
venture firms rather than seek employment in the IT industry. Such trend brought labor
shortage and became a problem for the IT industry, as demands for computer and internet
related businesses were high but supply of needed labor low. This imbalance of labor brought
a heated competition between corporations in ICT industry to scout for IT professionals.

1997-98 to recent years. The data suggests that the number of venture corporations
have increased from 2,042 in 1998 to 39,101 in 2020.

Ultimately, the government’s ICT policies introduced to overcome the economic crisis
through job creation successfully fostered ICT industry. Specifically, the policies
increased labor force with related skills in ICT and the ratio of localized ICT product ion
while generating new jobs that made enormous contribution to South Korea’s economic
growth (Lee, 2008). It is particularly worth noting that a handful of Korean corporations
– LG Electronics’ display sector, Samsung Electronics’ semiconductor and memor y
sectors, and SK telecom’s communication sector – were able to develop a world -class
competitiveness due to Korean government’s decision to concentrate resources in
fostering ICT industry.

Chapter 4. SME Innovation Support in Korea

 Financial Policy
Government’s financial support – both direct and indirect – can facilitate SME
innovation by assisting innovative SMEs with financial problems. Therefore, Korean
government has also provided both direct and indirect financial support for SMEs. For
instance, SMEs with potential for growth in the future has received various benefits such
as tax reduction, loan support and special listing in stocks market. In addition, the
government offered financial support to SMEs with low security solvency that could not
obtain loan credit guarantee services. In particular, Korean government operated
various funding programs to support SMEs with technological capacity but faced
financial constraints.
While Korean government operated various funds such as Small and Medium Business
Fund (SMBF), Informatization Promotion Fund (IPF), Cultural Industry Promotion Fund
(CIPF), Science and Technology Promotion Fund (STPF), Credit Guarantee Fund (CGF),
Technology Credit Guarantee Fund (TCGF), the government also supported SMEs
through various other measures such as the Venture Investment Fund. [Figure 4-3]
summarizes the financial support system for SMEs operated by the government.
• Small and Medium Business Fund (SMBF), a fund supported by Small and Medium
Business Corporation, provides funds needed for start -up support and structural
advancement of small and medium -sized enterprises. The Korean government has
established this fund to secure financial resources necessary for the promotion of small
and medium-sized enterprises’ start-ups, balanced industrial development and the
establishment of industrial infrastructure and expansion of management infrastructure
and advancement of structures.
• Informatization Promotion Fund (IPF) is a fund established with the goal of improving
citizen’s quality of life and promoting economic development by promoting
informatization, establishing an infrastructure for the information and communication
industry, advancing information and communication infrastructure and supporting
information and communication R&D (Article 33 of the Framework Act on Promotion
of Informatization). South Korea, through audacious and flexible investment of the
Informatization Promotion Fund, has laid a foundation for the knowledge and
information society by establishing 1) a high-speed information and communication

===== PDF_PAGE 112 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
100
network that provides access to high-speed internet for everyone 2) an infrastructure
to advance e -government by creating an electronic document distribution
environment for all central administrative agencies and the expanding G4C base and
the national tax database. In addition, with the IT industry emerging as a key growth
industry for the Korean economy, the IPF generated an environment conducive for IT
venture companies such as software support centers and investment associations to
actively venture and grow. Furthermore, with the technological development support
through IPF, it became possible to advance information technology research and foster
ICT human resource. These findings suggest that IPF, as a financial vehicle, enabled the
IT industry to grow and become central to Korean economy after the Asian Financial
Crisis (Yoon et al., 2002; Special Committee on e-Government, 2002).
• The Cultural Industry Promotion Fund (CIPF) provides loans to SMEs related to cultural
industry, invests in new technology business investment associations or SME venture
investment association to financially support and foster SMEs as well as venture
businesses related to cultural industry, and manage contributions and subsidies related
to promoting cultural industry.
• The Science and Technology Fund (STF) is a fund established to support promotion of
science and technology and development of science and technology culture. STF
supports projects promoting science and technology such as international exchange,
human resources and research and academic activities related to science technology.
The fund also supports companies, educational institutions, research institutions and
science and technology related organizations seeking to use research outputs into
practical use. In sum, the fund is used as an investment on new technology operators
or venture businesses, loans and investments prescribed by Presidential Decree.
• Korea Credit Guarantee Fund (KCGF) was established to establish a sound credit order
and facilitate corporate financing by guaranteeing the debts of SMEs. As a fund
management type quasi -governmental institution under the Financial Services
Commission, it is the most specialized institution providing various types of support for
SMEs in Korea.
• Korea Technology Appraisal Guarantee System (KTAGS) is an institution established to
enhance technological competitiveness of companies by focusing on technology
assurance and evaluation. KTAGS, a fund management type quasi -governmental

===== PDF_PAGE 113 =====
Chapter 4. SME Innovation Support in Korea
101
institution under the ministry of SMEs and Startups, changed its name to Korea
Technology Finance Corporation in 2016. The KCGF and KTAGS are funds created to
support SMEs and technology innovation company and provides a very important
government credit guarantee service to finance SMEs.
• In addition to direct/indirect financial support through fund management and credit
guarantee services, the Korean government also provided technology development
subsidies to SMEs for the fostering of SMEs and technological innovation. These
subsidies pro vided financial support necessary for regional SMES to promote
technology development.

4. Government Financial Support for
Regional SME Innovation

 Disparity between Large Capital Area and Local Areas
Amid the economic crisis, South Korean government achieved notable results through
its SME and venture corporation policies. However, the problem of unbalanced growth
between capital and local areas remains a problem waiting for a solution. The disparity
between Large Capital Area (LCA) and local cities and provinces have exacerbated. In
2001, GRDP of LCA comprised 47.1% of the total areas and the local tax revenue of LCA
58.7% of the total tax revenues. In terms of fiscal independence, LCA scored 84.1%
compared to the national average at 62.9%.
The regional disparity problem is also serious in manpower and R&D fund. In 2001, 62.7%
of R&D funds, 58.6% of labor force, and 66.7% of R&D institutions were located in LCA.
Further, 67% of universities related R&D institutions and 60.1% of students resid e
outside of the LCA, but funds and quality of manpower remain an issue in local areas.
[Figure 4-4] GRDP Ratio and R&D Fund of each Region in Korea: Year 2001

Source: Statistical Database of Korean Statistical Information Service (www.kosis.kr/eng)
GRDP Ratio

===== PDF_PAGE 120 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
108
 Necessity of Regional Balance for Competitiveness
The issue of regional balance was one of the pivotal campaign pledges of President Roh,
who became president of South Korea in 2003. In the campaign, President Roh
emphasized that the inefficiency of concentration in LCA will eventually reach a critical
point and that investment in local area will reap profits in the long run. After being
elected, President Roh launched a powerful Presidential Commission. The commission
suggested that l ocal industries , combined with the knowledge and skill base of
neighboring colleges and universities , should be the engine for regional development .
Based on this recommendation, the commission designed the Regional Innovation
System (RIS).
Included in the RIS were various strategies for regional industrial development, including
development via SME innovation. However, because regional SMEs faced various
limitations, the government’s financial support was a priority above all. For instance, to
promote innovation, regional SMEs need technological skills to introduce new and
effective technologies . However, regional SMEs lack adequate financial resources to
invest or develop such technologies. What also makes such investment and
development challenging is that the profit made from the technology is not guaranteed.
More importantly, most regional SMEs simply lacked technological infrastructure to
develop next stage of advanced technology. Due to these limitations, Korean
government began to provide a large-scale financial support to regional SMEs, especially
for the technological innovation of regional SMEs.
In effort to promote regional industries, the Korean government has invested huge
amounts of public funds conducive to regional SME innovation with following
assumptions. First, the investment and support of regional SMEs may strengthen their
market competitiveness. Second, the investment and support would considerably
impact their local competitiveness and in turn, promote regional economic growth.
According to Doh and Kim (2014), the government’s financial support has positively
contributed to the innovation of regional SMEs. Specifically, a positive relationship has
been observed between 1) the technological development assistance of government
and patent acquisitions of regional SMEs 2) the technological development assistance

===== PDF_PAGE 121 =====
Chapter 4. SME Innovation Support in Korea
109
and new design registrations 3) networks with universities and regional SMEs’ patent
acquisitions and new design registrations 4) R&D expenditures and patent acquisitions.
In this line of argument, role of local government is critical, given their proximity to
regional SMEs. Specifically, local governments are, in particular, very active in the design
and delivery of programs for SME innovation . Therefore, the national government
should empower local government to promote SMEs innovation. Ultimately, the aim of
government support for SMEs is establish ing a competitive and innovative SMEs not
needing government’s financial aid. However, when the government is involved, its role
should be facilitative, not dictatorial.
By facilitating, it means the local government should provide a variety of support
services for regional SME innovation. This includes taking initiatives in provisions for
targeted and quality business support services ; immediate, technical, and managerial
training programs; lowering administrative costs and burdens of regional SMEs; building
network cross sectors ; provisions for financial incentives and assistance ; legal
framework reinforcement.
In South Korea’s case, among local government’s various policies supporting SMEs,
technology development fund played a critical role enhancing regional SME’s
technological innovation. Specifically, the government intervention in the form of
payment such as the technology development fund helped overcome the economic
crisis and the limitations of regional SMEs in ameliorating the regional growth problem.

 Government Financial Support for Regional SME Innovation in
Korea: Technological Development Assistance Funds (TDAF)
Regional SMEs face severe constraints in the competitive market. In such market,
technological development and innovation are a necessary condition for regional
economic growth. Therefore, to enhance SMEs’ performance, SMEs need to acquire
technological sk ills and introduce effective and new technologies into their firms.
However, SMEs face several constraints such as lacking adequate financial resources and
technical infrastructure to support and develop advanced technology. Furthermore, the

===== PDF_PAGE 122 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
110
profit brought to SMEs through the technology is not guaranteed. All in all, these
conditions hamper and limit performance of SMEs.
It is in this context government interventions are required to overcome the constraints
of SMEs. Government interventions by financial assistance (technology development
assistance fund) can enhance SME performance such as technology development and
innovation and management capacity. Korean government’s industrial policies can be
divided into two categories. First, applying national (central) industrial policies to the
local (regional) level and second, promoting local industrial policies to achieve their own
regional economic prosperity. To achieve these goals, government technology
development assistance policy plays a critical role. A part of national industrial policies
devolved from the Department of Knowledge and Economy to local government level in
Korea, the policy aims to apply national industrial policies to local level.
In this regards, South Korea’s local industrial policies have primarily tried to reduce the
gap between regional economies and increase GRDP by vitalizing local firms, particularly
the SMEs. To this end, the Korean government has concentrated its investmen t in
expanding local infrastructure, growth of self -sufficient corporations through SMEs’
technology development assistance and through strengthening industrial
competitiveness at the local level since 1999 (Doh and Kim, 2014)
As such, government intervention in South Korea is critical to overcome regional SMEs’
constraints. Specifically, Technology Development Assistance Fund (TDAF) has been
implemented at the provincial government level. As a part of local industrial policy, i t
aims to strengthen the competitiveness of local firms and the economy by supporting
common technology, core technology and fundamental technological innovations.
In addition, considering the importance of regional SME innovation in the Korean
economy, the Korean governmental authorities have, for many years, carried out a
variety of programs to support SMEs. Specifically, the Korean government has
implemented vario us strategies for a balanced regional development to decrease
income disparity between the industrial and agricultural sectors since DJ Administration
brought policy initiatives to promote regional industries. As a means of promoting
regional industries, the Korean government has invested huge amounts of public funds

===== PDF_PAGE 123 =====
Chapter 4. SME Innovation Support in Korea
111
conducive to SME innovation on the assumption that innovative SMEs may strengthen
their competitiveness in markets and make a considerable impact on local competitiveness.
Since the Korean government provided financial support for SME innovation in regional
industries, more attention has been given to the design and implementation of such
programs. Compared to 1999, when faced with the Asian Financial Crisis, in 2009, 10
years later, the number of SMEs and new jobs have increased. Such result in large part
can be credited to government’s SME support policies.

As a result of the consideration of government, South Korea is regarded as having set a
successful model for SME development. According to Small and Medium Business
Administration (SMBA) of Korea’s internal report, Korean SMEs have developed steadily
as a backbone of the national economy and are making important contributions to
nation’s economy, creating new job opportunities, the leverage necessary to strong,
sustainable and balanced growth within the frame of the global economic system.

5. Policy Implications for SME Innovation

Today, South Korea invests high share of GDP on technologically innovative SMEs and
venture corporations. Among OECD countries, South Korea’s level of investment in
innovative SMEs and venture corporations ranks among the top. These investments in
particular have been used as an important financial resource for venture companies.
Only about twenty-five years ago South Korea faced one of the economic crises in Asia’s
history, Asian Financial Crisis 1997-98 with an underdeveloped economic structure. But
the A sian Financial Crisis 1997 -98 South Korea experienced in late 1990s brought an
opportunity to transform South Korea’s economic structure into an advanced one.
Further, the SME support policy such as the financial support for fostering venture
companies has been evaluated positively for overcoming the crisis through job creation.
In particular, active venture business fostering policies based on ICT industry and SME
actively promoted during Kim Dae Jung administration’s early stages – expansion of
venture bu siness financial support, venture business start -up fund support, venture
investment association fund management, support for purchase of export bills for SMEs,
repayment delay for SMEs’ foreign loan, and financial support for SMEs – were mostly
intended to resolve financial difficulties of SMEs. However, South Korean government’s
success in overcoming the Asian Financial Crisis early and in fostering technologically
innovative SMEs were possible not only due to their financial support for businesses but
also due to various other measures by the government such as legal foundation for SMEs,
actively fostering venture companies and technologically innovative SMEs in the ICT field,
providing technology development subsidies for technologically innovative SMEs and
promoting balanced growth policy for metropolitan and non-metropolitan areas.
In this research, we analyzed South Korea’s historical experience that created and
expanded jobs through SME innovation in facing the Asian Financial Crisis that lead to
sustainable economic development and growth. According to the analysis of this study,
the Korean government has performed various functions and roles and promoted
establishment of related institutions and governance. Therefore, the functions and roles
of the government displayed by the Korean government so far provides important policy
implications for underdeveloped and developing nations promoting SME innovation for

===== PDF_PAGE 126 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
114
economic performance. Although the context of other countries may not match South
Korea’s, the process of the government promoting diverse support policies for specific
industries or business groups in the midst of Asian financial crisis provides important
policy implications as follows.
First, for SME innovation, it is necessary for the government to establish SME Innovation Plan.
SME Innovation Plan is a formal document that contains visions and goals of SME
Innovation and strategies to attain vision and goals, comprehensive guideline on
resource allocation and based on an assessment of the nation’s needs and capabilities
as well a s internal and external environments surrounding SME innovation in each
country. The plan also includes a plan focusing on SME innovation policy and a sectoral
plan focusing on SME innovation based on the guidelines of the planning authority or a
higher-level plan. It is also necessary to consider the period for SME Innovation Plan (ex.
Five-Years, Ten-Year Plan).
Second, for SMEs to successfully innovate, human resources with high expertise and
skills (high skilled people) need to flow into SMEs. Thus, the government needs to
establish and promote human resource development plan appropriate for SME
innovation. Korean government during the early stage of ICT industry began to actively
support ICT industry’s venture enterprises and SMEs as a means to overcome the Asian
financial crisis, and in the process the Korean government invested financial resources
to develop human resources needed for ICT industry.
Third, the government needs to prepare legal and institutional foundations to promote
necessary policies supporting SME innovation. The central government needs to prepare
the necessary finances at a national level for SME innovation and enact legislation that
can support SMEs innovation both directly and indirectly. Moreover, based on these
legislations, the government needs to establish and operate organization responsible
for financially supporting SME innovation and a governance system including
cooperation between central government departments and between central and local
governments as well as a cooperative governance system among various organizations
related to SME innovation.

===== PDF_PAGE 127 =====
Chapter 4. SME Innovation Support in Korea
115
Fourth, a government support policy for regional SME innovation is necessary. In
particular, since most SMEs are distributed across regions, promoting technological
innovation through local SME’s technology development support is needed. Further, it
is necessary to strengthen the role of local governments, the main body with the role of
providing financial support to SMEs. Local governments must develop and operate
various cooperative programs so that local techno parks, local universities, and local
SMEs can cooperate to achieve synergy effects through the establishment of industry -
academia-research networks.
Fifth, government needs to manage basic data related to SMEs operating in each
industry. Establishing such data of SMEs in each industry serves as a basic data for
understanding their performance and activities, and analysis of these data allows us to
evaluate effectiveness of government support policies for SME’s innovation.
Finally, SME innovation cannot be accomplished quickly nor by SMEs alone. Therefore,
government needs to make efforts to promote policies fostering and supporting SME
innovation in a mid to long -term perspective and ensure that government’s support
policies for SME innovation can focus through the establishment of SME innovation
clusters. Ultimately, efficient government policies will eventually accelerate SME
innovation both in terms of process and outcome, an important aspect underdeveloped
and developing countries with limited financial resources to consider.

1. Introduction

Research and Development (R&D) is a key factor to improve the national economy,
particularly R&D on science and technology (S&T). Policymakers have taken R&D and
S&T for innovation to strengthen commercial productivity. It aims to transform
economic structure to industrialization and modernization in achieving the sustainable
development goals (SDGs) by 2030. Watson et al. (2003) revealed that S&T helps the
government to enhance poverty alleviation and economic development. However,
development would increasingly depend on an ability of each country to comprehend,
interpret, select, adapt, practice, transfer, diffuse, produce, and commercialize scientific
and technological knowledge to match its culture, aims, and development’s level. In
South Korea, the government has implemented the 5 -year technology promotion plan
since the 1960s, which became the foundation of science and technology development,
particularly the establishment of Korea Institute of Science and Technology (KIST) (IDN,
2011).
Governments around the world are urging to broaden and deepen their support for
innovation development in the private sector and the economy. In the case of Korea,
the innovation of SMEs is measures by technological innovation, such as patent, utility
model, trademark, and new design registrations. Doh and Kim (2014) suggested that the
assistance of the Kor ean government on technological development and universities’
researches play a major role in regional MSME innovation s. In addition, regarding a
study on external relationships in the organizational innovation carried out by Simao et
al. (2016), it revealed that relationships with business partners contribute to positive

===== PDF_PAGE 134 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
122
performance of organizational innovation, in particular through new organizational
practices, rewards to employees and communication that allow introduce
organizational innovations along the supply chain. Despite this, based on results from
emerging and developed economies, PwC (2010) reported that innovation depends on
well-functioning markets. It also emphasized that the rule of law and arm’s length
transaction are essential for such markets to function well , which need to be initiated
before tax or other incentives can be effective.
In order to ensure the achievement of SDGs and innovation, most countries have
developed national development planning and their implications for the sustainable
development agenda, particularly the national master plan for technology and
innovation development. Plan production requires greater ownersh ip and political
control of the processes. By examining 107 national development plans, Chimhowu et
al. (2019) revealed that the plans vary in terms of evidence used, consistency ’s degree
between different parts of the plan, plan developing’s process, and the extent of funding.
The World Bank Group also released a practitioner’s guide to innovation policy in
developing countries. The guide aims to reduce the large information gap about what
works in developing countries to improve the design and implementation of innovation
policy instruments and increase their effectiveness. It includes 21 instruments grouped
in 10 sets of related instruments: (1) grants for innovation or R&D projects, (2) vouchers
for innovation and collaboration, (3) loans and guarantees for innovation, (4) tax
incentives for R&D, (5) demand -pull instruments, (6) technology adoption and
generation instruments, (7) early-stage support for innovation ventures, (8) inducement
instruments, (9) quality infrastructure, including standards, metrology, and testing, (10)
clusters and networks for innovation (Cirera et al., 2020).
As Lao PDR (hereafter referred to as Laos) has been struggling to improve its economy
since its establishment in 1975, it has made considerable progress in economic
development. However, this country does not rapidly shift from agrarian economy to
industrial one. It is because more than half of labor force still engages in the agricultural
sector, whereas its output is less than a quarter in GDP contribution. There are several
challenges of the transformation because Laos does not have any heavy industry. O nly
mineral ores exploitation, assembly plants, and light manufacturing in Special Economic

===== PDF_PAGE 135 =====

123
Zones (SEZs) that are available. In recent year, Laos has relatively advanced its
manufacturing. For example, many provinces initiate to exploit potash mines, which is
form as small and medium industries. In addition, there are feasibility studies of iron
exploitation project in different parts of the country. However, by setting the Republic
of Korea’s state of development as a benchmark 14 , Laos is still at an early stage of
transformation from agrarian to industrial economy.
With these respects, the 9 th Five-Year National Socio -Economic Development Plan
(2021-2025) has been set out as the fundamental direction for creating a new turning
point in socio -economic development. It aims to ensure quality establishment of the
political-ideological factors, the economic system and the material and technical basis,
which align with the green and sustainable development direction. The 9 th NSEDP also
aspires to ensure comprehensive preparation to support the country’s firm graduation
from the Least Developed Country status. There are 5 prior focusing areas, such as
agricultural, mining, tourism, logistics sectors, and digital economy.
One of the most important drivers of the 9 th NSEDP achievements is a support from
Science and Technology Innovation (STI). In order to implement the plan, it is essential
to focus on a development of enterprises, particularly micro, small, and medium
enterprises (MSMEs). Because MSMEs dominate econo mic activity and accounts for
substantial employment in Laos. According to the latest economic census (2019 -2020),
there are more than 130,000 enterprises of which 99.3% are MSMEs. As Lao MSMEs
struggle with many constraints, such as primarily level of management and organization,
limited use of technology, insufficient skilled labor, credits access constraints, and
market access limitations. It is necessary to improve MSMEs’ capacity to compete,
particularly an enabling environment for them to grow.
It is essential to develop t he National Master Plan for Technology and Innovation
Development and Application in Lao PDR (NMPSTR) and the National Policy on Science
and Technology Research (NMPSTR) as a fundamental of MSME innovation . The plans
