2. Vision
 Background
2.1.1 Changes in International Surrounding MSME Innovation
2.1.1.1 A Brief History of Innovation
The term “innovation” is simply defined as doing something different. It can also be
explained as a new idea, product, device, or novelty. However, throughout its history,
innovation has been broadly conceived and defined. It concerns any kind of novelty,
such as artistic, scientific, technological, organizational, cultural, social, or ind ividual
(Godin, 2008). It first appeared in the thirteenth century in law texts as “novation”,
which referred as a term for renewing contracts or a new version of a contract. In the
particularly entrenched religious atmosphere of the sixteenth and the seve nteenth
century in Europe, innovation gathered negative associations thrown at people who
sought to rewrite religious texts, resulting doctrinal innovation was anathema (Godin,
2010).
With respect to the Industrial Revolution in the nineteenth century, a term innovation
became associated with science and industry. It focused more on invention, particularly
technical invention. It was because of the rise of consumer culture, increased nu mber
of patens, and the support of the government on building labs for R&D. The twentieth
century representation of innovation had a more positive value, and it owes to
‘usefulness’ (Godin, 2011). In 1939, Austrian economist Joseph Schumpeter also defined
invention as an act of intellectual creativity, which implied to a new product. Meanwhile,
he also defined innovation as the adoption of that new thing or bringing a new product
to market. To make innovation’s meaning clearer , Godin (2010) emphasized that
innovation term between the 1950s-1980s could be defined as a process of theoretical
research in labs, applications of research, and commercialized products.
To sum up, regarding Canadian Historian Benoît Godin’s extent researches, innovation
is also an incarnation of terms like “imitation” and “invention”. It implies that innovation
was developed through three concepts from imitation (literary theory and art t heory),

===== PDF_PAGE 138 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
126
invention (history, sociology, management, and economics of technology), and
innovation of creativity.

2.1.1.2 MSME Innovation Policy in Asia
Based on the work of Park & Kim (2020), it offers an essential summary of innovation
policies in selected Asian countries, including necessity, definition, instruments, and
governance. They categorized these selected Asian countries into four group in
accordance of their income level and market size: 1) high -income countries (Japan and
Korea), 2) big-market countries (China and India), 3) middle and upper -middle income
countries (Malaysia and Thailand), 4) low-income countries (Bangladesh).

1) High-Income Countries
Japan is the first Asian country to succeed in industrialization and catch up to advanced
economies. Japanese innovation policies have been developed since the late 1800s,
which referred to the Meiji Restoration period. Its early innovation policies focused on
expanding access to international knowledge and accumulating indigenous human
capital. From the mid -1960s, the focus shifted to the development of in -house
indigenous technologies. The economic and technological gap between Japan and the
US narrowed as ind ustrial productivity increased sharply in the late 1980s. In the early
1990s, Japan expended research apparatus and promoted a new R&D system for the
country through the Science and Technology Basic Plan (STBP) by continuing strengthen
university and industry linkages, commercialization of intellectual assets, increase
funding of competitive research grants, and encourage four priority domains (life
sciences, IT, environmental sciences, and nanotechnology). With respect to governance
of innovation policies, the Comprehensive Council for Science, Technology, and
Innovation is in charge of the function of S&T budgeting, linkages among academia,
public institutions and industry, and R&D evaluation to support innovative research
investments beyond the existing framework – as well as the S&T master plan.
Korea experienced dramatic growth in innovation input -output and economic growth.
During the 1960s and 1970s, S&T were need for technology evolution. The government
aspired to enhance S&T capabilities through various instruments. For instance, the

===== PDF_PAGE 139 =====
Chapter 5. SME Innovation Support in Lao PDR

127
establishment of the Korea Institute of Science and Technology (KIST) and the Ministry
of Science and Technology (MoST) in 1966 and 1967, respectively (Figure 2) . The
government also enforced the S&T Promotion Act to promote technological innovation
in the country. Since 1971, the Korea Advanced Institute of Science and Technology
(KAIST) has been referred as the first national research university, particularly research-
oriented science, and engineering. As the Korean government launched the first
national R&D program, promoted private firms’ research institutes, and reformed tax
incentives to stimulate private firms’ R&D investment, Korea became a country where
companies led innovation, with private R&D expenditure surpassing public R&D
spending since the mid-1980s. Currently, the “Korean Vision 2040 STBP” is a foundation
of innovation policies, which includes issues for improved human society and economic
and scientific development. It implies that innovation policies that address social
problems and S&T issues are recently emerging.

2) Big-Market Countries
China has successfully acquired the market economy in the socialist planned economy,
and is expected to overtake the US in terms of GDP by 2030. After the foundation in
1949, the government established the Chinese Academy of Sciences and launched the
First Five -year Plan (1953 -1957) in respond to the demands of S&T development. It
aimed to modernize agriculture, industry, and defense. In the particularly entrenched
atmosphere of the Cultural Revolution (1966-1976), the accumulated research base was
undermined. From the late 1970s, China experienced a rapid change in planned
economy. The government increased corporate productivity by providing incentives
while ensuring managerial autonomy and participation to the global value chain. In
addition, the Technolog y Spreading Program was introduced and Innovation Fund for
Technology-based SMEs was established in 1990 and 1999, respectively. The
government also improved the mid- to long-term S&T development plan since the 2000s.
From the 2010s, China transformed from public and government -driven to private -
driven, which showed a significant transition to digital convergence and urbanization.
With respect to governance, the key ministries in charge of innovation policy are the
State Council, Ministry of Science and Tec hnology, and Ministry of Industry and
Information Technology.

===== PDF_PAGE 140 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
128
India is the top ten largest economy in the world, but its GDP per capita remain
insufficient to reach the level of the upper -middle-income country. During 1947 -1955,
India established many public laboratories and research institutions, and built the
political foundation for reflecting S&T. However, an attempt to link S&T with national
development plan was failed. In 1958, the linking S&T with the national development
plan was finally realized. As a result, the overall direction and resource allocation for the
S&T policies came from the Five-year Economic and Social Development Plan. With the
Science Policy Resolution (SPR) in 1958, India published the Technology Policy
Statement (TPS) in 1983, Science and Technology Policy (STP) in 2003, and Science
Technology and Innovation Policy (STIP) in 2013, which were foundation of India’s
innovation policies. With respect to governance, the advisory and coordinator bodies
consist of the National Innovation Council (NInC), the Science Advisory Council to the
Prime Minister (SAC-PM), the Science and Technology Advisory Committees (STAC), and
the Inter-Sectoral STAC (IS-STAC).

3) Middle and Upper-Middle Income Countries
Malaysia did not have a distinct science, technology, and innovation (STI) policy until
the mid-1980s. Although the first ministry, the Ministry of Technology, Research, and
Local Government (formerly MOSTI) was established in 1973. After that the government
launched the New Economic Policy (NEP) to focus on industrialization. The significant
STI-driven policy was the First National Science and Technology Policy, which also had a
separate chapter for S&T in the Fifth Five -year Plan since 1986. Decades later, with the
government assistance, Malaysia successfully transferred R&D capabilities from public
institutions to private sectors as other high-income countries. However, the number of
patent applications is limited for residents, which innovation activitie s depend on
foreign companies or foreigners. With respect to governance, STI policies overlap among
various ministries, such as the Ministry of Higher Education, the Ministry of International
Trade and Industry, the Ministry of Energy, Green Technology and Water, the National
Innovation Agency of Malaysia, et cetera.
Thailand’s import substitution policy shifted to an export -oriented strategy from the
early 1970s. In the early stages of industrialization, productivity was achieved through

===== PDF_PAGE 141 =====
Chapter 5. SME Innovation Support in Lao PDR

129
labor-intensive manufacturing. In order to improve labor’s capacity, the government
expended school and healthcare facilities as a part of poverty eradication agenda. In the
early 1990s, holistic approach for innovation and SME Promotion Master were launched,
reflecting the first and the second SME Promotion Master Plan for 2002-2006 and 2007-
2011, respectively. In the particularly entrenched of 20 years strategy, Thailand 4.0 was
designed to promote and support innovation, creativity, R&D, advanced technology, and
green technology. Currently, Thailand is already classified as an upper -middle-income
country. With respect to governance, STI policy operations is somewhat fragmented,
complex, and partially overlapped.

4) Low-Income Countries
After struggling from the war, Bangladesh became independent in 1971. But the
government was unsettled for around 20 years, which slow down the initiatives for
innovation policies. At the early stages of development, it focused on industrial policies
for economic development. In the late 1990s, t he first policy envisioned industrial
development. Its objectives outlined in the statement had clear sense of direction. In
the 2010s, the government formulated the National Science, Technology, and
Innovation (NST&I) Policy considering innovation-based development. The ICT Act 2006
(as amended in 2013) was also formulated to achieve Vision 2021 for Digital Bangladesh.
Several innovative projects have been executed by the information and communications
technology division since then. Despite these, the government attracted and deepened
FDI, making the GDP continued to grow and Bangladesh became a lower-middle-income
country in 2015. With respect to governance, the Ministry of Posts, Telecommunications,
and Information Technology was established in 2014.
To sum up, MSME innovation policy in high -income and big -market countries is
integrated and systematic. There is specific ministry or institutes responsible for R&D
and S&T that reduce overlap plans and implementation. In addition, research institutes
also play a key role in R&D and innovation. At the beginning, SME policy trends begin
with an expansion access to international knowledge and accumulating indigenous
human capital. Then its focus shifted to the development of in -house indigenous
technologies by establishing and improving research institutes. With strong political and

===== PDF_PAGE 142 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
130
social stability, it also plays an important part for investment and arm’s length of
business dealing. On the other hand, the governments have offered support to improve
technological innovation for the private sector. As the government leaves a room for
technological development to the private sector to be more autonomy, they also
legislate regulations to enforce and protect rights of businesses and consumers in order
to utilize technology sustainably.

2.1.1.3 Current Situation
Currently, the COVID -19 pandemic and its measures to control the outbreak entails
economic performance. The global economic turned down to -3.1% in 2020 from 3.0%
in 2019 (IMF, 2022a). An attempt to lift mobility restrictions and enhance economic
activities encouraged the global economy gradually recovers. It was estimated to grow
around 6.1% in 2021. However, a reimposition of mobility restrictions and energy crisis
have enforced a weaker position of the global growth in 2022. The growth rate is
expected to decline to 3.2% in 2022 (0.4% lower than in the April 2022 World Economic
Outlook) (IMF, 2022b). On the other hand, increasing food and energy prices squeeze
living standard and revise up global inflation, which worsen monetary and fiscal
situations worl dwide. As a result, global output would decline to 2.9% in 2023 (IMF,
2022b).
Despite a contraction of global economy, digitalization and innovation play an important
role in SME development and survival. SMEs face innovation challenges compared to
large enterprises due to limited resources. It is by far worse when it comes to developing
countries. They are constrained in their innovative activities due to a lack of financing
and a shortage of skilled labor. In contrast, SMEs seem to have a fast -decision-making
process when react to technological opportunities compared to larger comp anies,
particularly information technology. For instance, German SMEs use digitalization to
advance their position in the market and expand niche strategies. As SMEs want to
accelerate their digital transformation processes after hit by the pandemic, many EU
SMEs have turned to new technologies to meet customer expectations by empower
their employees and ensure a safe workplace. Moreover, by using technology, they find

===== PDF_PAGE 143 =====
Chapter 5. SME Innovation Support in Lao PDR

131
new ways to communicate and improve digital transaction to leverage their
competitiveness.

2.1.2 Changes in Domestic Surrounding MSME Innovation
2.1.2.1 Innovation in Lao Context
The term innovation in Lao context was complex due to language interpretation and its
development process from a term of invention. It first appeared as a term “invention”
on Section 1, Article 3, item 5 of Law on Intellectual Property (Amended), No.01/NA,
officially issued on December 20, 2011. Invention was defined as the technical solution
to create new pr oduct or process of production to resolve a specific problem. The law
also defined a term “utility innovation” as a new innovative work derived through
technical improvements, which involve simpler steps than with inventions (Section 1,
Article 3, item 7). According to the intellectual property law, it sets a foundation for
production development that any product of the human intellect is protected by the law,
which enhances production to be more innovative.
In 2013, a term innovation was first appeared on section 1, Article 3, item 1 of the Law
on Science and Technology, No.30/NA, officially issued on July 19, 2013. The law defined
it as the use of knowledge, capacity, production methods and scientific and
technological experience to research, invent, serve [and] develop new forms of quality
products and goods which are to be introduced to the society. With respect to R&D, it
was noticed on Decree on Science and Technology Development Fund, No.174/GOL, 23
May 2014. R&D was defined as science research and develop existing technology to
create new knowledge, application, technology systematically in order to design new
products to serve a society.
The term innovation and R&D are also emphasized on related legislations, wh ereas a
term research is generally used at research institutes. For example, the Law on
Technology Transfer, No.33/NA, officially issued on November 8, 2017, the Law on
Intellectual Property, No.38/NA, officially issued on November 15, 2017, the Law on
Advanced Technology, No.07/NA, officially issued on November 16, 2021 , and the Law
on the Promotion of Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises (Amended), officially issued

===== PDF_PAGE 144 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
132
in July 2022 . In order to ensure the implementation of R&D, the government also
legislated several decrees, such as the Prime Minister ’s Decree on Organization and
Activities of Ministry of Post and Telecommunications, No.22/PM, official issued on
January 16, 2017, and the Prime Minister ’s Decree on Organization and Activi ties of
Ministry of Science and Technology, No.314/PM, officially issued on September 29, 2017.
With respect to governance, in 2011, the Lao government established the Ministry of
Science and Technology (MST) in regard with the decree number 309/PM, officially
issued on September 28, 2011. It indicated the government’s interest in fostering R&D.
However, Lao PDR sought to reduce the overlap of R&D policies. In February 2021, the
government settled the MST and transferred its roles and responsibilities to the Ministry
of Education and Sports (MOES), the Ministry of Post and Telecommunications (MPT),
and other relevant line ministries. In October 2021, the government established the
Ministry of Technology and Communications (MTC) under the Decree on Organization
and Activit ies of Ministry of Technology and Communications, No.625/PM, officially
issued on October 22, 2021. It is responsible mostly for MST’s responsibilities and roles
in terms of governance and management of technology, innovation, telecommunication,
internet, post, and cyber security.

2.1.2.2 Innovation Policy in Laos
Since the country graduation in 1975, Lao economy has experienced substantial
structural change by transforming from a central -planned economy to a market -
oriented driven after a major reform in 1986. The reform showed significant attempts
to reduce state control over Lao economy and enhance more openness to international
trade and foreign investment. As the Lao government interested to foster the industrial
sector for economic growth contribution, they initiated the 20-Year Industrialization and
Modernization Strategy in 2001 and the 2030 Green Growth Strategy in 2018. The aims
of strategies focus on helping Laos to graduate from the Least Developed Country (LDC)
by 2020, but failed. In 2020, the government also initiated the 20 -Year Visions (2040),
10-Year Strategy (2030), first 5 -Year National Economy (2021 -2025), and the Startup
Development Plan. These strategies were aimed to strengthen a continuation of

===== PDF_PAGE 145 =====

industrialization and modernization direction in order to achieve LDC graduation by
2026. A summary of changes of innovation policies in Laos is displayed in [Figure 5-1].

2.1.2.3 SME in Lao Context
The definition for SMEs in various countries is different. In general, SMEs are defined by
both qualitative and quantitative definitions regarding economic development and
other circumstances . In terms of quantitative definition, the criteria for classifying
enterprises into different categories are usually number of employees, value of assets
and sales.
With respect to Lao context, definition of SMEs has been revised since 1996, which solely
based on number of employees. In 2004, SMEs were also categorized by the value of
assets and the annual average turnover. It stated that: “Small and Medium -Sized
Enterprises are independent establishments which are legally registered and are
running their activities in accordance with the laws of Lao PDR.” However, this definition

15 Some documents presented at the 3rd Ordinary Session of the IX National Assembly in 2022 showed the
contribution of digital economy to GDP could rise to 10% by 2040.

===== PDF_PAGE 147 =====

135
was not updated for over a decade. There were many critical concerns on its application,
especially for the terms of total assets and annual turnover, which tend to be under -
evaluated. As a result, many commercial banks established their own criteria of SME for
the use of loan assessment (Onphanhdala, 2013).

Regarding the Prime Minister’s Decree on Categories of Micro, Small and Medium

decree also categorizes MSMEs by sectors, which includes manufacturing, commercial,

2.1.2.4 Laws Related to SME Development in Lao PDR
Doing business in Lao PDR had to comply with the new Enterprise Law No. 11/NA, issued
date on November 09, 2005, which replaces Business Law No. 03/94/NA, issued date on
July 18, 1994. The legislation that directly relevant to SMEs was the Decree on the
Promotion and Development of Small and Medium Sized Enterprises No.42/PM
(consists of 6 Chapters and a total of 29 Articles), which the government has completed
the process of converting it into the Law on Promotion of Small and Medium Enterprises,
No.011/NA, issued date on December 21, 2011. It was amended and promulgated the
latest version in 2022.
The latest draft of SME law was just presented at the 3 rd Ordinary Session of the IX
National Assembly on June 29, 2022. It was amended and promulgated in July 2022 as
the Law on the Promotion of Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises . The law consists of
11 Parts, 5 Chapters, and 72 Articles. It determines principles, regulations, and measures
to implement, manage, and monitor MSMEs in order to align with current economic
performance and ease possible constraints of MSME development. The law also
emphasizes i nput promotion to reduce business costs for industrial and agricultural
MSMEs. It aims to enhance invention and innovation adaptation to lift quality of MSMEs
and start-up. Moreover, the law defines directions and policies to implement the SME
Promotion and Development Fund , which ensure a consistency with the Law on State
Budget (amended version). The revised context could help to solve various constraints
of previous SME fund implementation, which attempts to contribute to job creation,
income generation, living standards rising, and to lay the foundations for gradual
industrialization and modernization.
Based on the law (2022 version) , it emphasized directions and policies for MSME
promotion and development: (1) c reating an enabling regulatory and administrative
environment; (2) accessing to financial sources; (3) customs, tax, and accounting; (4)
building and developing entrepreneurs; (5) business Development Services; (6) MSME

===== PDF_PAGE 149 =====
Chapter 5. SME Innovation Support in Lao PDR

137
and large enterprises cooperation; (7) increasing productivity and quality; (8) accessing
and expanding markets; (9) business group/partnership; (10) business place
development; (11) using advanced and eco -friendly technology; (12) patent and
intellectual property right; (13) information providing and accessing; (14) enhancing
inputs; (15) enhancing invention and innovative adaptation; (16) Start -Up promotion;
(17) accessing to procurement; (18) other related policies.
In order to implement the Action Plan for SME promotion and development, all
measures should be planned and implemented in the form of projects and programs .
Those should be aligned with socio-economic development plans and the laws. Based
on Part VI of the law, it emphasizes MSME promotion organization as follows16:
(1) MSME Promotion Committee
(2) MSME Promotion Office
(3) MSME Promotion for the Private Sector

2.1.3 Current Situation
Over the past decade, Laos was one of the fastest -growing economies in ASEAN. The
GDP growth increased steadily since 1990, even its trends turned down in recent years,
but still ranging from around 6.3% to 7.2% on average regarding the mining sector. As
the pandemic was reported, Lao economic performance sharply dropped to 3 .3% and
2.3% in 2020 and 2021, respectively (NIER, 2021a, 2021b); World Bank, 2022a). However,
the projection by international agencies – World Bank (0.2% and 2.5%), ADB (-0.5% and
2.3%), and World Bank ( -1.8% and 2.5%) (ADB, 2022; IMF, 2022b; World Bank, 2021;
2022a). In 2022, Lao economy was expected to place in a stronger position due to
natural resources exports between 3.2% to 3.8% (ADB, 2022; IMF, 2022b; World Bank,
2021; 2022a). However, increased prices suppress living standards and employment,
which worsen fiscal and monetary situations, particularly high inflation, local currency
depreciation, and defaults.

16 More details can see in Annex 1

===== PDF_PAGE 150 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
138
Since the COVID -19 outbreak was reported, it forced people to change household
consumption habits, mainly food consumption. Affordable households preferred
cooking and eating at home by using grocery and food delivery services or advance
ordering for takeaway. Delivery and takeout possibilities increased significantly to serve
this change. Food delivery vendors expand their businesses, especially in large cities. In
addition, online transaction becomes more popular, but limited among food, groceries,
and small parcel delivery. Buyers and sellers use electronic methods for payment via
mobile phone. For example, Sengkhamkhoutlavong et al. (2021) revealed that more
than half of goods and services payment at micro and small shops in large cities is made
via mobile application. Their empirical results also confirmed that businesses relevant
to restaurants, entertainment venues, accommodations, retailers, and wholesalers are
more likely to adopt e-payment, which is statistically significant at a 10% level. Moreover,
several platforms that perform electronic transactions have been launched with an
increasing demand for online services. However, it is limited in large cities and capable
enterprises whom can access such resources.

 Future Vision 2030
To promote R&D and application of STI into all sectors. The setting goal is to enhance
innovative utilization for Lao SMEs and build its own technological innovation in the
future. It implies that it should aim to improve human resources in science and
technology R&D, create effective policy ecosystem to facilitate MSMEs, and strengthen
funding mechanism to support MSMEs’ operation and development. Generally, we set
our goals to improv e properly operation of MSMEs. It aims to strengthen capacity to
compete of Lao MSMEs in terms of novelty or originality of technology and/or business
model, marketability and growth potential of products or services, and financial stability.

 Roles of STI for MSME Innovation
2.3.1 R&D Performance
Although Laos has made considerable progress in lifting access to education in response
to increasing social demand and setting a lower secondary education for compulsory

===== PDF_PAGE 151 =====
Chapter 5. SME Innovation Support in Lao PDR

139
education regarding the education law 2015. It further needs to improve the quality and
relevance of the education system , R&D, and institutions. Asian Development Bank
(ADB), in its recent analysis of the Lao economy and human resources, identified an
increasing lack of educated and skilled human resources as a critical constraint to
inclusive growth in Lao s’s transforming economy and a constraint on private sector
growth and economic diversification (ADB, 2011, & ADB, 2012). In addition, h uman
development index shows that Laos ranks at the medium level of human development
(HDI, 2021).
The Global Innovation Index (GII) ranks world economies according to their innovation
capabilities. The GII consists of seven pillars: (1) institutes, (2) human capital and
research, (3) infrastructure, (4) market sophistication, (5) business sophisticatio n,
(knowledge and technology outputs), (7) creative outputs. To put Laos’ benchmarking
against other lower middle-income economies, Laos’ innovation performance is weaker
in most GII pillars, excepting business sophistication pillar with a relatively diffe rence.
Regarding to these indicators, Laos ranks the 117th among 132 economies in 2021, with
only 123 innovation inputs and 112 innovation outputs (WIPO, 2021).

2.3.2 Startup Innovative ICT products
The national master plan for startup was initiated and the startup promotion was
recently emphasized in the promulgated MSME law in 2020 and 2022, respectively.
Before this initiation, the startup has been operated under the 2011 SME promotion law.
There a re relatively numbers of startup in Laos, particularly foreign own and joint -
venture companies. This kind of company tend to adapts technology for operation, as a
result, startups at the early growth stage are those online platform businesses, such as
Delivery Hero (known as Food Panda delivery), Loca (taxi services), M-Money (electronic
wallet by the Lao Telecom company), etc. Business Start -ups in Laos seem to focus on
online platform development for e -payment, logistics services, digital currency
exploitation. For example, the Loca provides electric taxi services with Loca Pay that
accept international cards such as Visa for payment. Despite this, with respect to
business ventures, foreign developers plan to build the high technology park in
Vientiane Capital. It could contribute to digital economic development by bringing new
technologies and accelerate start-ups in Laos.
Whereas, manufacturing and production businesses are barely developed as Start -ups,
particularly agricultural production and other primarily manufacturing, etc. However,
agricultural producers aim to produce for exporting, particularly to China. It is beca use
of Lao producers could utilize the Laos -China Railway and gain more from agricultural
export preferences that granted by the Chinese government.

2.3.3 Governance
2.3.3.1 Science, Technology and Innovation
The MTC is the line ministry primarily responsible for governance and management of
technology, innovation, telecommunication, internet, post, and cyber security of Laos
as stipulated in the Prime Minister’s Decree No.625/PM, officially issued on October 22,
2021. It is also responsible mostly for MST’s responsibilities and roles in terms of
governance and management of technology and innovation, as the government sought
to reduce the overlap of R&D and S&T governance. MST’s roles also went to MOES and
MPT (as men tioned earlier in Section III, Subsection 1). The MTC consists of 15
departments, centers, and institutes, as shown in [Figure 5-3].
Department of Technology and Innovation (DTI) plays a major role in S&T for innovation.
It acts as a secretariat to the MTC, which is responsible for setting up national master
plans, policies, and strategies for R&D, and formulating legislation on the management
of technology and innovation. The department is in charge of promoting research on

===== PDF_PAGE 155 =====
Chapter 5. SME Innovation Support in Lao PDR

143
S&T and managing the interaction between science sectors and related organizations at
the central and the local government to implement and certify S&T research projects .
The DTI comprises with six divisions: (1) Administration Division, (2) Policy and Strategy
Division, (3) Technology Management Division, (4) Innovation Division, (5) Advanced
Technology Division, (6) Satellite Imagery Information Technology Division.
[Figure 5-3] Governance Structure of Ministry of Technology and Communications

Source: The Prime Minister’s Decree on Organization and Activities of Ministry of Technology and
Communications, No.625/PM, officially issued on October 22, 2021.

To strengthen international cooperation in terms of knowledge and technology
transferring, the Department of Planning and Cooperation (DPC) acts as a secretariat to
the MTC, serving as an interconnector between the MTC and other ministries. For
instance, it coordinates directly with the Ministry of Planning and Investment (MPI) for
using government budget and ODA in the science, technology, and innovation ( STI)
sector; whereas it coordinates with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs (MOFA) for bilateral,
multilateral, and international organization cooperation.

plays a role in functioning and coordinating with the Sectoral Scientific Committee of
Ministries, Equity and Establishment of the MTC. It is also responsible for preparing and

2.3.3.2 SME Promotion and Development
The MOIC was reformed and restructured since its establishment. It was recently
legislated in 2021 as stipulated in the Prime Minister’s Decree on Organization and
Activities of Ministry of Industry and Commerce, No. 604/PM, officially issued on
October 15, 2021. The MOIC acts as a secretariat to the government in responding to
governance and management at the macro level. Its responsibilities cover various fields,
such as industry, handicrafts, industrial zones, chemical management, internal and
international trade, and electronic commerce. The MOIC is also responsible for
protecting consumers, adjusting prices, managing the market, and enhancing businesses’
competition. Enterprise registration, promotion, intellectual property, measures, trade
facilitation, and regional and international integration are responsibilities of the MOIC.
Its governance structure consists of 14 departments, cabinet, and institutes, as shown
in [Figure 5-4].

Department of Small and Medium Enterprise Promotion (DOSMEP) was established
under the Prime Minister's Decree No. 42/PM, 20 April 2004, entitled "Decree on the
Promotion and Development of Small and Medium Sized Enterprises". Its main objective
is the promotion of sustainable development of Lao SMEs. Currently, based on the Law
on the Promotion of Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises (amended), issued in July
2022, DOSMEP changed to the name ‘MSME Promotion Office’ and its responsivities and
duties remain the same (more details can be seen in Annex 1).
It comprises with six divisions and fund: (1) General Affairs Division, (2) Entrepreneur
Development Division, (3) SME Support Division, (4) Productivity and Quality Division,
(5) SME Planning and Statistics Division, (6) SME Promotion Fund. The SME Promotio n
Fund plays an important role in financing and providing supports to SME promotion and
development programs.

2.3.4 Stakeholders
R&D stakeholders consist of the public and the private sectors. The public sector
includes line ministries, local government, and research institutions. The MOES and the
MTC are two primarily pillars of R&D for innovation. Regarding MOES’s roles, higher
education institutes are deepening their responsibilities for R&D, particularly the NUOL.
In addition, the MOES established the Research Institute for Technology and Innovation,
in the hope to strength en R&D and STI. Meanwhile MTC offers an assistance for
connecting to science sectors and related organization within central and local to
implement and certify S&T research. Both ministries also work together with other line
ministries in order to enhance R&D and STI development in Laos. Each line ministry has
its independent education and research institutes, as shown in [Table 5-4].
There is a relatively small number of the private sector as R&D stakeholders. However,
the LNCCI as an association takes a lead role in the private sector’s stakeholder.
Enterprises that have their own R&D section probably are Phu Bia Mining, DPS
Exploration and Mining Company Limited, HSMM Group of Companies, ALS, etc. Most
of them are mining exploitation companies. On the other hand, other enterprises usually
utilize research outputs from the public sector or their business partners rather than

===== PDF_PAGE 158 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
146
operate their original labs or R&D centers – outsourcing bases, such as CP Group. For
example, agricultural enterprises utilize research results from the NAFRI for agricultural
production, etc.
International stakeholders providing supports to innovation and technology
development, particularly MSME innovation, are Korea, China, and Japan. More details
can be seen in Annex 2.

2.3.5 Resources
2.3.5.1 Human Resources and Qualifications
The Ministry of Home Affairs (MOHA) reported that there are 176,151 civil servants in
2021. Teacher staffs’ coverage reach more than 40% of total government staffs. Since
teacher staffs have the largest proportion of government staffs, we could consider
teacher staffs at a university level as a key proxy on undertake R&D for innovation.
Four public universities confront with a degree level program and continuing programs
for general and higher education. There are National University of Laos (NUOL),
Champasak University (CU), Souphanouvong University (SU), and Savannakhet
University (SKU) since 1996, 2002, 2003, and 2009, respectively. In 2007, the University
of Health Sciences (UHS) was formed following the separation from a faculty of NUOL
and the Ministry of Health (MOH) administers the UHS . All 5 public universities offer
bachelor-degree programs, and only NUOL and UHS offer master degree programs and
PhD programs, while SKU offers master degree programs since 2019.

===== PDF_PAGE 161 =====
Chapter 5. SME Innovation Support in Lao PDR

149
[Table 5-5] shows the qualifications of faculty and staff members in two selected
universities in accordance to the latest data availability. It is obvious that NUOL’s most
members hold master’s degree, which accounts for approximately 54.2% (increases
from 44.8% in 2020). With respect to higher educational level, PhD holders of NUOL and
SKU account for approximately 10.5% and 3.3%, respectively. It implies that teaching
staff’s qualifications in the other universities remain low and insufficient. These higher
education institutes tend to have a lower qualification of academic staff comparing to
the MOES strategy that requires 30% of PhD staffs (actual is 9.4%), 60% of master degree
staffs (actual is 52.3%), and 10% of bachelor degree staffs (actual is 38.0%). Moreover ,
there is an insufficient academic staff who has an advanced academic title. For instance,
at the NUOL, only 5 lecturers hold professor title, and 87 lecturers hold associate
professor title.
One of the key reasons for the low quality of education and R&D in Laos is the lack of
qualified academic staff. Due to low salaries paid to academic staffs, education institutes
have difficulty retaining skilled and experienced academic personnel . It is leaving
institutes with too many young, underqualified, and inexperienced academic staff. Lack
of academic staff with advanced degrees is a key constraint on undertaking R&D in
innovation (Onphanhdala & Philavong, 2022).

2.3.5.2 Financial Resources
1) R&D Fund
The impact of R&D was mentioned earlier in the introduction part, which is a key factor
to improve the national economy. According to its importance and necessity,
governments around the world are broadening supports into R&D and STI. In 2020,
public expenditure on R&D accounts for approximately 2.63% of global GDP. Whereas
Korea and Japan’s R&D spendings are greater than t he global level, accounting for
approximately 4.81% and 3.26%, respectively. For Laos, public expenditure on R&D
accounts for approximately 0.04% in 2002, which is the lowest in Southeast Asian
nations (Figure 5-9).
Since the government interested in fostering S&T, the CAST was established together
with the setting of the Science and Technology Development Fund (STDF) that
established since 2014 under the MTC (former MST). Based on a regulation, the
amended Decision on STDF Implementation that stipulates in 2022, it also categorized
3 funding types for S&T development: (1) ODA to support STI research projects, STI
activities, research subsidiary, and rewards; (2) joint donation between STDF and
relevant sectors; (3) loans from STDF referred to MOF and Fund Board’s conditions.
However, based on actual implementation, the Survey on Capability of Science and
Technology Organization on Science and Technology Implementation in Lao PDR in 2020
supported by the STDF, there are 4 funding sources: (1) government budget, (2) fees, (3)
external sources, (4) ODA. The government budget source for R&D and S&T refers to
annual 1% of total public expenditure. The spending is limited due to the fiscal deficit
and budget constraints. As a result, at the beginning, the budget only went to natural
science research field. Currently, it splits to both natural and social science research
fields.
Findings in Table 6 show that a major funding source for S&T is from the government
budget, which accounts for around 69% of total sources. The ODA also plays an
important role in supporting S&T implementation. When categorizing sources by
implementation, higher education institutes received the largest proportion of funding.
They obtained funds from the central budget. Meanwhile, R&D implementation

===== PDF_PAGE 163 =====
Chapter 5. SME Innovation Support in Lao PDR

151
(projects) received most supports from the ODA, which accounted for approximately 55%
of total funding for R&D implementation. On the other hand, MTC implantation share a
similarly proportion of sources between the central budget (45%) and ODA (44%).

With respect to expenditure on STDF decision, the fund can be categorized into 3 types:
(1) R&D and STI promotion fund for developing products and services, by using 50% of
STDF’s annual budget, (2) S&T research fund shall use 40% of STDF’s annual budget, (3)
fund for policy development and capacity building of STI shall use 10% of STDF’s annual
budget. For administrative expenditure shall not exceed 5% of total values of research
project.

2) SME Promotion Fund
The government authorized to establish the SME Promotion and Development Fund to
provide support to SME promotion and development programs under the Prime
Minister’s Decree No. 123/PM, officially issued on March 3, 2010 . The SME Promotion
and Development Fund shall be managed by the National SME Promotion and
Development Office (former the Department of SME Promotion and currently the MSME
Promotion Office , Ministry of Industry and Commerce). Separate regulations for the
management of the SME Promotion and Development Fund will be issued. The latest
regulation in regarding to the implementation of SME development fund refers to the
Government Decree on SME Promotion Fund, No. 299/GOL, officially issued on
September 4, 2019, which amended from the Decree No. 123/PM promulgated in 2010.
Currently, the SME Promotion Fund was emphasized on the latest MSME law (2022
version).

a small proportion of enterprise expor ts to foreign markets. The low level of foreign

159
Enterprises face several internal and external constraints. The external constraints of
SMEs are divided into four subgroups : (1) competition and taxes, (2) infrastructure,
(3) operational costs , (4) regulation issues. On the other hand, shortage of human
resource, low level of technology and lack of capital are three major internal constraints
of Lao SMEs. Based on the enterprise survey20 by the GIZ in 2013 revealed that the level
of technology as a perceived constraint of enterprises in Laos. Many medium enterprises
rated low level of technology (GIZ, 2014). Lack of capital was also considered a very big
issue for medium enterprises and a big issue for a similar proportion of micro enterprise.
Several SME surveys by using the CIS local assessment methods21 in 5 main dimensions:
(1) Management Strength (MS), (2) Technical Capability (TC), (3) Sales Capability (SC), (4)
Human Resource Capability (HR) , (5) Organization Strength (OS) were conducted to
evaluate an operational credibility of SMEs in different sectors. By using this method,
there are several SME surveys in Vientiane capital, Luang Prabang, Savannakhet,
Champasak provinces, Luang Namtha, and Oudomxay. 22 In Vientiane capital, a survey
was conducted to assess manufacturing sector in 2017. Whereas, surveys conducting in
Luang Prabang, Savannakhet, and Champasak provinces focused on services sector,
particularly tourism sector in 2018. Its subsectors include accommodations, restaurants,
and souvenir retails and travel agents. In 2019, CIS assessment surveys were conducted
in Luang Namtha and Oudomxay provinces, which also focused on tourism sector.
Regarding survey results, Lao SMEs barely use technology to improve their operations
due to a small investment of human resource development and R&D. Whereas, joint
venture or foreign own enterprises seem to show better performance in all dimensions

20 They are small scale surveys since 2005 by the GIZ (German organization) every two years covering five major
provinces, namely Vientiane Capital, Luang Namtha, Luang Prabang, Savannakhet, and Champasak. All the surveyed
reports are accessible online both in Lao and English.
21 Credibility Index for SMEs (CIS) is a tool to evaluate the operational credibility of SMEs and will be applied to SMEs
in ASEAN Member States (AMSs) to enhance competitiveness of SMEs in the region. The CIS base model was
developed by Japanese expert team from Advanced Institute of Industrial Technology, which comprises a set of
checklists formed by six appraisal dimensions including Management Strength, Technical Capability, Sales Capability,
Human Resource Capability, Organization Strength and Financial Strength covering 478 questions. The CIS base
model was developed from the base model since 2017 (Dr. Phanhpakit Onphanhdala led the production at that
time). The assessment covers manufacturing and services sectors.
22 More details can be seen in Annex 3.

of CIS assessment, as shown in [Figure 5-7] (APEN, 2017; Onphanhdala and Philavong,
2017; Onphanhdala et al., 2018, Onphanhdala et at., 2020).
Despite the CIS assessment, there is also a working paper on private sector development
under the Lao-China Economic Corridor (LCEC) that investigated impacts of the LCEC or
the Laos -China Railway (LCR) on the Lao private sector. 23 Results of Key Informant
Interview in O udomxay, Luang Prabang, and Xiengkhouang provinces reveal that the
private sector expected lower cost of transport and increasing number of tourists. This
could offer an opportunity to generate more income if Lao enterprises would be able to
access Chinese market. However, Lao firms struggle with several constraints in terms of
readiness when the LCR operates. It is due to difficulties to access to finance, lack of
skilled labor and technique, and out of date machinery (Bannalath and Insisienma y,
2021).
Most Lao MSMEs are primarily management and organization. They barely use
technology into production and services, except some business start -ups. For these
reasons, the MSME innovation Master Plan should focus on building capability of
MSMEs by initiating technological utilization and further extend to create own
technology.

2.3.7 Issues, Challenges, and Opportunities
2.3.7.1 Issues
With respect to resources, the government invests in human resource management by
establishing higher education institutes, vocational institutes, and skill development
institutes over the past decades. They have been increased in number, but limited
qualification. There are scholarships for excellent students to study abroad from the
government and international grants. But due to low salaries paid to academic staffs,
education institutes have difficulty retaining skilled and experienced academic
personnel. On the other hand, although, there is SME Promotion Fund providing
financial resources for MSMEs, it seems MSMEs find it is difficult to access to those
credits comparing with large enterprises. According to these obstacles, the MSME
innovation Plan should emphasize public expenditure on human resource development.
Further expanding to oversea scholarships is to accumulate human capital and high paid
to returned students is to retain advanced degree personnel. Moreover, it is essential to
ensure accessing to credits that is provided to MSMEs.
With respect to institutions, Lao SMEs as well as R&D implementation are at the early
stage of innovative development , which focus on technology utilization rather than
invention or creation . There are numbers of research and education institutes, but
applied research to improve products and services is poor. Research based course is
insufficient to build research base and human capital. Research fund is limited. The
government should enhance and focus on more on basic research and innovation. The
MSME innovation Plan should also emphasize a consistency between various sectors
and a cooperation between the public and the private sectors.
With respect to governance, administrations that responsible for SME and innovation
were assigned to avoid overlap responsibilities. The government also lifts the SME
promotion department to more autonomous MSME Promotion Office. Ministry of
Technology and Communications was established and other responsibilities were
assigned to related line ministries. However, collaboration mechanism should be
strengthened and systematically to avoid sophisticated procedures and burdens.

===== PDF_PAGE 175 =====
Chapter 5. SME Innovation Support in Lao PDR

163
With respect to policies, this year, the government has legislated the latest law of MSME
promotion and taken an initiation to resolve previous challenges of MSME development
process. But the law should emphasize that loans for MSMEs should be allowed to invest
overseas to provide more room for doing businesses internationally. In addition, as the
government also attempts to ease procedures for facilitate doing business
environments, but the effort is insufficient to catch up with demands and global trends.
Despite these, in terms of R&D, policies lack of a term development, particularly in
science and technology research for SMEs’ innovation. Definition, conditions, and
instrument are not clear and complex. Implementation periods of R&D and S&T projects
are not appropriate to support a continuation of researches, which obstruct to
accumulate knowledge and human capital. Therefore, it is essent ial to modify research
term. For example, short-term, mid-term, and long-term projects could be determined
as 1 year, 2-3 years, and 4-5 years, respectively. Besides this, it is necessary to strengthen
a continuation of researches and implementation after project completion. In addition,
regulations related research, such as terms, intensive, cooperation, public governance,
and MSME development should be reconsidered and reformed, particularly the
government assistance to support resources for R&D and S&T implementation.

Opportunity, and Threat align with MSMEs, Start-ups, and R&D aspects.

3. Strategies and Objectives

 Strategy 2. MSME Innovation:
The MSME Innovation Plan is basically set as the five years plan that align with the Five-
Years SME Development Plan (2021-2025), the Five-Years Manufacturing and Handicraft
Sector Development (2021-2025), and the 9th NSEDP.
It aims to p romote the development MSMEs to generate demands for technologies .
Main objectives include:

3.1.1 Objective 2-1: Improved human resources in Science and
Technology R&D.
3.1.1.1 Action 2-1-1: Promotion of scientific R&D with a concentration of
basic research
1) Directions
• Offering policies and improve infrastructure for strengthening human capital, building
human resource, and enhancing R&D, particularly via education institute, research
institutes, and broadening access to international knowledge and technology.
• Providing fund research projects.
• Enhancing international cooperation to support researches.
• Reconsider and reform regulations related researches to strengthen a continuation of
researches and implementation after project completion, and extend various fields of
research projects.

2) Implementations
• The MOES and the MTC shall be responsible for promote scientific R&D with a
concentration of basic researches, allowing local and international researchers to
participate academic institutes for enhancing more research projects.

===== PDF_PAGE 178 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
166
• Both ministries shall be responsible for review and legislate relevant regulations to
manage and enhance R&D, particularly among education institutes.
• Trainings and capacity builds are essential to improve human resources, particularly
academic staffs, government staffs, entrepreneurs, etc.
• The MOES and the MTC shall work with the MOIC to strengthen cooperation between
the public and the private sectors, providing opportunities to accelerate innovation and
R&D.
• The MOF shall be responsible for reallocating national budgetary for R&D fundings. In
addition, international sources of funding should be managed appropriately to support
R&D.
• International cooperation is essential for exchanging and transferring knowledges,
lesson learnt, and technologies.

3) Evaluation
• To evaluate R&D performance, R&D public expenditure should be taken into account.
It includes a total expenditure, expenditure by size of research project, by research field,
a percentage of GDP, etc.
• Quality and quantity of academic and research works are required to evaluate R&D
performance. It should focus more on accepted academic journals and other
publications internationally. One of indicators could be Global Innovation Index (GII).
The GII consists of seven pillars: (1) institutes, (2) human capital and research, (3)
infrastructure, (4) market sophistication, (5) business sophistication, (knowledge and
technology outputs), (7) creative outputs.
• To evaluate human resource development, Human Development Index (HII) could be
one of indicators, particularly the knowledge dimension.

===== PDF_PAGE 179 =====
Chapter 5. SME Innovation Support in Lao PDR

167
3.1.1.2 Action 2-1-2: Qualified human resources and skilled labor to
enhance entrepreneurship and improvement of MSMEs’
competitiveness
1) Directions
• Investing in human resources to offer more opportunities to become entrepreneurs.
• Creating opportunities for people to access sources of capital, production techniques,
and modern technology.

2) Implementations
• The MOES and the MOIC should continue their roles to develop human resources and
provide advises to strengthen potential entrepreneurs.
• The MOIC shall take a lead to promote and develop MSMEs, particularly the MSME
Promotion Office in order to offering low interest loans for MSMEs.
• In order to ensure funding, the fund, BOL, and MOF should harmonically collaborate in
accordance with regulations, policies, and implementation to be consistency.
• International cooperation is essential for exchanging and transferring knowledges,
lesson learnt, and technologies.

3) Evaluation
• To evaluate human resource development, Human Development Index (HII) could be
one of indicators, particularly the knowledge dimension.
• The growth of local business registration and capital investment.
• Local divestment trends to ensure the growth of businesses and the improvement of
local entrepreneurs.

3.1.1.3 Action 2-1-3: Strong and modern cooperation and integration with
the region and internationally
1) Directions
• Strengthening cooperation to receive attention from external assistance agencies or
foreign universities.

===== PDF_PAGE 180 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
168
• Strengthening and consolidating research capacity development activities with
universities or research institutes at the subregional and the international levels.
• Enhancing harmonization among member universities or research institutes in terms
of R&D and credit transfer systems, etc.

2) Implementations
• The MOES shall build a closer partnership in science, technology, and innovation
including through jointly formulating and implementing the masterplan is an aim to
connect our strategies of innovative development and foster new impetus for
cooperation.
• The MTC and the MOES take a lead to strengthen i nternational cooperation for
exchanging and transferring knowledges, lesson learnt, and technologies.
• In order to ensure funding, the fund, BOL, and MOF should harmonically collaborate in
accordance with regulations, policies, and implementation to be consistency.

3) Evaluation
• To evaluate Action 2 -1-3, numbers of annual co -research projects/outputs shall be
considered as well as research findings’ utilization.
• A process of scholarship/credit transferring regionally and internationally.

3.1.2 Objective 2-2: Effective policy ecosystem to facilitate MSMEs.
3.1.2.1 Actions 2-2-1: Effective technology and innovation utilization in
accordance with green and environmentally friendly
1) Directions
• Offer opportunities to obtain technology transfer and adapt into businesses.
• Promote patent, trademarks, and R&D investment to develop products.
• Helping MSMEs identify, acquire, assimilate and utilize existing technologies from
foreign or domestic sources.
• Creating an agency and/or organization that plays a linkage role between technology
owners and MSMEs.

===== PDF_PAGE 181 =====
Chapter 5. SME Innovation Support in Lao PDR

169
• Focus on implementing priority sectors in accordance with the green growth strategy
to bring harmony between the various sectors, including science and technology.
• Increase the efficiency of production and services, create added value, upgrade
agricultural production to advance to the industry that uses modern science and
technology.
- The agricultural sector:
▪ To raises the productivity and use of modern tools, application of new
techniques and sciences and innovations.
▪ To create access to credit and markets; strong agricultural infrastructure and
natural disaster resilience.
- Industry sector: Technical-Science-Innovation-Wisdom-Technology, with packaging
and labeling to enhance product quality and competitiveness.
- Service sector: Upgrading telecommunication, logistics and postal transportation to
be agile and modern in order to support commercial transactions, clearing and other
activities efficiently.

2) Implementations
• The MTC and the MOIC take a lead to promote and development MSMEs and enhance
their competitiveness and shall harmonically collaborate in accordance with
regulations, policies, and implementation to be consistency.
• MTC, as key stakeholders together with MOIC and MOES, responsible for technological
innovation and R&D, which include patent, trademarks, research outputs,
technological transferring, and innovation endorsement.
• As a key stakeholders, the MTC shall work with line ministries to utilize technological
innovation improve production.

3) Evaluation
• Adoption of technology into production checklists and spending.
• The growth of patent and trademarks registration.
• Business spending on R&D investment.

===== PDF_PAGE 182 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
170
• Value-added products and market extension.

3.1.2.2 Actions 2-2-2: Efficient public administration in accordance with the
direction of public administration by law
1) Directions
• To formulating regulations, policies, and laws by ensuring its development on the fair
competition, equality and beneficial to all, particularly MSMEs.
• To offer policy and trade ecosystem to facilitate MSMEs, such as to enhance E -
commerce, electronic payments, logistics, productivities, and services to growth
sustainably.
• To be an interconnector to collaborate with MSMEs, Startup sectors to support them
adapt into modernization services and access digitalization.
• To improve the E-government for reducing doing business procedures and enhance
transparency by mobilize and manage government services. For example, 50% of
government services should be done via online to rise indicators of incomes through
digital services.
• To strengthen one-stop central and local government services.
• Strengthen strong and modern cooperation, integration, and interconnection within,
with the region and internationally.

2) Implementations
• The MOIC takes a lead to promote and development MSMEs and enhance their
competitiveness, particularly the MSME Promotion Office. The ministry should
continue its role and ensure an enforcement of the latest MSMEs promotion law.
• The MOIC shall work with the BOL to improve and accelerate electronic payment to
facilitate business operations.
• The PWT shall responsible for logistics and infrastructure improvement.
• The MTC shall responsible for improve the E-government in accordance with relevant
ministries for reducing doing business procedures and enhance transparency by
mobilize and manage government services.

3) Evaluation
• A proportion of Lao, joint-venture, and foreign own MSMEs – numbers, assets, markets,
etc.
• Improved doing business indicators and rankings.
• A Frequency and amount of value of electronic transaction.
• The growth of business productivities.
• 50% of government services should be done via online to rise indicators of incomes
through digital services.

3.1.2.3 Action 2-2-3: Strong and efficient cooperation between the public
and the private sectors
1) Directions
• Enhancing cooperation mechanism between the public and the private sectors
efficiently and verifiably.
• Development of technology/industrial parks to facilitate interactions among
enterprises, entrepreneurs, and researchers.

2) Implementations
• The MOES and the MTC shall work with the MOIC to strengthen cooperation between
the public and the private sectors, providing opportunities to accelerate innovation and
R&D.
• The MOIC shall responsible for expanding international markets for exports by
advancing bilateral and multilateral cooperation with major partners.

3) Evaluation
• Improved quality and reduced quantity of cooperation procedures.

3.1.3 Objective 2-3: Strengthening funding mechanism to support MSMEs’
operation and development.
3.1.3.1 Actions 2-3-1: Finance reliable to sources to support MSMEs’
operation and development
1) Directions
• Strengthen funding mechanism to help MSMEs access to credits and improve their
liabilities.
- Conditions, collateral, terms, interest rate, etc.
▪ Enhance MSMEs to use technology for financial management and transaction
for improve its capacity to compete in terms of payment facilitation and
transparency.
- Encourage MSMEs to proper manage financial account documents, bookkeeping, tax
payment document, installation daily maintenance and real estate owners.
- Ensure cooperation mechanism between line ministries relevant MSME promotion
funding in accordance with efficiency, transparency– MSME Promotion Fund (MOIC),
BOL, MOF, etc.

2) Implementations
• The MOIC takes a lead to promote and development MSMEs and enhance their
competitiveness, particularly the MSME Promotion Office.
• MSME Promotion Office/Fund plays a key role to support an operation and
development of MSMEs by offering low interest loans to businesses.
• The BOL and the MOF shall be responsible for offering loans for MSMEs and
reallocating national budgetary for MSME promotion.
• The BOL and the MOF should harmonically collaborate in accordance with regulations,
policies, and implementation to be consistency.

3) Evaluation
• Credits and loans for MSMEs in terms of conditions, amounts, etc.
• Reduced NPLs.

organization systematically
1) Directions
• Create opportunities to access sources of capital, production techniques, modern

• To develop digital and ICT infrastructures and exchanging platforms for MSMEs.
• To assist MSMEs to create plan, set up management, and business direction.

• The ministry should continue its role and ensure an enforcement of the latest MSMEs
promotion law.
• Under the MOIC’s supervision, the MSME Promotion Fund plays a key role to support

• In order to ensure funding, the fund, BOL, and MOF should harmonically collaborate in

• The MTC shall responsible for ITC infrastructure improvement to ensure ICT coverage,

• The MOF shall be responsible for reallocating national budgetary for infrastructure
expenditure.

• ITC infrastructure coverage.

 Periods of S&T IC in Korea and their Evolution
The periods of international S&T cooperation can be divided from the Korean war to
the present into five sub -periods: period of technology aids, technology introduction,
technology cooperation, cooperation expansion, a d v a n c e d cooperation a n d S & T
d i p l o m a c y , respectively. This division is not exclusive, but it can be seen that
although overlapping characteristics exist, they represent the most important
characteristics as it is shown in the [Figure 6-1].

The S& T IC strategy was changed in terms of organization, volume, type counter-part,
program and so on over the periods. The main changes are as follows; organization: from
ministry of Economy to ministry of Science & Technology ; volume: $ 1 million ( ’51-’55)
for Tech. Aid to $ 2.86 billion ( ’21) for ODA; joint research: KRW 1.6 billion ( ‘85) to 17
billion (’16); type: bilateral for assistance to bilateral for cooperation with multilateral
relations; technology assistance, technology adoption, technology cooperation, human
exchange, joint research, hosting research institutes, etc ; c ounter-part: advanced
countries centered to diverse counter parts such as i nternational organizations,
developing counties, northern countries (Russia, Eastern Europe, etc.) ; R&D Program:
joint research (’85) and cooperation foundation (’96).

 Technology Aid Period (1951-1971)
In this period, there was the ideological confrontation between capitalism and
communism after the end of World War II. Due to the Korean war (1950), the economic
foundation of Korea was devastated, and this became a social background in which
Korea had no choice but to pursue economic development with foreign aid. Therefore,
in the 1950s, Korea had no choice but to rely on aid from advanced countries including
the United States for capital and materials for economic activities. In the early 1960s,
per capi ta GNP was only about $100, and dependence on the primary industry
represented by agriculture and fisheries was absolute, and industrialization was not
progressing.
Against this background, from the early 1960s, the government established an economic
development plan to achieve independent economic development and began to
promote it in earnest. At that time, the domestic industrial base was weak, so the
technology needed to foster strategic industries was imported from advanced countries
and then digested. In the early 1960s, the government tried to resolve the legitimacy of
the unstable regime through economic development. And this interest in economic
development le ads to the scientific and technological promotion, and the technical
promotion five -year plan is established to support the first five -year economic
development plan that started in 1961. This plan has the meaning of being the first

===== PDF_PAGE 200 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
188
comprehensive plan to promote the development of science and technology as a whole,
and shows that the government recognized that science and technology policy is one of
the important driving forces for national economic development beyond the existing
educational policy. Ministry of Science and Technology, 2008).
At the same time, at the Korea -US summit in May 1965, the Korea Institute of Science
and Technology (KIST) was established with the support of the US to establish a research
institute. In January, the Science and Technology Scarlet Act was passed by the National
Assembly, and on April 21, 1967, the Ministry of Science and Technology was established.
The 1 st Five-Year Technology Promotion Plan is significant in that it is the first
comprehensive plan to promote the overall science and technology. It shows th at
science and technology policy was recognized as one of the important driving forces for
national economic development beyond the existing educational policy. With the
establishment of this plan, the development of science and technology was recognized
as a national task and promoted as a part of the national long -term development plan.
included in the plan.
International cooperation in science and technology from immediately after the Korean
War to the early 1970s was a period in which Korea mainly received aid from advanced
countries and international organizations. In 1963, a technology grant project that
invited trainees from developing countries was also started, but the amount of funding
received from the United States and international organizations was larger than Korea's
own funds. In addition, this period can be defined as the period when Korea receiv ed
technical assistance mainly from advanced countries or international organizations
because it was insignificant compared to the amount of support received from advanced
countries or international organizations.
Technical assistance came from advanced countries such as the United States and Japan,
international organizations of the United Nations and UNDP, and multilateral
cooperation organizations such as the Colombo Project, and most of them were grant
aid. Among these, aid from the United States was the largest, followed by the United
Nations and the Colombo Program.

===== PDF_PAGE 201 =====

 Technology Introduction Period (1972-1980)
In the 1970s, a new international technological cooperation plan was raised with the UN
centering on it. As a representative example, the Country Program has been adopted
since 1972, centering on the UNDP, the leading organization for technical cooperation
among UN organizati ons. In the past, the support method based on the unilateral
decision of the donor country was changed to a new technology cooperation method
centered on the recipient country that provides support in accordance with the long -
term development plan of the recipient country.
Meanwhile, with the advent of the GATT system, the protectionism policies of advanced
countries have strengthened and the international free trade environment has
deteriorated significantly. In addition, resource nationalism emerged internationally as
the Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries (OPEC) was established in 1972, and
the Middle East War broke out the following year, resulting in an oil shock. Such rapid
changes in the international environment were adversely affected by Korea, which was
pursuing export-oriented economic development.
As the basic direction of economic policy in the 1970s was set to advance the industrial
structure by fostering the heavy and chemical industry, science and technology policies
were also promoted to support it. Heavy and chemical industrialization requires

===== PDF_PAGE 202 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
190
enormous capital and technology. Accordingly, the government pursued a bold foreign
capital and technology introduction policy, unlike the 1960s, when foreign capital was
regulated. The government's response to changes in the international environment in
the 1970s was to establish the foundation of industrial technology to support heavy and
chemical industrialization led by the government. At that time, most of the technologies
required by private companies were imported from abroad or embodied in capital goods,
so the ability to absorb and digest them was accumulated. To this end, the Ministry of
Science and Technology enacted the Technology Development Promotion Act in 1972
to promote the independent development of industrial technology and the introduction
of appropriate advanced technology, and to support digestion and improvement. This
Act stipulated both direct support such as subsidy payment and policy finance, and
indirect support such as tax and customs support and market creation. In addition,
government-funded research institutes for each specialized field established under the
leadership of the Ministry of Science and Technology were mainly responsible for the
development of independent industrial technology and support for the introduction and
digestion of advanced technologies by companies.
Korea's international cooperation in science and technology initially started as a form of
economic cooperation, but with the creation of the Ministry of Science and Technology
in 1967, it became possible to systematically and comprehensively promote
technological cooperation that had been sporadically promoted by each ministry. In
1972, in order to support the 3rd Five -Year Economic Development Plan and the Third
Five-Year Science and Technology Plan, the manpower supply and demand plan and
R&D strategy, t he Five -Year Plan for International Technology Cooperation was
announced. The significance of the Five -Year International Technical Cooperation Plan
for international cooperation in science and technology is that science and technology
are not a means of e conomic growth, but an important factor leading the way, and
advanced technology even if a price is paid rather than passive technical cooperation. In
1979, the second liberalization of technology introduction was carried out, and a
technology introduction consultation center was established at KIST to introduce and
mediate appropriate technology following the liberalization of technology introduction.
In addition, information support for technology introduction contracts was
strengthened, and tax and loan support were provided in accordance with the

===== PDF_PAGE 203 =====
Chapter 6. STI International Cooperation in Korea

191
Technology Development Promotion Act for cases where the technology was digested/
improved after technology was introduced.

It started to participate in the international science and technology network from the
technology source period. Some examples are the invitation of Korean scientists and
engineers from abroad, which began in 1968, the dispatch of domestic scientists and
engineers to international conferences, and the invitation of eminent foreign scientists.
These projects are meaningful in that Korea wanted to participate in international R&D
networks beyond a one -sided technology source and promote leading international
cooperation.

In this period, the main changes of S&T IC are as follows; o rganization: international

million ( ‘80); t ype: bilateral for aid to bilateral for cooperation (Tech. Aid, Tech.
Introduction, Tech. Donation ); counter-part: including developing countries as well as
international organizations, developed counties, developing countries.

 Technology Cooperation Period (1981-1990)
In the 1980s, major advanced countries, including the United States, invested huge
amounts of money and manpower in the development of advanced technologies while
seeking a transition to a new development strategy in order to escape the swamp of a
long economic recession. As a result, the development of advanced technologies has
been rapidly developed, centering on advanced countries. Technology protectionism
began to intensify as competition for technology development between advanced
countries developed fiercely.
Developed countries have provided protection for new intellectual property rights,
including software and material patents, while preventing leakage of their advanced
technological information to competitors. In addition, through trade negotiations, etc.,
measures to effectively protect the country's industry and technology have been
strengthened. Meanwhile, Korea achieved rapid economic growth with the
development of heavy and chemical industries in the 1970s, but in the 1980s, the Korean
economy reached a limit where it could no longer maintain its competitiveness in the
past, which relied on cheap labor. Accordingly, the demand for economic advancement
and industrial structure reorganization increased, and a new approach that reflected this
situation was required in terms of science and technology policy.
In the 1980s, as Korea's export competitiveness, which relied on cheap labor, reached
its limit, the government took note of the need to actively respond to changes in the
international economic environment and technological environment, so that Korea's
science and technology must enter the level of advanced countries. Therefore, in the

===== PDF_PAGE 205 =====
Chapter 6. STI International Cooperation in Korea

193
1980s, the 5th Five -Year Economic and Social Development Plan: Science and
Technology Sector Plan (1982 -1986) and the 6th Five -Year Economic and Social
Development Plan: Science and Technology Sector Plan (1987-1991) were established.
In addition, in 1986, the Ministry of Science and Technology established a long-term plan
for science and technology development for the 2000s (1987 - 2001) in order to
systematically promote science and technology policies from a long-term perspective.
In order to continuously pursue economic development while actively responding to the
above-mentioned changes in the domestic and overseas environment, it was important
to secure independent scientific and technological research and development
capabilities to develop cutting -edge technologies. With this shift in perception,
technology development was emphasized as a national task, leading to the conversion
of export drive policy to technology drive policy.
In addition, a project was developed to actively attract excellent scientific brains from
abroad and to support the dispatch of excellent domestic manpower abroad.
In the 1980s, Korea tried to develop cutting -edge technology in Korea beyond simple
technology introduction from advanced countries, but international cooperation in
science and technology with major advanced countries was still important. However, as
Korea's economy grew, it tried to reestablish international cooperation as a mutually
beneficial relationship on an equal footing with advanced countries, away from the
position of receiving support unilaterally. Therefore, it actively promoted the signing of
science and technology cooperation agreements for equal cooperation with major
advanced countries that had been promoting scientific and technological cooperation,
and based on this, various cooperative projects represented by international joint
research were promoted.
Until the early 1980s, Korea's technology introduction was limited to major advanced
countries, and among them, the United States and Japan accounted for most. From 1962
to 1985, 78% of Korea's technology introductions and 74.5% of technology introductions
were made to the United States and Japan (International Science and Technology
Cooperation Statistics, 1997).

===== PDF_PAGE 206 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
194
If technology introduction is concentrated in a specific country, if that country's
technology protectionism is strengthened, it may become difficult to acquire core
technology or necessary technology, and there are also concerns about weakening
technological competitiveness and technology dependence. Therefore, it is necessary to
secure technological diversity through technological cooperation with various countries,
strengthen its own R&D capabilities, and secure new markets. As a result, the number
of countries that signed the Science and Technology Cooperation Agreement, which can
be said to be the basis of scientific and technological cooperation, increased from 5
countries in 1979 to 36 countries in 1989.

Since the late 1970s, as industrialization progressed, the number of technology
introductions and payments for technology introduction have gradually increased. In
this situation, the international joint research project was promoted to meet the
increasing demand for technology and to strengthen its own research field (Science and
Technology Yearbook, 1986).

===== PDF_PAGE 207 =====
