195
After the international joint research project was promoted in earnest in 1985, looking
at the performance of the international joint research project, a total of 354 tasks were
promoted from 1985 to 1990, and a total of 16.8 billion won was invested.

By signing a science and technology cooperation agreement with advanced countries, it
was intended to transform into a reciprocal relationship unlike the past, where support
was only unilateral. Technology diplomacy-type international cooperation in science and
technology, which had been weak in the past, was strengthened . The implementation
of the international joint research project has the meaning of a full-fledged international
cooperation policy for science and technology . This is a different type of international
cooperation in science and technology from the past.

 Cooperation Expansion Period (1991-1999)
With the beginning of the 1990s, the international community underwent major
changes. With the collapse of the communist regime, the collapse of the Cold War
system and the dissolution of international relations based on ideology and military
power, intern ational relations centered on the economy began to take on the core
importance.
From January 1995, the WTO system, which set new standards for international trade
and investment, was launched. In science and technology policy, a situation in which
policy must be planned/implemented according to the rules stipulated by international
conventions has begun to develop. The development of globalization following the
collapse of the Cold War system was similar to Korea. In particular, the Kim Young -sam

===== PDF_PAGE 208 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
196
government presented globalization as a national goal and actively promoted
globalization. However, the opening brought up along with globalization has cost Korea
a great deal. The rapid opening, which was not sufficiently managed, became a major
cause of the foreign exchange crisis, which eventually led to a situation in which the IMF
requested a bailout and implemented a restructuring policy at the request of the IMF.
Until the mid-1980s, the issue of comprehensive coordination was not discussed much
because the Ministry of Science and Technology had been in charge of the functions of
the department in charge of science and technology in the indifference of other
ministries. However, as mentioned above, as the government's R&D budget continued
to increase in the 1990s and various ministries and ministries promoted R&D projects in
various forms, the need for comprehensive coordination of science and technology
policies increased.
To this end, in February 1998, the Ministry of Science and Technology was elevated to
the “Ministry of Science and Technology”, and in January 1999, the National Science and
Technology Committee was established with the president as its chairperson under the
‘Special Act for Science and Technology Innovation’. After the establishment of the
National Science and Technology Committee, through the establishment of the
「Science and Technology Long -Term Vision for 2025 」 in 1999 and the 「Basic Plan
for Science and Technology」 based on the Framework Act on Science and Technology
in December 2001, the 'medium -term vision/goal for the development of science and
technology in Korea' and A 'promotion strategy for achieving the goal' was presented.
The key change in science and technology policy in the 1990s was that the strategy to
create new technologies by breaking away from the imitation strategy that was
dependent on foreign sources of science and technology began to be discussed in
earnest. Aga inst this background, a large -scale national R&D project that intensively
supports national R&D resources was started in order to secure strategic
competitiveness and technological competitiveness to compete with advanced
countries. These projects prioriti zed areas where competitiveness could be secured
through mid- to long-term technology forecasting and demand surveys, while clearing
the strategic nature to achieve specific goals.

===== PDF_PAGE 209 =====
Chapter 6. STI International Cooperation in Korea

197
The 1990s was a time when the Cold War system, which had continued for half a century,
ended, and exchanges with Russia, Eastern European countries and China began and
rapidly expanded. In the field of international cooperation in science and technology,
cooperation with these countries was more focused than other countries. In particular,
at a time when Korea was experiencing difficulties due to the strengthening of
technological barriers due to technology protectionism despite the need to narrow the
technological gap with advanced countries, scientific and technological cooperation
with Russia or China was found to be a new cooperative partner, not a western advanced
country. Therefore, international cooperation in science and technology with northern
countries such as Russia and China was newly added during this period in addition to the
existing international cooperation in science and technology with advanced western
countries.
In Korea, information gathering activities on the latest scientific and technological trends
abroad have been promoted since the 1970s. However, in the 1990s, as our
international activities expanded to various regions such as Eastern Europe, Latin
America, Africa, and ASEAN in addition to the existing developed countries in the West,
infrastructure-building projects were strengthened. In particular, while promoting
international cooperation with Russia, it was necessary to establish a foundation in
advance to facilitate joint research and attraction of manpower. Accordingly, the
cooperation-based project started with the Korea -Russia manpower exchange project
in 1992, and in 1996, it was separated from the international joint research project due
to differe nces in the task selection procedure, and was newly established as a
foundation for international cooperation.
International cooperation in science and technology was promoted not only by
government organizations but also by public institutions. As the scale of IC S&T increased,
the scope of cooperation became more diversified, and professionalism was required,
the government began to entrust work to public institutions . The scope of science and
technology cooperation has been expanded . IC S&T was also promoted with northern
countries such as Russia, China, Eastern Europe, and North Korea, where cooperation
was rest ricted due to ideological barriers in the past . The science and technology
international cooperation program has become more diverse. In addition to the existing

===== PDF_PAGE 210 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
198
joint research -type projects, human resource exchange and foundation -building type
projects were added.

 Advanced Cooperation Period (2000-2012)
In the 21 st century, with the rapid development of information and communication
technology, knowledge has emerged as the biggest factor of production. Meanwhile,
with the development of information technology, economic and scientific and
technological development ac tivities around the world are gradually becoming more
open, globalized and networked. In Korea, the limitations of the catch -up innovation
system, such as a lack of creative manpower, a lack of ability to develop source
technology, sluggish industrialization of R&D results, and a focus on mature technology
that allow latecomers to catch up, were pointed out. Therefore, overall strategy revision
through innovation was inevitable, and the need for new growth engines to be
discovered and a transitio n to an innovation system that went beyond imitation and
pursuit was raised (Ministry of Science and Technology, 2008).
The Roh Moo -hyun administration has presented “building a science and technology -
oriented society” as one of its major national tasks, and has been advocating 'entering
the second science and technology' and 'establishing a Northeast Asian R&D hub' as key
national tasks. To this end, the 「Science and Technology Centered Society Promotion
Planning Group」 was formed in January 2004, and the position of information science
and technology assistant was newly established within the Blue House
In order to prepare for the above environmental changes, Korea has expanded its
investment in R&D as well as in promising new technologies such as biotechnology (BT),
nanotechnology (NT), environmental technology (ET), aerospace technology (ST), which
have high added value and are the basis of next-generation growth industries. This is an
essential strategy for inducing high growth by discovering future growth engines
centered on high value -added next -generation industries in Korea, where natural
resources are limited.

===== PDF_PAGE 211 =====
Chapter 6. STI International Cooperation in Korea

199
In 2002, efforts were made to enhance the ability to plan science and technology
development at the national level, such as creating a national technology road map for
99 key technologies and a vision for science and technology 10 years later. As a result,
national R&D projects have also been focused on leading the industry and discovering
specialized technologies in the public welfare field, which the government should focus
on nurturing. As one of these efforts, the Roh Moo -hyun administration has made
efforts to continuously increase the government R&D budget with the goal of increasing
government R&D investment by 7% of the government budget by 2008.
[Figure 6-3] R&D ratio to GDP

Source: OECD Main Science and Technology Indicators data base

Korea also needed to systematically and efficiently utilize overseas scientific and
technological resources to overcome the domestic poor scientific and technological
research resources, and to participate in solving global problems such as global warming,
environmental pollution, and energy depletion as a member of the international
community. Against this background, in 2001, the 'Science and Technology
Internationalization Promotion Strategy' was announced. According to this science and
technology internationalization promotion strategy, the basic direction to promote the
whole government's international cooperation in science and technology more

systematically was set, and each project was more systematically coordinated and

On the other hand, in the 2000s, there was a shift in the internationalization base
construction project for international science and technology cooperation that Korea
had promoted so far. In the 2000s, a new type of international cooperation project
called the project to attract excellent overseas research institutes was added. In the
2000s, as the size of the Korean economy grew and the role that the international
community demanded of Korea increased, international cooperation wi th regional
multilateral councils in science and technology international cooperation expanded. In
2003, the EU and the EU agreed to sign a science and technology cooperation agreement,
and promoted various multilateral scientific and technological international cooperation,
such as participation in the International Thermonuclear Experimental Reactor (ITER)
project and the 6 th EU Framework Program. In order to promote the exchange and
utilization of excellent overseas scientific and technological manpower, an incentive
system was prepared, such as simplifying the procedure for obtaining nationality for
foreign highly skilled manpower.
With the establishment of a science and technology internationalization promotion
strategy, an annual science and technology internationalization plan was established
and executed, thereby promoting international science and technology cooperation
more sta bly. Partner countries have been expanded to include Central and South
America, Africa, Southeast Asia, as well as Western advanced countries and northern
countries, and international organizations and regional cooperation organizations (APEC,
ASEM, etc.) have been added . It is participating in various multilateral science and
technology cooperation programs such as the EU Framework, EUREKA, CERN ( Conseil
Européen pour la Recherche Nucléaire), and HFSP (Human Frontier Science Program).

 Science Diplomacy Period (2013-2022)
According to the World Economic Outlook data of the International Monetary Fund (IMF)
on the 26 th of December 2021, Korea's nominal gross domestic product (GDP) for this
year was estimated to be 1823.9 trillion dollars (about 216.6 trillion won) in US dollars.

===== PDF_PAGE 213 =====
Chapter 6. STI International Cooperation in Korea

201
This is the 10th largest among 191 countries in the world. Korea's GDP next year is
expected to be 1.97.7 trillion dollars (about 226.6 trillion won), and it is also expected to
rank 10th in the world. If the IMF's forecast is realized, Korea will maintain the world's
10th largest economy for three consecutive years from last year to next year. 26 At the
68th Trade and Development Council held at the Geneva headquarters in Switzerland
on the 2nd of July (2021, local time) of the United Nations Conference on Trade and
Development (UNCTAD), 195 member countries unanimously changed Korea's status
from 'developing country group' to 'developed country group' changed The developed
countries group has increased to 32 countries with Korea included. Since the
establishment of UNCTAD in 1964, Korea is the first country to change its status from a
developing country to a developed country.
Korea has already been classified as an advanced country in the international
community in terms of economy and diplomacy. According to the International
Monetary Fund (IMF) report, Korea ranked 10th in the world with a gross domestic
product (GDP) of $1.5 5 trillion in 2020. According to the World Trade Organization
(WTO), Korea's exports reached $512.5 billion last year, ranking 7th in the world. Last
year, GDP per capita was $31,497, ranking 26th in the world27
As Korea’s contribution to the international community is requested, Global problems
such as climate change, biodiversity and Covid -19 are getting serious, and the role of
science and technology innovation is becoming more important in solving these
problems. Global protectionism is intensifying and trade conflicts and competition for
hegemony between the US and China are intensifying. In this period, pursuing strategies
to create new industries and markets by combining science and technology and ICT, and
to create good jobs by strengthening existing industries with three trends; Leading the
achievement of the national income of 30,000 dollars through the role of science and
technology, Pursuing investments to improve the quality of life such as culture, welfare,
safety, and environment, focusing on R&D investment for the purpose of economic

growth and Paradigm shift to a leading R&D system such as activation of open
technological innovation , c reating an innovative ecosystem where challenges and

It corresponds to the globalization of science and technology and the strengthening of
the strategic nature of the 3rd (2013-2017) and 4th (2018-2022) Science and Technology
Basic Plan . It intends to expand its contribution to the international community by
expanding ODA, establishing the Science and Technology Diplomacy Center, and
promoting the Asian research framework for resolving common issues in Asia . In this
time, Korea secured donor country from recipient country with joining the Development
Assistance Committee (DAC) (2009) and loan donor country (2016) . In addition, Korea
abandoned WTO developing country status (2019) as a result of index showing the

The S&T IC program belongs to category foundation of R&D and occupied very small
volume of budget in the total R&D of ministry of Science and ICT. This means the ratio
of S&T IC program’s volume should be increased and sub-divided into specific areas such
as regional division and cooperation type and so on.
Current Issues can be divided two categories the definition of S&T diplomacy and other
issues related to the competition of emerging technology between US and China.

 Definition of S&T diplomacy
Nowadays there are a lot of mentions about science diplomacy in the S&T IC area ,
foreign affairs, public diplomacy, international organization and so on. But it is
impossible to find the definition of science diplomacy in official document and there are
many similar words such as digital diplomacy, technology diplomacy, cyber security
diplomacy, etc. E ven though we do not have official definition of science diplomacy,

===== PDF_PAGE 221 =====
Chapter 6. STI International Cooperation in Korea

209
the royal society and AAAS divided science diplomacy into 3 dimensions of activities as
shown in [Figure 6-8] below.
[Figure 6-8] Three dimensions of S&T Diplomacy

Source: New Frontiers in Science Diplomacy (2010)

First, the diplomacy for science is official diplomatic means to help achieve science and
technology goals such bilateral and multilateral IC, joint research, human exchanges,
S&T agreements etc. Second, science in diplomacy means S&T knowledge and
competence to provide expert information and advisor y to diplomatic processes and
tools such as participation activities in the international organizations and scientific and
technological organizations (IPCC, IAEA, OECD, UN, etc.). In these three dimension has
limitation to classify the current issue, reshaping of the global supply chain, which seems
to be close to the hard power rather than soft power. Compared to the science for
diplomacy as soft power, the new category is put into the dimensions of S&T diplomacy.
The four dimensions of S&T diplomacy are shown in [Table 6-9] below.

===== PDF_PAGE 222 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
210
[Table 6-9] Four Dimensions of S&T Diplomacy
Classification S&T as hard power S&T as soft power
Command-
based
leadership
Diplomacy in Science: A type of
competition for S&T resources
(economic power: hard power)
- S&T diplomacy to secure a stable
supply chain
Science in Diplomacy: Controlling S&T
standards, institutions, norms, etc. (e.g.
COP26) to make others do things they
don't want them to do
- S&T serve as scientific advice (soft
power)
Persuasion-
based
leadership
Diplomacy for Science: A type of
cooperation through persuasion
and argument based on scientific
and technological achievements
(hard power)
- S&T IC
Science for Diplomacy: A type of using
S&T as a persuasive resource (soft
power) for incentives and rewards
- Active use of various forms of S&T
(ODA, CERN, Track 2, etc.)

The existing three dimensions are corresponded to command-based leadership (science
in diplomacy) and persuasion -based leadership (science for diplomacy) as soft power
and command-based leadership (diplomacy in science) and persuasion-based leadership
(diplomacy for science) as hard power. The division between command and persuasion
is related to the relative strength of cooperation and competition, while the difference
of S&T between hard power and soft power influence is based on the relative closeness
to the economic values and common human values. As the importance of S&T diplomacy
is increasing and the realms of science and technology and diplomacy are converging,
the boundary of dimensions of S&T diplomacy is getting more blurred.

 Conflict between US and China
As mentioned in 2.2, the conflict between US and China made the new definition of S&T
diplomacy emerging and us face the d rawing science and technology diplomacy
strategies. As the era of free trade and cooperation has passed, and the era of

===== PDF_PAGE 223 =====
Chapter 6. STI International Cooperation in Korea

211
protectionism and competition has arrived, China and the United States are trying to
build their own empire by uniting friendly forces.
Countries such as Korea, which pursued cooperation between China and the United
States at the same time, faced a moment of choice, and for a considerable period of
time had concerns about making a strategic choice in a conflict situation. In particular,
in the case of the semiconductor supply chain, economy and security are converging,
and science and technology are directly affecting the era of economic security like
technological sovereignty. Therefore, setting a direction based on international norms
is important, and rather than choosing one of the two, it is the direction that the United
States and China will seek to achieve the necessary cooperation based on principles.

 Implication from Korea’s case
In addition to Korea's economic development (in connection with the economic
development plan), international cooperation in science and technology has also been
developed. First, the external environmental influence such as world economy, end of
cold war, climate change, etc. should be carefully considered. In the case of Korea,
international cooperation is r elatively low importance (investment ratio) compared to
other fields such as emerging technology, basic research, infrastructure and so on.
Implementation of international cooperation is not highly feasible due to separation of
plan and its budget, which is applied to other fields as well. Now, as an official advanced
country, enhancing international leadership by strengthening science and technology
diplomacy is a big task.
Before the planning of master plan, the necessary data should be secured. In the
absence of data, policy can be established on the base of survey or text mining. Based
on the data, current status of S&T IC needs to be evaluated and this is the start of
planning. After that, the goal of S&T IC should be set up regarding the external
environment and internal other plan and schemes. According to the Lee and Lim (2001),
they suggested three types of technological pursuits of latecomers such as path -
following catch-up, path-skipping catch-up and path-creating catch-up. Lao PDR needs
to select the way to obtain the goal.

Prof. Vimontha KHIEOVONG, National University of Lao PDR

1. Introduction

 Background
Lao People’s Democratic Republic is the landlocked country in Southeast Asia. Currently,
Lao PDR has a total population of 7,379,358(2021) [1]. with a GDP per capita of
USD2,568(current US$,2018), Lao PDR is classified among the group of lower middle -
income countries. The Human Development Index (HDI) 2018 ranked Lao PDR as 139
out of 189 countries, which is on the category of “medium human development”, Laos’
human development improves slightly as the ranked country in 137 of 189 counties.
Over the past twenty years, Lao PDR has made great progress in social and economic
development, including halving the absolute poverty rate, reducing hunger, and
improving education and health outcomes [2].
In recent years, science and technology development has advanced rapidly. Therefore,
Lao PDR should seize this opportunity for socio -economic development, especially the
application of science and technology in production, services, and human resource
development to achieve higher quality growth and to be able to compete at the regional
and international levels, in addition to improving public governance and administration
to be more efficient in revenue collection and expenditure.
According to the implementation report of 9 th Socio Economic Development Plan for
(2021-2025) is taking place at a critical moment, both domestically and international,
offering important opportunities for Lao PDR to achieve its development goals. The key
factors that will drive development in the coming five years are 1) young population, 2)

===== PDF_PAGE 228 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
216
rich natural resources, 3) geographical location of Lao PDR and growth of countries in
Asia, including neighboring economies and 4) application of modern science and
technology. There are 6 goals relates to technology and communications sectors 1) The
national economy is growing in a quality, balanced and sustainable manner. 2) Improved
human re sources, with the ability to research and apply science and technology and
wisdom to promote diversified production and services with higher value. 3) The
material a nd mental well -being of the people has been gradually improved. 4)
Development in the direction of green and environmentally friendly. 5) Strong and
modern cooperation, integration , and interconnection within, with the region and
internationally. 6) Effective public administration and society are sacredly protected in
accordance with the direction of public administration by law.
The purpose is to focus on international Science, Technology, and Innovation (STI)
collaboration that relate to of 9th Socio Economic Development Plan for (2021 -2025),
the key factor 4 which is application of modern science and technology as Science,
Technology, and Innovation. In addition, follow the goal5 to strong and modern
cooperation, integration , and interconnection within, with the region and
internationally. Therefore, this is to report the status of international STI collaboration
in Lao PDR.
International STI collaboration can improve STI systems of country and build STI
capabilities by learning from international experiences and benefiting from external
sources of knowledge, expertise, and technology. Improvements can help them learn
better about existing technologies as well as creating new knowledge and innovations

 International environment
Developed and developing countries, in addition to developing science and technology
to be a force for national development, also invest in new innovations, increase the
production of various modern technologies and cooperation aimed at sharing lessons
and technology, and foster the development of human resources in terms of STI.

217
In the ASEAN Plan of Action on Science, Technology and Innovation (APASTI), [3]
implementation plan includes activities that are supportive of both social and economic
developments and consistent with the relevant characteristics of the ASEAN Economic
Community (AEC) 2025 as well as the ASEAN -Socio-Cultural Community (ASCC)
Blueprints 2025 . The strategic thrusts (1) to collaborate between public and private
sector to strength strategic collaboration among academia, research institutions,
networks of center s of excellence, and the private sector to create an effective
ecosystem for capability development. (2) to enhance mobility of scientist and
researchers, connecting people to people and enhancing the participation of women
and youth in STI. (3) to Establish innovative system and smart partnership with dialogue
and other partners to nurture STI enterprises to support MSMEs, nurture knowledge
creation and STI applications to raise competitiveness. (4) Raise public awareness and
strengthen STI enculturation to enhance ASEAN science and technology cooperation.
ASEAN-China STI Cooperation [4], STI cooperation is further enhancement between
ASEAN and China in area of mutual interest and promote innovation driven
development. ASEAN -China STI cooperation , to explores new and sustainable
cooperation mechanism, to e nhance exchanges of STI talents to promote regional S&T

training courses of advanced applicable technology and S&T management, to identify

experience and expertise in research and development, technology transfer and
commercialization. Collaboration among science park to share the best practices and
experiences while promoting and intensifying the usage of existing STI parks to enhance
innovation and networking. collaboration in technology transfer through the sharing of
experiences and best practices, and further enhancing collaboration among research
institutes, higher learning institutions and enterprises among all ASEAN Member States
and China. Utilize the ASEAN-China Cooperation Fund to implement all related activities
under ASEAN-China STI initiatives.
Laos and the Republic of Korea have been developing bilateral relations and cooperation
more and more over the past two decades since they established diplomatic relations
on October25,1995 and in 2019, Laos and the Republic of Korea [5] had signed three

 Domestic environment
History of Science and Technology in Lao PDR [7,8], In August 1988, the Council of
Ministers changed the name of the National Scientific Technique Committee to establish
the Ministry of Science and Technology. So that the new Ministry of Science and
Technology support economic -social development, the gove rnment designated that it
should work in four areas: 1. Scientific and technological services,2. Scientific research
and technological development, 3. Science and technology management, 4.
International co -operation in science and technology. During this period, the role of
science and technology was focused on international co-operation activities with foreign
countries and international organizations. By June 1991, the First Workshop on the
Formulation of Basic Sciences and Technology Policy was organized in Vientiane.
On 23 July 1997, Lao PDR participate the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN).
This was an important step forward to work co -operate on science and technology at
regional level.
In 2003 the First National Science and Technology Policy was released and was
implemented up until the year2010. The policy outlined five main goals related to
science, technology and innovation. The first goal improved R&D to ensure the
mobilization of ST I resources. The second goal is to upgrade education for higher STI
knowledge, the aimed to provide agricultural, industrial and services development work
for the poverty reduction strategic program and to the industrialization and
modernization of the cou ntry. The third goal increased the coordination and
cooperation between various sectors and research institutes to ensure the cooperation

===== PDF_PAGE 231 =====
Chapter 7. STI International Cooperation in Lao PDR
219
of the production sector and the scientific community to obtain the maximum benefit
of the country from STI activities. The fourth goal upgraded and strengthen the public’s
awareness on science and engineering knowledge. The fifth goal promoted the
technological innovation and adaptation system. These policy goals intent to be
accomplished through increased funding and international cooperation.
The 2003 STI policy called for a reorganization of government agencies and international
cooperation to compensate for weak capacity. In 2007, due to the expansion of science,
technology and environmental activities beyond Vientiane to the provinces, the
government transformed the Science, Technology and Environment Organization into
the National Organization of Science, Technology and the Environment. With the
purpose of improving the efficiency implementation of its activities, there are two new
organizations: Resources and Natural Environment (RNE) and The National Authority of
Science and Technology (NAST).
In 2007, the National Authority for Science & Technology (NAST) was under the Prime
Minister’s Office. NAST acted as a secretary to central government and managed all the
science and technology activities in the country, intellectual property rights,
standardization and metrology, even formulating legislation.
On 28 September 2011, a decree of the Prime Minister of Lao PDR No.309/PM the
government of Lao PDR established the Ministry of Science and Technology officially,
which is (re) created the Ministry of Science and Technology (MOST) from the original
operation between 1988 and 1993. MOST is addressing important challenges such as: (i)
Improvement of the organizational structure for the design and implementation of STI
policies; (ii) development of the legal framework related to science, technology and
innovation; (iii) capacity building and human resources development; (iv) infrastructure
development; and (v) normalization and standardization functions, in the priority fields
of Science and Technology are : (a) Biotechnology and Ecology,(b)Renewable Energy and
Materials, (c) Information Communication and Technology(ICT).
Since MOST was establishment, several laws have submitted to National Assemble and
first proposed laws were on intellectual property and electronic transactions. These laws
are placed to encourage socio-economic development and facilitate integration into the

===== PDF_PAGE 232 =====

Community.

wide scope, especially in the priority areas of development.

• Article8: International Cooperation
The government promotes the relationship and cooperation with foreign counties,
regional and internationally on SME promotion activities, especially on the field of
productivity, by exchange knowledge and information; the enhancement of their
human resources’ knowledge capacity, transferring technology acquiring assistance
and others, basing on the independence, equal and benefits of both sides, in
accordance to the international agreements and conventions, that the Lao People’s
Democratic Republic is a signatory members
• Article19: The cooperation between SMEs and large sized enterprises and foreign
investment enterprises
The government promotes the cooperation between the SMEs and the large sized
enterprises and foreign investment enterprise, by establishing an enabling policy and
favorable mechanism and enacting necessary legislation and measures.
The large size enterprises and foreign investment enterprises shall give opportunity to
the SME, on supplying raw materials, semi products, commodities a service as their
sub-contractor network, and to enhance the strengthen for SMEs by transferring
advances technology and skills on management.

The SMEs shall improve ability on management and technology development to ensure
the effective provision of quantity and quality of commodities and services suitable to
the requirements of the large sized enterprise and foreign investment enterprises.

2. Policy Analysis

 Institutions
In the 2014, The institutions related to STI which is under Ministry of Science and

and telecommunications to reach the national- regional an international standard on

• Renewable Energy and New Materials Institute (REMI)
REMI has role in research, development, transfer, promotion, applications and services
related to renewable energy and new materials. REMI conducted several research and
demonstration projects. In addition, REMI organized training courses on renewable
energy technology and applied.

• Biotechnology and Ecology Institute (BEI)
BEI has role in research, development, application and service in biotechnology and
ecology. BEI was set up as an herbarium and a natural history museum and has a
function to disseminate and raise awareness on science and ecological systems. BEI
conducts some research activities to apply biotechnology to identify plant and animal
diseases.

• Office of National Council of Science
Referring to Prime Minister’s decree number 04, dated 28 January 2002, the National
Council of Science (NCS) is responsible for setting up the Cabinet Office of NSC that will
assist NSC and Ministry of Education and Sports in coordinating with research institutes,
universities, ministries and equivalent agencies across the country for research and
development on Science and Technology, and for promoting NCS activities. * In the
past, National Council of Science was under Ministry of Science and Technology*.

• Council for Science and Technology (CST)
The core mandate is to facilitate science and technology research and innovation in the
Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF). CST contributes to the achievement of the
MAF research outcomes.

• National Agriculture and Forestry Research Institute (NAFRI)
Modernized agriculture technologies are developed for improvement of agricultural
production systems which support national food security and nutrition,
commercialized production, and the use of resources is managed at a sustainable level.
The main research themes are the following: (i) promotion of bio-diversity to create a
gene bank, while deepening the understanding of native plants and wild animals; (ii)
improvement of agricultural productivity, including a programme for each rice-plant

===== PDF_PAGE 238 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
226
and livestock species and other programmes which focus on maize, cassava, and coffee;
(iii) response to climate change is given priority, such as research on protecting crops
from floods; and (iv) information provision to farmers (CRDS, 2015)

• Lao Tropical Medicine and Public Health Institute (Lao TPHI)
The role and functions of Lao TPHI are (a) serving as a central focal point for promoting,
supporting, consulting, advising, coordinating and implementing health research; (b)
developing regulation and standard measures to protect benefits for human subjects
participating in research; (c) providing evidence based scientific research to decision
makers for developing, improving health policy, strategies, action plans, and health
work plans of the Ministry of Health(MOH) to enhance the quality of health services for
better health of the population; (d) developing and improving health research data
bases as a center of excellence, providing the evidence base and research outputs that
can be synthesizes so as to offer policy recommendations; (e) providing in -country
short-term training on Public Health management for health professionals at all levels,
according to each five year socio-economic plan of the country; (f) providing regional
long-term training on Public Health and tropical medicine in order to: (i) enhance the
competence of junior medical doctors and public health professionals committed to
improve ASEAN populations’ heal th, (ii) deliver an international master degree,
focusing on practice in priority health issues: Public Health, epidemiology, clinical
medicine, therapeutics, research, and management, (iii) to foster and monitor health
development projects based on students’ initiatives and implementations, (iv) to create
a forum for academic exchange and research, with an international perspective, and to
expand scientific networks in the region; (g) co-ordinate for the Council of Medical
Sciences and serve as secretariat for the National Ethics Committee for Health Research;
and (h) other roles assigned by MOH.

• Institute Pasteur of Laos (IPL)
The IPL has a mandate from Lao Ministry of Health to fulfil activities of public services:
(1) Research and diagnostic on emerging infectious diseases and vector borne diseases;
(2) training, Education and Capacity building and (3) technical assistance to National
Centre for Laboratory and Epidemiology (NCLE) for investigation of epidemics.

• Natural Resources and Environment Research Institute
The NREI play a role as secretariat to Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment

===== PDF_PAGE 239 =====
Chapter 7. STI International Cooperation in Lao PDR
227
in studying, research on natural resources and analysis environment quality and
provide technical services in the area of natural resources and environment. Its
mandate includes implementing the policy, strategy, regulation, action plan on natural
resources and environment; to promote research, development, and the utilization of
prototype on natural resources and environment which best suitable for Lao context;
and other tasks.

• National University of Laos (NUOL)
NUOL provide higher education in areas required for the socio-economic development
of Lao PDR; performing research in natural and social sciences; preserving the arts,
culture and traditions of the nation; and providing academic services to society. There
is the degree of bachelor in all and some master’s degree programs in some of its
academic disciplines are available, and it is preparing to offer Doctoral degree programs.
The university is part of the following regional networks: (a) ASEAN University Network
(AUN); (b) ASEAN University Network/Southeast Asia Engineering Education
Development Network (AUN/SEED-NET); (c) Agence Universitaire de la Francophonie
(AUF) and (d) Greater Mekong Sub-Region Academic and Research Network (GMSARN).

• CHAMPASACK University
The main activities of CHAMPASACK University are (1) Providing Teaching-learning at
the higher education level; (2) developing scientific, interdisciplinary and applied
research; (3) providing capacity building and academic services base on communities-
base in accordance to government policy for better w ell-being of its people .
CHAMPASACK University has focused on agriculture and will be a center of excellence
in Agriculture.

• SAVANNAKHET University
The mission of SAVANNAKHET University are (1) SKU Development is aligned with the
socio-economic development strategy of the three provinces (Savannakhet,
Khammouane and Bolikhamxay); (2) provide equal access to higher education for both
the urban and rural people, in particular, underprivileged people from rural and remote
area; (3) development of SKU must be closely related to national strategy of education
development so as to gradually approach the national and international standard and
(4) Centre for human resources development, in the central region.

• SOUPHANOUVONG University
The University conducts the educational functions to prepare the specialists,
researchers and multidisciplinary scientific scholars; organizing scientific research;
protecting and promoting Lao’s unique national and multiethnic arts and culture; and
to provide academic services to society.

• SOUSAKA College
SOUSAKA College has role to develop human resource in business administration and
information technology fields to support the need of national socio and economic
devilment. SOUSAKA College promotes human resources at post diploma level, offering
bachelor’s degrees in business administration, accounting, IT and computer engineering.

• University of Health Sciences
The University of Health Sciences (UHS) is a unique health institution that produces
health professionals at higher level (bachelor’s degree) in Lao PDR. The University has
three main functioning roles: training, service and research.

 Analysis of current situations
2.2.1 Descriptive Statistics
The primary mechanism for policy implementation was setting the target for the Gross
Domestic Expenditures on R&D (GERD) between 4% and 5%. This was approximately 100
times the expenditures on R&D estimated by the 2002 R&D survey.

2.2.2 Governance
Ministry of Science and Technology (Currently Ministry of Technology and
Communication) was established in 2011, this is new step to develop Science,
Technology and Innovation. Ministry is one of the main actors with Science and
Technology(S&T) governance structure in Lao PDR[10]. The ministry consists of seven
departments (including Science, Tec hnology and Innovation, Intellectual Property,
Standardization, IT, human resources, Inspection and Planning and has three institutes:
the Institute of Ecology and Biological Technology, the Institute of Renewable Energy
and New materials and the Institute of Computer Science and Electronics. It hosts two
offices and 18 science and technology bureaus at the provincial level. The ministry
particularly focuses on three thematic areas: biotechnology and ecology, renewable
energy and materials, and ICT. The mai n mission of the ministry is to apply scientific
knowledge in order to reduce poverty and to overcome the status as “least developed
country” by 2020. In order to implement this mission, five goals have been identified:
Improvement of the organizational st ructure to promote policies, Development of the
legal system related to S&T, establishing of sustainable innovation infrastructure,
Development of human resources related to S&T, Standardization of local functions.
The government of Laos has dissolved the Ministry of Science and Technology, transferring
its departments to various ministries. Departments under the ministry will be transferred,
along with all public servants, to five departments under existing ministries including the
Ministry of Education and Sports, Ministry of Post, Telecom and Communications (recently
Ministry of Technology and Communication), Ministry of Industry and Commerce, Ministry
of Agriculture and Forestry, and the Ministry of Energy and Mines.

===== PDF_PAGE 245 =====
Chapter 7. STI International Cooperation in Lao PDR
233
Ministry of Technology and Communication (MTC) is a number of key functions related
to the research, development, management, promotion and promotion of technology
and communication (Prime Minister's Decree No. 625 / PM, October 22, 2021) are the
following: 1) study on policies, strategies, laws and legislations of the technology and
communications sector to submit to the Government. 2) Create, improve technological
and communication infrastructure across the country to facilitate technology,
innovation, telecommunications, internet, post and cyber security. 3) Develop, manage
and promote the use of public administration systems and services with digital
technology to the same standard as modern urban systems, public sector networks,
public and integrated in formation systems, national database systems, digital identity
systems, administrative systems, administrative systems, administrative systems. 4)
Promote the transformation of the digital economy, industrialization and modernization
of management, product ion, trade and services as the core for all parties in the
development of digital technology. 5) Create, develop and manage cyber security
management across the country to ensure the Internet network. 6) Manage, administer,
promote, develop and provide inf rastructure services and information center systems
of the country to provide e -information services for the government and society.7)
Encourage the intelligence, innovation of individuals and social organizations in research,
development, transfer, use of various techniques and technologies and use the results of
research, innovation and application of technology in various forms, research on business
ventures, production, import-export, investment in the development of the hardware,
software, digital and high-tech industries and advanced and modern technologies.

• Institutes

2.2.4 Resources
In Lao PDR, the government’s STI budget for R&D is allocated by the Ministry of Finance
and approved by the National Assembly each year.

2.2.5 Issues
Science, Technology and Innovation (STI) has become one of the important potentials for
economic growth and social development. It plays an important role in rapidly promoting
the growth economy and encouraging the movement of the economic structure in a more
advanced and modern direction Through changing the production elements and raising the
competitiveness of the products of enterprises and the economic. Laos have international
STI collaboration with various countries and international organizations to encourage STI
through seminars, training, study abroad and research development.

 Challenges and Opportunities
2.3.1 Strengths
• collaboration in research and development, promotion of the use of modern
technology with countries, international organizations, including technical assistance,
funding are carried out according to the foreign policy guidelines of the government
that have been laid out.
• The government play roles importance to science and technology by setting policy
guidelines, formulate national socio-economic development 5-year plan in each phase
to develop all legal acts related to the protection and promotion of research,
development and use of advanced and modern technology.
• Political stability is strong, there is democracy, unity and society are peaceful.
• The national economic is expanding continuously and steadily, including the Gross
domestic product (GDP) increasing each year. There are abundant natural resources.
The terrain is conducive to science and technology, research and development.
moreover, connecting with neighboring countries on the regional and internationally.

===== PDF_PAGE 247 =====
Chapter 7. STI International Cooperation in Lao PDR
235
• Cooperation in research and development, promoting the use of modern technology
with countries and international organizations technical assistance and grants are
broad in nature according to the government's foreign policy.

2.3.2 Weakness
• The main fund to develop the STI in Laos still come from the FDI, Loans, ODA and private
sector is very less. The lack of human resource in STI.
• related legal system on science and technology is not complete system. the
coordination mechanism between central and local is not systematic.
• Personnel with knowledge, ability, experience and conduct research in science and
technology are limited in terms of quantity and quality. human resource development
is not exactly plan and it is not prioritized focus area.
• Science and Technology infrastructure is still limit and unable to use the modern
equipment which the existing equipment is old and out of date.
• government budget and involved sector for science and technology is not enough.
• Increased energy consumption has led to a shortage of energy and research on using
of renewable energy and materials Lack of food supplies and the need for safe food to
consume, Management of natural resources and the environment change, The
weather that may be affected by various natural disasters. therefore, people need to
develop knowledge of science and technology, there are enough human resource with
knowledge and ability in science and technology both in quantity and in quality to
develop country.
• establish the infrastructure of Science and Technology has significant need fund to
ensure the development in each phase.

2.3.3 Opportunities
• Government open cooperation with foreign countries.
• The rate of investment, foreign assistance, international organizations, business and
enterprises sector including personal businesses to the socio-economic development
and science and technology development tends to increase.

===== PDF_PAGE 248 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
236
• Human resource potential, the natural resources and topography of a country are the
facilitating conditions for scientific research and technology development.
• In the era of globalization, regional development of the ASEAN Community, Lao PDR 's
socio-economic development, competitiveness and international economic
cooperation, international economic linkages which require modern science,
technology and innovation.

2.3.4 Threats
• Demographic and social changes are increasing rapidly, Aging society and population
mobility, social and cultural infiltration, Receiving and distributing news from multi
direction, The distribution of prosperity is unstable characterized that affect the socio-
economic development of Lao PDR.
• region development into the ASEAN Community, Membership of the World Trade
Organization, Competition and cooperation for advance economic development which
the government have to well manage mechanisms and strongly promote the
coordination in science and t echnology. There is a complete set of systematic
protection regulations to be comparable and equal to such developments. adoption of
modern technology is not affected to environment.

 Policy Recommendations
• Policy to facilitate research and development collaboration.
• Policy to promote research and innovation.

3. National STI Master Plan

 Direction of the Plan
3.1.1 Vision
“Transforming Lao PDR to a Sustainable Lower Income Economy by 2030 through
Strengthening Science Technology and Innovation Capability”

3.1.2 Strategy
◼ Strategy3: International STI Cooperation
“Take advantages of S&T International cooperation for the strengthening of S&T
capability”

 Objects and Actions
The International Cooperation strategy regarding to the cooperation such as Bilateral,
multilateral, small multinational and International Organization.
The investment and technology attract FDI, development of R&D capability utilize ODA
for research manpower, R&D equipment, policy capacity building.

◼ Objective3-1: Capacity building and human resource development
Action3-1: “Development of human resources in science and technology by enhancing
the development of skills and knowledge for employees of the relevant ministries and
universities, institutions, including those of the industrial zone”
Strategic Plan for development of Science and Technology, human resource
development is necessary to drive Socio Economic development. In Lao PDR, the
number of human resources is growing rapidly. In 2006, the number of staff who
graduated PhD degree is 255, master’s degree 2 ,017, bachelor’s degree is 10,833 and

===== PDF_PAGE 250 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
238
other 70,461. In 2010, the number of PhD graduated increases to 495, master graduated
is 4,212 bachelors graduated is 22,185 and other is 85,639. In 2006 to 2010, higher
education increased at an average rate of 46 percent per year. In 2009 -2012 higher
education enrollment ratio of 1,898 students per 100,000 population. In 2011 to 2012,
the ratio increased by 2,015 per 100,000 population.
Government sent Lao student to study abroad in various filed in Vietnam is 61.2% of all
studying abroad, Thailand is 12.9%, Republic of China is 9,7% and study other country is
15,2%
According to data, the increasing of human resource is the quantity but low quality
because the mechanism of human resource development is not consistent with the field
of study that needs to be developed in each phase. The distribution of knowledge-based
resources does not meet the actual needs, the most of resource work at institutions
rather than working in the business sector and local area. higher education level has only
been initiated the master's degree in some field area, but it has not yet had enough
capabilities and conditions to implement doctoral degree in various fields.
Competent and highly educated employees working in science and technology
development are insufficient to meet the demands.
Human resource development in science and technology is still very small compared to
ASEAN country, in 2010 data show that there are only 1.1 researchers per 10,000
population in science and technology. Therefore, the strategy of science and technology
development focuses on human resource development at various levels to have
quantity and quality, it can be equivalent and suitable to join the ASEAN Science and
Technology Community and connecting internationally.
Ministry of Technology and Communications, Ministry of Education and Sports will be
responsible for monitoring and evaluating.
The funding: Government budget, ODA, FDI

the country's science and technology level closer to international. The import of science

safety, does not affect the environment and to be sure for sustainable development of
the country and equality, both sides have mutual benefits.

Action3-3: Promotion of joint R&D
Countries in the region and in the world are increasingly invested in research and
development opposite to Least developed countries still have limited budget and
capabilities of research and development. The adoption of science and technology is still
very low compared to developed countries and developing countries. Lao PDR invests
small amount in research and development than other ASEAN countries (in 2012 it was
0.04% of the Gross Domestic Product (GDP) or 1% of the government's investment
budget.
In the past year, the National Budget Plan did not invest in research, and development
much. A science and technology development fund has been established. Attracting
international aid and cooperation is still limited and inefficient. Science and technolo gy
research and development projects that the government pay particular attention to do

===== PDF_PAGE 253 =====
Chapter 7. STI International Cooperation in Lao PDR
241
not have a specific budget plan or mechanism for budget management and investment
plans.
The vision to 2030 will strengthen the framework of science, technology and innovation
in Laos in term of advance, modern and equivalent to other countries in the region and
to support potential sector that can adapt, invent new technolog ies to develop the
country with an economy of wisdom, encourage establishing infrastructure such as
equipment’s and laboratory that necessary for scientific research and technological
development. And also cooperated with various research institutes and uni versities,
both d omestically and internationally to strengthen the framework for research and
development of science, technology and innovation.
According to Strategic Plan6, Cooperation of Science and Technology Development with
the global community is very necessary, therefore, it must be carried out in a
competitive, motivated, creative, autonomy, national security and equally efficient
manner.
Plan and implement research projects in development of Science and Technology
between business units, universities, and research institutes in Lao PDR , increase
international scientific seminars, organize an exhibition of research in science and new
technology, attract foreign scientists to pa rticipate in research projects at different
levels for human resource potential and encourage freshly researcher ,create the
National Institute of Science and Technology by cooperating with countries that have
Scientific and technological expertise ,increa se regional and international cooperation
in science and technology, including neighboring countries to attract more investment

Prof. Youjung Shin, Jeonbuk National University

1. Introduction

The brain, i.e. the educated people, is an important resource for developing countries.
Yet, the brain who has earned social and economic capital with higher education tends
to look for better living condition. So, they often move to different places or countries
instead of staying in their own countries. In result, developing countries which urgently
need the brain and their contributions used to struggle to attract and capture the brain
– not to mention to raise them. This is called as “brain drain problem.” According to the
United Nations Global Migration Database, about 34% of global migration occurred from
developing countries to developed countries and this number has been rapidly
increasing.28 [Figure 8-1] shows the brain drain rate depending on the level of economic
development in each country. Not surprisingly, low -income countries showed highest
brain drain rate compared to high -income countries; interestingly, it is not low -income
countries but lower-middle income countries that showed the highest brain drain rate,
which this paper will revisit again later. What’s notable in Figure 1 is that, if we compare
the less educated and college graduates, it was college graduates which showed the
greatest gap in leaving the country depending on its economic size.

28 Frédéric Docquier, “The Brain Drain from Developing Countries,” IZA World of Labor, May 2014,
https://wol.iza.org/articles/brain-drain-from-developing-countries/long.

Chapter 8. STI Brain Drain and
S&T University: A Study
on the Struggle of KAIST

===== PDF_PAGE 260 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
248
[Figure 8-1] The Brain Drain Problem

Source: Frédéric Docquier (2014)

South Korea was one of the countries which suffered from the brain drain problem. After
the Korean War (1950-1953), most of the national infrastructure including educational
system and infrastructure was devastated.29 In the late 1950s, the so-called Minnesota
Project (1955 -1961) was carried out for the reconstruction of the Seoul National
University. In the name of the Minnesota Project, the University of Minnesota in the U.S
supported the Seoul National University to restore its basic material and intellectual
foundation by, for example, giving aids for equipment, instruments, books, and building
reconstruction.30 Yet, it was far from enough to revive the educational system and
culture in South Korea. Particularly, the graduate school system remained as weak; even
within Seoul National University as the Minnesota Project mostly concerned about their
undergraduate system. In this context, those who wanted higher education or those
who wanted to continue their study as researchers used to go abroad and rarely came

29 Geun-Bae Kim, The Structure of Scientific and Technological Revolution in Korea (Paju: Dulnyouk, 2016); KyongHee
Han, The Making of Korean Engineers: Historical Formation and Development (Paju: Dulnyouk, 2021); for more
about the impact of the Korean War on the Korean Economy, see Jong -Won Lee, “The Impact of the Korean War
on the Korean Economy,” International Journal of Korean Studies 5.1 (2001): 97-118.
30 For more about the Minnesota Project, see Ock-Joo Kim and Sang-Ik Hwang, “The Minnesota Project: The Infulence
of American Medicine on the Development of Medical Education and Medical Research in Post-War Korea,” Korean
Journal of Medical History 9.1 (2000): 112-123.

A Study on the Struggle of KAIST
249
back to the country. From 1962 to 1976, the annual average of non -return rate among
those who left the country was about 62.62% in South Korea which was really high;
higher than for example Jordan, Iran, Iraq, India, and Lebanon.31
Not always did the brain leave their country for economic reason. Scholars have pointed
out diverse motivations and reasons – from economic reasons to political reasons,
individual reasons, and social reasons – for brain drain.32 In South Korea, a continuous
political conflict with North Korea as well as the dominance of authoritarian regime
made the brain to concern about the political stability in South Korea. It played a role to
a certain extent in draining the brain out of the country. Also, individual and social
reasons such as health -care issues, family education, and living condition were also
important factors in motivating the brain to leave the country. Several Korean
researchers have periodically conducted a survey on se arching the major reasons of
brain drain.33 Some pointed out the importance of economic reason and others pointed
out the role of political factors. For example, Ha-Joong Song conducted a survey on the
way Korean scientists and engineers in the U.S. decided their career path – either staying
in the U.S. or returning to South Korea – in the 1980s and 1990s. 34 However, what is
important here than the result of survey per se is the fact that the major reasons of brain
drain have been changing even within one country. Then, the question is how to keep
monitoring the changing reasons of brain drain and appropriate ly dealing with this
problem.

31 Manyong Moon, “Korea’s ‘Brain -Drain’ and the Role of the Korean Institute of Science and Technology,” Korean
Culture 37 (2006): 229-261; depending on the survey method, the number of non -return rate of South Korea was
fluctuated but it was high enough to make the government concern.
32 For example, see Andreas Ette and Nils Witte, “Brain Drain or Brain Circulation?: Economic and Non -Economic
Factors Driving the International Migration of German Citizens,” in The Global Lives of German Migrants:
Consequences of International Migration Across the Life Course , eds., Marcel Erlinghagen et al (Springer Cham,
2021).
33 Hyun-Ah Park, Hye-Young Gim, An-Sik Chang, Seoung-Ho Ryu, “Analysis of Factors Contributing to Brain Drain and
Work Motivation Regarding Human Resource Development in Korea: With Focus on 2015 IMD World Talent Report,”
Review of Culture and Economy 19.1 (2015): 123-151.
34 Ha-Joong Song, Who stays? Who returns?: The Choice of Korean scientists and engineers, Ph.D. diss., Harvard
University, 1991; Ha-Joong Song, Coping With Brain Circulation: Current Situation and Policy Direction in Korea (The
Ministry of Science, 2001).

===== PDF_PAGE 262 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
250
It should be noted, however, that the brain drain is not always a problem. The circulation
of the brain can play a role in bringing new ideas and knowledge in developing countries
and stimulating higher education. Recently, scholars have pointed out the po sitive
aspects of brain drain and emphasized a need to change existing perspective which
framed the brain drain as a problem.35 However, from the developing country’s point of
view, the problem is that there is a unidirectional lopsided circulation of the brain,
instead of multilateral healthy circulation. In order to attain positive impact of the brain
drain, he or she, who left the country, should come back at some points. Their leaving
should be temporary than permanent. In order to make the brain’s leaving as temporary,
however, there must be a certain kind of incentives and systems that the brain would
continue their research, which is not always an easy task for developing countries. Thus,
in spite of recent scholarly discussions, develo ping countries have been still
problematized the phenomenon of the brain drain, struggling to find a way to relieve
the lop-sided drain of the brain. The term, brain drain, has been actively used to denote
the concern of developing countries in monitoring and meditating the flow of educated
people.36
While many countries used the term brain drain, few however knew where the term,
brain drain, originally came from. It was the U.K. which first used the term brain drain
and framed it as a serious problem. 37 In the 1950s, after the second World War, a
considerable number of scientists in the U.K. found a difficulty in pursuing their research
in Europe and fled to the U.S. It raised a huge concern in U.K., even blaming “Americans’
‘parasitising’ of British bra ins.”38 In 1963, the Royal Society released the report on the

35 Ha-Joong Song, Gi -Geun Yang, Chang -Min Kang, “A Study on the Brain Drain Circulation Model for Advanced
Science and Technology Human Resources,” Korean Policy Studies Review 13.2 (2004): 143-174; Tito Boeri, Herbert
Brücker, Frédéric Docquier, and Hillel Rapoport eds., Brain Drain and Brain Gain: The Global Competition to Attract
High-Skilled Migrants (Oxford University Press, 2012).
36 See for example, Adam Tyson, The Political Economy of Brain Drain and Talent Capture: Evidence from Malaysia
and Singapore (Routledge, 2018); Junaimah Binti Jauhar, Ahmad Bashawir Abdul Ghani, Rabiul Isam, Brain Drain:
Propensity for Malaysian Professionals to Leave for Singapore (Springer, 2016).
37 Matthew Godwin, Jane Gregory and Brian Balmer, “The Anatomy of the Brain Drain Debate, 1950-1970s: Witness
Seminar,” Contemporary British History 23.1 (2009): 35-60.
38 Brain Balmer, Matthew Godwin, and Jane Gregory, “The Royal Society and the ‘Brain Drain’: Natural Scientists
Meet Social Science,” Notes and Records of the Royal Society 63 (2009): 339-353.

===== PDF_PAGE 263 =====
Chapter 8. STI Brain Drain and S&T University:
A Study on the Struggle of KAIST
251
status of emigration of the U.K. scientists and its impact. The report alarmed the
importance of tracing the move and flow of the brain and the serious situation of the
U.K. from the 1950s. With this report, the term, brain drain, was coined and began to be
widely used. It also began to spread to other countries including developing countries
as an analytical term to understand the status and situation of the educated people in
each country. In other words, the term brain drain itself and its following discussion had
been initially shaped in U.K., a high -income country with rich tradition in research and
education in science, and then spread to other countries.
Though using the same term, the brain drain in the U.K. was not the same with the brain
drain in developing countries like South Korea. If comparing U.K. and South Korea, U.K
is the country where the scientific revolution was configured and took place. They had
a long tradition and experience of nurturing science and scientists, and thus had a rich
amount and quality of educated people in science. In contrast, in the case of South Korea,
especially in the Japanese colonial period, few could experience highe r education in
science and it was difficult for Koreans to cultivate their own culture for nurturing
scientists. In other words, South Korea and U.K had a totally different situation in terms
of experience and existing culture in nurturing scientists. Also , while in the 1960s, the
number of educated people in science, who could be drained to other countries, was
quite high in U.K., the number of the brain itself, who had already attained higher degree
in science, in South Korea was very small. There were lo ts of potential brains in South
Korea, not the ready-made brain. So, the kind of the brain that was drained in the name
of brain drain was quite different in the U.K. and South Korea. Furthermore, while there
were some well-established scientific institutes like Royal Society that governed science
and technology policy, there was few organization which involved eminent scientists in
shaping science and technology policy in South Korea.
So, before talking about the brain drain, it should be underlined that various countries
had different experience and system in raising and handling the brain itself. They had
various pre-existing conditions in terms of experience, system, and culture in making the
brain flow. This is important especially in developing countries as, because of the lack of
relevant experience and culture, developing countries tended to find difficulty in
handling the brain itself. Before attempting to attract the brain, the crucial task for

===== PDF_PAGE 264 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
252
developing countries is to know what kind of the brain they were talking about, who the
brain is, what the brain wants, and how to raise and nurture the brain. Yet, developing
countries lacked experience in navigating the answers to those questions; there is no
well-established answer and the important thing is the way of navigating these
questions. Thus, in the process of handling brain drain problem, it became developing
countries’ task to simultaneously cultivate a culture and system to navigate the natu re
of the brain. South Korea was this case. While dealing with the brain drain problem,
South Korea simultaneously navigated the kind of the brain the country wanted, the kind
of the culture the brain wanted, and the kind of system the country had to estab lish in
order to nurture the brain. This is the reason why an in-depth study about brain drain in
developing countries interesting and the reason why we need more empirical studies on
the flow of the brain from developing countries’ view instead of bringin g term and
framework, originated from high-income countries.
This paper examines the way South Korea has addressed the brain drain problem by
focusing on the development of Korea Advanced Institute of Science (KAIS). KAIS was
established in 1971 as a special educational institute for nurturing scientists and
engineers in South Korea, and it was renamed into Korea Advanced Institute of Science
and Technology (KAIST) in 1980. 39 Nowadays, KAIST is one of the top universities in
science and technology in South Korea, producing more than ten thousand Ph.D.
graduates since its establishment. As a science and technology oriented educational
institute, KAIST became a role model for ot her universities like Gwangju Institute of
Science and Technology (GIST), Ulsan National Institute of Science and Technology
(UNIST), and Daegu Gyeonbuk Institute of Science and Technology (DGIST) in South
Korea. It also became the subject of benchmarking for other developing countries. For
example, in 2020, the Kenya government signed a contract with KAIST to build the Kenya
Advanced Institute of Science and Technology (Kenya -KAIST). With the goal of reviving
Kenya economy with talented scientists and engi neers educated through Kenya-KAIST,
like KAIST in South Korea, the Kenya government decided to establish it in the Konza

39 KAIS was merged with the Korean Institute of Science and Technology (KIST) in the early 1980s and its name was
changed into KAIST. Though KIST was separated again in the late 1980s, the institute’s new name, KAIST, remained.
This paper will use the term KAIS when indicating it in the 1970s and KAIST thereafter.

Technopolis near to Nairobi.40 Likewise, KAIST has received attention as the cornerstone
of South Korea’s educational policy which has contributed to resolving its brain drain
problem and promoting the rapid growth of Korean economy.
It should be noted however that, though the establishment of KAIS was important in
relieving the brain drain problem in the 1970s, it did not totally eradicate concerns and
controversies over brain drain in South Korea. In the 1980s, after the launch of KA IS, a
domestic newspaper still alarmed that “there [was] a lot of outflow of nationally trained
brains.”41 Even in the 1990s, the concern on brain drain was resonated again as shown
in [Figure 8-2].42 The brain drain problem did not vanish with the establishment of KAIS.
[Figure 8-2] A changing yet continuing concern over brain drain in South Korea

Source: NAVER News Library

Indeed, the brain drain problem was not something that could be “solved” in developing
countries. Instead, it was what to be “cared.” Focusing on the development of KAIST,
this paper illuminates the meaning of caring the flow of the brain in developing countries.
By showing the struggle of KAIST in dealing with changing yet continuing concern over

brain drain in South Korea, the paper highlights the nature of the issue of brain drain in
developing countries. This paper consists of three parts. By periodizing the development
of KAIST into three – (1) emergence stage in the 1970s, (2) evolution stage in 1980s and
1990s, and (3) experimentation stage after 2000s – as shown in [Figure 8-3], the paper
analyzes the way KAIST shaped its educational curriculum and strategies in line with
continuing yet changing discourse over the problem of brain drain. It r eveals different
ways of addressing the brain drain problem in the changing economic, social, and
political context of South Korea and their implications in understanding the nature of
the brain drain problem in developing countries.
