2. The Emergence Stage

Long before the establishment of KAIS(T), the brain drain has been a serious problem in
South Korea. From 1953 to 1969, there were about 11,037 people who went abroad to
study. Among them, only 1,160 people which was about 10% came back to the country
after getting a Ph.D. 43 So, the Korean government tried to, on the one hand, set up a
high barrier for those who want to go abroad by for example making a qualification exam
mandatory. The Ministry of Education in South Korea carried out the qualification exam
and only those who passed this exam could get an opportunity to go abroad to study. In
1958, the Korean government also established the inspection rule for students studying
abroad.44 While strengthening this regulation, the Korean government also, on the
other hand, tried to attract the talented brain who stayed in other countries. One of the
biggest problems in South Korea in attracting the brain was its poor research
environment. After the devastation of the Korean War (1950 -1953), it was dif ficult for
Koreans to find a proper research laboratory with well -equipped research instrument.
In the case of Seoul National University, the Minnesota Project (1955 -1961) played a
role in setting up basic research instruments and equipment, but it was far from enough
in improving the overall research environment in South Korea.
So, in the 1950s and the 1960s, the Korean government tried to prevent the outflow of
the brain by strengthening the regulation, while stimulating the inflow of the brain by
improving research environment. In this context, in 1966, a new research institute , the
Korea Advanced Institute of Science and Technology (KIST) was established. It aimed to
bring a few eminent Korean brain in abroad by providing well -equipped laboratory and
funding support. It aimed to play a role as the “reverse -brain drain center.”45 In result,

43 Manyong Moon, “Korea’s ‘Brain -Drain’ and the Role of the Korean Institute of Science and Technology,” Korean
Culture 37 (2006): 229-261.
44 “Regulations on Studying Abroad,” National Archives of Korea, accessed September 9, 2022,
https://www.archives.go.kr/next/search/listSubjectDescription.do?id=008652&pageFlag=&sit ePage =1 -2-1;
Hwang-Hee Cho, Eun-Kyoung Lee, Chun-Keun Lee, Sun-Woo Kim, Science and Technology Human Resource Policy
in South Korea (Seoul: Science and Technology Policy Institute, 2002).
45 For more about the KIST, see Manyong Moon, The Formation of Modern Research System in South Korea: The
Establishment and the Development of KIST, 1966-1980 (Seonin, 2010).

===== PDF_PAGE 268 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
256
about 25 Korean scientists and engineers who had been mostly in the U.S. came back to
the country. However, KIST was a research institute. It meant that, though KIST could
recruit several prominent scientists and engineers, it was difficult for KIST to
continuously supply and raise research assistants, i.e. graduate students or postdoc, for
KIST researchers. It should be noted that a researcher did not do research alone. A
research needs not only well -equipped laboratory and instruments but also a group of
great researchers and assistants. In the case of developed countries like U.K., there was
a great deal of well-established educational institutes which could supply well-educated
students and assistants for research institutes. Yet, there was no educationa l back-up
system in South Korea in the 1960s.
Thus, in 1968, KIST conducted “a study on the promotion of S&T universities and
graduate schools.”46 Based on this, in 1969, KIST proposed “a plan to establish a KIST -
affiliated science and technology graduate school.” It was a plan to build a new graduate
school within KIST. By doing so, KIST attempted to nurture human resource by itself.
Regarding KIST’s proposal, the domestic media noted that:
“the reason why [KIST] could not but having an interest in graduate school is
because that what can determine the success and failure of our research
institute depends on the securing of the talented people. Currently, domestic
graduate schools are terribl y under -developed, and as a result, excellent
students turn their eye to foreign countries. In fact, the issue of nurturing
graduate school has been a topic that has been debated in academia for long
time.”47
By building its own graduate school, KIST tried to resolve this situation. Ultimately, KIST
aimed to perform both as a research institute and educational institute in South Korea.
It was the result of realizing the importance of supporting human resource t o the brain

46 The Editorial Board, “Strengthening Graduate Schools: To Prevent the Brain Drain,” Kyunghyang Shinmun , 13
December 1969, 5.
47 Ibid.

===== PDF_PAGE 269 =====
Chapter 8. STI Brain Drain and S&T University:
A Study on the Struggle of KAIST
257
coming back to the country in addition to material resources like instruments and
equipment.
However, KIST’s plan faced harsh opposition from other universities and the Ministry of
Education. From other universities’ point of view, KIST’s proposal to launch a new
graduate school could distract the government’s attention in improving existing
universities’ condition. The government’s funding was always limited. In this situation,
if KIST established a new graduate school, there was a plausible possibility that the
material and social support of the government to traditional universities would go to
KIST to some extent. In addition, KIST was not under the Ministry of Education. As a
research institute, KIST was operated under the jurisdiction of the Ministry of Science
and Technology. In that sense, building a new graduate school in KIST meant, from t he
perspective of the Ministry of Education, that the Ministry of Science and Technology
would expand its role toward the educational system. The Ministry of Education could
not easily accept this idea. So, the Ministry of Education and traditional univers ities
urged the government to invest more money in improving the existing educational
environment instead of attempting to build a new graduate school in KIST. In the end,
KIST’s proposal was dismissed.
Yet, almost exactly at this moment, another new impetus to build a science & technology
graduate school in South Korea emerged not within South Korea but in the U.S. by a
Korean scientists, Kun-Mo Chung. Chung was a physicist, who earned his Ph.D. degree
in Michigan State University in 1963. Right after his graduation, Chung became the
assistant professor in the department of physics at the University of South Florida. Yet,
in 1966, Chung dropped his professorship to pursue more research in nuclear
engineering at MIT as a research professor. In 1967, he became the associate professor
in the department of electrophysics at Polytechnic Institute of New York University. In
short, Chung was one of the Korean scientists who went abroad for study and achieved
a stable academic career in science in the U.S.48 As the brain drained out of South Korea,
Chung himself actually had a huge interest in understanding the phenomenon of brain

48 For more about Kun-Mo Chung, see Youjung Shin, “1970, To live as a scientist or as a science policy maker,” Horizon
(forthcoming); for Chung’s autobiography, see Kun -Mo Chung, A Scientist in the County which Made Miracle
(Korea.com, 2020).

===== PDF_PAGE 270 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
258
drain – its causes and effects – and the way to relieve the brain drain phenomenon for
South Korea. So, during the period when he conducted research in nuclear engineering
at MIT, he also made time for studying science and technology policy at Harvard
Kennedy School. In the late 1960s, at Harvard Kennedy School, the two eminent scholars,
Don K. Price and Harvey Brooks, run the seminar series on science, technology, and
public policy, and Chung participated in it.
As a term paper for the seminar course at Harvard Kennedy School, Chung wrote a paper
on “a policy measure to prevent brain drain in a developing country.” It explored the
reasons of brain drain in developing countries, which Chung would reflect his own
experience, and a policy measure to relieve the brain drain phenomenon. Based on what
he had experienced as a scientist in the U.S. and what he had studied as a student at
Harvard Kennedy School, he speculated what would be needed for developing countries
to reverse the flow of the brain and attract them. One of his ideas was to build a new
graduate school specialized in science and technology, equipped with unprecedented
material as well as social support in South Korea. However, he did not expect his paper
to be actually realized. It was more like an idealistic vision rather than practical guideline.
He had no concrete idea on the way of drawing funding or support for a new graduate
school that he proposed. So, Chung did brainstorm the idea of preventing the brain drain
but he left it behind, and moved to Polytechnic Institute of New York University to
continue his career as a scientist.
However, in 1969, the time came to Chung. When he was in Polytechnic Institute of New
York University, he happened to see the news that John A. Hannah, who was the
president of Michigan State University, became the head of the United States Agency
for International Development (USAID). 49 Chung had a close relationship with John A.
Hannah when Chung was a graduate student in Michigan State University; Hannah even
participated in Chung’s wedding. Chung saw Hannah’s interview addressing that USAID
would change its way of aiding developing countries. While before the 1960s, the USAID
and foreign aid agencies tended to aid developing countries by directly delivering goods

49 For more about the beginning of KAIS, see Dong-Won Kim and Stuart W. Leslie, “Winning Markets or Winning Nobel
Prizes?: KAIST and the Challenges of Late Industrialization,” Osiris 13 (1998): 154-185.

===== PDF_PAGE 271 =====
Chapter 8. STI Brain Drain and S&T University:
A Study on the Struggle of KAIST
259
or investing money, some concerns were raised in terms of its effectiveness in the late
1960s. Also, especially, in the context of continuing war in Vietnam, the U.S. witnessed
the increased amount of money investing for winning the war and unsettled domestic
economy. In this context, John A. Hannah as the new head of the USAID in the late 1960s
said that “instead of giving fish, [USAID] will teach them how to fish.” It was an attempt
to change the way USAID provided foreign aid in developing countries othe r than
directly supplying products. Chung thought that his paper which stressed the need of
establishing graduate school would be in line with USAID’s new policy direction, and met
Hannah in 1969.50
Chung’s personal connection with Hannah and the changing context over the U.S.
foreign aid policy came to merge in the late 1960s. After meeting Chung, Hannah
proposed him to draft policy proposal based on the paper that he wrote in Harvard
Kennedy School. So, on October 1969, Chung wrote “ The Establishment of a New
Graduate School of Applied Science and Technology in Korea” and submitted to USAID.
Then, the work progressed quickly. USAID delivered this policy proposal to the Korean
government, indicating that once the Korean government decided to proceed this policy,
USAID would offer financial support. To explain the policy proposal, Chung was invited
to the Korean government in 1970. Chung stressed that: “the brain goes where the brain
is and the brain goes where it is challenged. The problem of the brain drain from
developing countries to advanced countries can be solved by creating the center of
knowledge in developing countries ” (emphasis is added). 51 A new graduate school
specialized in science and technology would be (and should be) built as “the center of
knowledge” in South Korea.
However, again in South Korea, there was an opposition as in the case of KIST’s proposal.
Since what Chung proposed was to build a new graduate school, it was quite similar with
KIST’s proposal though it would be not KIST -affiliated. Similarly, traditional universities
and the Ministry of Education opposed to this idea as it could distract existing
government’s support on higher education. The Ministry of Education emphasized that,

50 Ibid.
51 Kun-Mo Chung, “The Establishment of a New Graduate School of Applied Science and Technology in Korea” (KAIST,
1969).

===== PDF_PAGE 272 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
260
instead of creating a new graduate school, it would be more effective and efficient if the
government increased funding and support on existing universities. Yet, this time, the
Ministry of Education could not win over. As USAID indicated that it would pro vide
financial aid, around 6 million dollars, once the Korean government pushed forward this
agenda, it was difficult to simply deny it. It was a different situation compared to when
the KIST proposed a new graduate school. Also, for the advocates of a new graduate
school, the opposition from the Ministry of Education was predictable and its logic had
been debated before with the KIST’s proposal. So, in the end, despite conflicts, the then
president Chung-Hee Park decided to establish a new graduate school in science and
technology under the Ministry of Science and Technology: Korea Advanced Institute of
Science (KAIS).
In short, KAIS was the result of struggling to build a new graduate school in science and
technology in South Korea. It was, in a nutshell, ‘a new wine in a new bottle’ strategy.
Instead of investing more money for existing universities, the Korean governm ent
decided to launch a new graduate school in science and technology. However, it was not
achieved at the first attempt. The KIST’s proposal to build a new graduate school could
not be realized due to harsh opposition from existing universities and then the Ministry
of Education. However, a new initiative to build a specialized graduate school in science
and technology in South Korea came from the changing context over foreign aid policy
in the U.S. in the 1960s. Chung’s idea and his personal connection wi th Hannah along
with the contingent U.S. political and social environment resulted in the policy proposal
to build KAIS. It should be noted that a policy needs appropriate timing. USAID’s
suggestion was important because of not just its financial support b ut also its symbolic
meaning. In repelling oppositions from traditional universities and the Ministry of
Education in building a new graduate school, which was predictable as in the case of
KIST, USAID played a symbolic role.52 In the end, KAIS was built in 1971 as the “center of
knowledge” in South Korea.

52 For more about the role of USAID in building KAIS, Buhm Soon Park, “The Role of USAID in the Establishment of
KAIST,” presentation slide, February 2021, STEPI (unpublished).

===== PDF_PAGE 273 =====

A Study on the Struggle of KAIST
261
By building KAIS under the Ministry of Science and Technology, KAIS could bypass
traditional regulations that the Ministry of Education imposed on traditional universities
in terms of professors’ salary, promotion system, budget, and etc. 53 This was the
advantage of ‘a new wine in a new bottle’ strategy. In result, KAIS provided
unprecedented economic incentive to draw the brain. KAIS’s professor received almost
four times higher salary than the average Korean in the 1970s. It was also even two times
higher than, for example, the Seoul National University professors’ salary. KAIS also
provided housing subsidies; like apartment which was not common in South Korea.54 No
wonder traditional universities’ professors were annoyed yet somewhat envious of
KAIS’s professor. However, it was difficult for professors in traditional universities to
move to KAIS, since KAIS mostly recruited the brain in overseas, especially in th e U.S.
KAIS’s utmost goal was to solve the brain drain problem. So, KAIS did not consider
domestic professors as the candidate of KAIS’s professors. Instead, it searched for the
brain who were outside of the country.
Again, it was ‘a new wine in a new bottle’ strategy: a new graduate school under a non-
traditional ministry’s jurisdiction with a new people coming from abroad. Again, no
wonder a tension arose between traditional universities and KAIS. Chung played a role
in searching the candidate for KAIS in the U.S. as he himself was a professor at
Polytechnic Institute of New York University. Though KAIS offered unprecedented
economic incentive, it should be noted that it did not naturally draw the brain. A lot of
persuasion processes were needed. Chung and other KAIS builders selectively and
carefully approached to a few candidates and interviewed them one by one. Thereby,
they explained KAIS’s advantages, like what KAIS would provide to them, as well as
KAIS’s characteristic. KAIS put much emphasis on meeting “the needs of Korean industry
and Korean industrial establishment” instead of “add[ing] the world’s store of basic
knowledge.” 55 For researchers in the U.S., which enjoyed research freedom and
academic curiosity, KAIS seemed like imposing intellectual constraints while providing

53 This was the reason why KAIS, though it was an educational institute, did not use the term, university.

economic incentive. To attract them, thus, a series of explanation and persuasion
process were needed. It should be remembered that economic incentive did not just
naturally attract the brain but required a central figure who would contact the brain and
persuaded them with various options and incentives. In the end, a few who were
persuaded decided to come to South Korea as the professor of KAIS. The below is the
recall of two early professors who came back from the U.S. in the 1970s.
“KAIST has been a groundbreaking university in many ways since its
establishment. There has never been a university that gives me money and a
house, and let me just study as much as I wan t. The treatment of the
professors was also exceptional” (Prof. Ho-Gi Kim, emphasis is added)”
“When I got my Ph.D., I was planning to settle down in the US… But the more
I heard Dr. Chung's suggestion, the more attractive it became…I thought that,

3. The Evolution Stage56

On March 1973, the first freshman which was around 100 students came to KAIS. There
were six departments: mechanical engineering, material engineering, electrical
engineering, industrial engineering, chemistry and chemical engineering, mathematics
and physics; in 1974, bi ological engineering department was added. Twenty -nine
tenure-track professors worked at KAIS in 1973; 44 professors in 1975. In line with KAIS’s
emphasis on meeting the needs of Korean industry – instead of adding “the world’s store
of bas ic knowledge” – new courses and curriculums were designed in a way to
emphasize the learning of practical skill and problem -solving ability. For example, in
mechanical engineering department, it stressed the need to “nurture eminent engineers
who have a to p problem -solving skill” and encourage students’ “curiosity which can
apply principle to real practice.”57 The entrance competition rate of KAIS was quite high
– 5:1 in 1973 – due to KAIS’s exceptional support on students for example tuition
exemption, dormitory, military exemption, and etc. On September 1973, the first class
was opened at KAIS in a newly built building like [Figure 8-4] shows the KAIS’s first
building constructed in a very modern way in the 1970s.
[Figure 8-4] The KAIS’s First Building

56 This section was written based on the author’s published article; Youjung Shin, “Making the Brain for Daeduk
Science Town: The Transformation of KAIST into an University in Daeduk,” Journal of Science and Technology Studies
21, no. 3 (2021): 4-37.
57 The Introduction of Department: Mechanical Engineering, KAIS, 1973, KAIST Archive.

===== PDF_PAGE 276 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
264
Source: KAIST Archive
However, two problems awaited KAIS. First, it should be remembered that KAIS’s ‘new
wine in a new bottle’ strategy costed a lot. By detouring existing regulations of the
Ministry of Education, KAIS offered unprecedented support to its professors and
students; two -times higher salary to KAIS’s professor compared to Seoul National
University professors. Yet, the number of the KAIS’s professor would increase and so
would the number of students. Then, the problem was how KAIS would be able to
sustain the funding for exceptional support to its professors and students. In that sense,
using economic incentive for attracting the brain could be effective, but it had a
sustainability issue. Without sustainability, economic incentive can be just a temporal
measure. One of the reasons that KAIS could provide unusual support to its professors
and students was because of the USAID’s financial aid, i.e. 6 million dollars, though there
was the matching fund from the Korean government. Yet, the USAID planned to support
KAIS f or its initial five years. After that, it was solely the KAIS and the Korean
government’s responsibility to run its new institute, which was not always easy.
Economic incentive for attracting the brain in a developing country had a sustainability
issue not just because of the increased number of professor and students at KAIS, but
also because of its effect on stimulating the needs of better support from tradit ional
universities. Professors at traditional universities, who had witnessed the increased
salary and support to KAIS professors, came to argue for the similar support, in terms of
salary, sabbatical leave, and etc, for the Ministry of Education. In the e nd, it resulted in
like a gradual increase of salary for professors at traditional universities. In that sense,
one can evaluate that KAIS played a role as a stimulant in improving the overall
environment of the Korean higher education system. Yet, one can also say that KAI S
raised a contentious tension between universities making dispute, discrepancies, and
barriers. It should be remembered the advantages and the pitfalls of ‘a new wine in a
new bottle’ strategy. Using economic incentives in ‘a new wine in a new bottle’ strategy
can be effective, as it did not bother existing old bottles, yet it could raise social tension
resulting in the sustainability issue.
The second problem KAIS faced after its establishment was a generation problem. So,
KAIS was launched by recruiting a series of eminent professor who had stayed outside

===== PDF_PAGE 277 =====

A Study on the Struggle of KAIST
265
the country. By doing so, it contributed to relieving the brain drain problem in South
Korea to a certain extent. However, if we think about the next generation that these
brains would raise at KAIS, the brain drain problem was not the dead issue. On April
1983, a domestic newspaper alarmed that “there is a lot of outflow of nationally trained
brains.”58 Here, the “nationally trained brains” referred to the graduates of KAIS as they
received government support with tuition, dormitory, and scholarship. The media
reported that “20% of KAIS graduates which was about 330 people went abroad for
immigration or study, and especially, by 1979, almost half of the graduates – 285
students among 743 – had not yet returned to the country.”59 It alarmed the continuing
phenomenon of the brain drain in South Korea. Though KAIS had reversed the flow of
the brain to certain extent by recruiting a few eminent professors, the new brain that
was raised out of KAIS began to flow toward outside of Sout h Korea. This was the
generation problem in brain drain phenomenon.
[Figure 8-5] The Generation Problem in Brain Drain

Source: Author

However, the outflow of the students at KAIS was not accidental but more of the result
of KAIS’s plan and strategy. It was KAIS’s decision to encourage the outflow of the

58 The Editorial Board, “There is a lot of outflow of nationally trained brains,” Maeil Economy, 6 April 1983, 8.
59 Ibid.

266
students. It is important to understand that the brain drain in the 1980s in South Korea
was more like intentional than natural with the hope of controlling its flow. In the
process of answering questions over, for example, what kind of the brain KAIS shou ld
raise and what kind of education KAIS would offer, the outflow of KAIS’s students was
shaped.
In the context of changing political regime – the long-ruling dictator Chung-Hee Park was
assassinated in 1979 and Doo-Hwan Chun seized the power through an internal coup in
1980 – KAIS began to evolve. In the process of drafting and implementing the KAIS’s 2nd
5-year plan in the late 1970s, two visions were collided in terms of setting its educational
direction. One was to focus on raising the so -called “technologist” who would be
basically technicians yet with the ability of higher -level thinking. It emph asized the
importance of tinkering skills with problem -solving and manipulative abilities. As KAIS
focused on meeting the industrial need for Korean economy, its utmost concern was to
educate students accordingly, expecting them to play an important role i n industry. To
this end, in 1977, KAIS announced its plan to launch a new “specialized technical
program,” which was consisted of “specialized master program” and “service
technology program.” This program aimed to offer advanced skills and knowledge to
working engineers. Its curriculum focused on the “project-based” class which would be
“directly related to the fields.” 60 The launch of this program showed the KAIS’s efforts
in educating practice-oriented technologists.61
While strengthening its curriculum emphasizing tinkering skills, a bit conflicting vision
arouse at KAIS in terms of its educational direction in the early 1980s. In 1983, a group
of KAIS professors wrote the report on “the project plan for launching scienc e gifted

education course.”62 It was the plan for promoting early and accelerated education for
KAIS’s students in the name of gifted education. The report addressed that an early and
accelerated education would be important in maximizing the thinking ability of the brain.
By analyzin g the Nobel laureates in science, the report concluded that many Nobel
laureates received their Ph.D. in 20s – for example, E. Fermi, W. Pauli, M. Planck, who
got their Ph.D. at 21, I. M. Frank, W. Heisenberg, J. J. Thomson at 22, F. Bloch, W. Bothe,
R. Hofstadter at 23, A. H. Compton, R. P. Feynman, D. Glaser, E. O. Lawrence at 24 – and
their most brilliant research was carried out in their twenties. With this particular
understanding of the history of science, the report concluded that most of “the creative
works…were accomplished in their 20 years, between 22 and 44…particularly a lot of
works around the age of 29.” 63 Thus, in order to maximize the thinking ability of the
brain and its creativity, according to the report, it was important to educate and produce
Ph.D. in their 20s.
However, with the Korean traditional education system, it was nearly impossible for one
to complete a Ph.D. degree within their 20s. [Figure 8-6] (the right column) shows the
traditional higher education system in South Korea. After graduating from middle school,
one usually attended high school (3 years), then college (4 years), then a master’s
program (2 years), and a Ph.D. program (3 years). This amounted to at least 12 years. In
case one attended to KAIS after college, this process can be reduced a bit, but it was still
difficult to get a Ph.D. within their 20s. It was a serious problem according to the report.
What the report suggested was instead to enable accelerated education by launching a
new undergraduate program at KAIS which could be completed w ithin three years.
Based upon the assumption that those who graduate from the undergraduate at KAIS
“would continue their study as a Ph.D. at KAIS,” the report hoped to let students finish

By doing so, it encouraged students to receive their Ph.D. within their 20s so that they
could maximize their thinking ability. It was a plan emphasizing KAIS students’ ability to
think with creativity so that they could achieve remarkable discoveries, like Nobel
laureates.
However, if one followed this accelerated education, there was a possibility that they
did not have enough time to study and acquire research capability. Regarding this

problem, what the KAIS report thought was to encourage post-doctoral training abroad.
After an accelerated education at KAIS in South Korea, one was expected to receive
further training as a post -doc overseas. Actually, in 1981, the Korea Science and
Engineering Foundation established a new Post-Doctoral Overseas Training Fellowship.
It supported Korean Ph.D. to go abroad to study science and technology. 65 Here, it was
notable that, at that time, the head of the Korea Science and Engineering Foundation
was Chung-Oh Lee, the KAIS’s professor in the mechanical engineering department. Lee
also served as the minister of science and technology. A considerable nu mber of KAIS’s
graduates received the Korea Science and Engineering Foundation’s Post -Doctoral
Overseas Training Fellowship. Along with this fellowship, 54 Korean Ph.D. went abroad
in 1982; 135 Ph.D. in 1986, and 200 in 1988. Most of them, 68%, went to the U.S. and
12% chose Japan; 7% to the UK, 5% to Canada, and 3% to Germany.66
In short, in the 1980s, there were two conflicting visions over educating KAIS’s students.
While the specialized technical program emphasized the importance of nurturing
tinkering ability to KAIS students with project-based courses, the accelerated education
program put more focus on maximizing students’ thinking capability with early and
accelerated courses. While the former oriented its course toward real industrial fields,
concerning performativity at practice, the latter oriented its education expecting
students to deepen their study with a post -doctoral training, concerning the creativity
of the brain. This conflict resulted from the lack of experience of setting up the
educational direction and system for the brain in South Korea. It was more about
education than research per se. As mentioned in the introduction, unlike the U.K., which
had a long tradition and culture in nurturing the brain in science and technology, South
Korea lacked the experience and knowledge about the way of educating and raising the
brain in science and technology. The particular understanding on the Nobel laureates in
KAIS’s report showed how insufficient knowledge the KAIS professors had in terms of
the history of science, and the way scientists carried out science in society. I n this
situation, conflicting visions and directions were developed which shaped the evolution

of KAIS in the 1980s. It showed the way the developing country muddle through to grasp
the nature of the brain while handling the brain drain problem.
In the end, the vision of early accelerated education won over the specialized technical
program. KAIS launched its undergraduate program in 1984. A considerable number of
KAIS undergraduates directly entered its graduate program, completing a Ph.D. at an
early age. In 1989, the domestic media reported the KAIS “as the birthplace of the Ph.D.
in 20s.”67 Compared to this, the specialized technical program was not expanded as
planned. Though the original plan was to launch seven specialized technical programs,
only three programs were launched in production engineering, chemical engineering,
and industrial engineering. Other than that, no further programs were initiated. In 1983,
even those three programs were closed. In the late 1980s, KAIS became the symbol of
accelerated education in science and technology in South Korea. A considerable number
of KAIS gr aduates went abroad for post -doctoral studies. With the Korea Science and
Engineering Foundation’s new Post -Doctoral Overseas Training Fellowship, the Korean
government generally relieved the regulation of studying abroad. In 1981, the
Regulations on Studying Abroad was signed by presidential executive order. It abolished
the qualification test and certification, thereby encouraging the outflow of the brain.
With the hope as well as the confidence of controlling the flow of the brain, the KAIS
intentionally encouraged the outflow of the brain with its accelerated education while
the Korean government lowered the barrier of its flow in the 1980s.

4. The Experimentation Stage

In the 1980s, the number of Korean students in the U.S. rapidly increased as shown in
[Figure 8-7] almost tripled from 6,150 in 1980 to 18,660 in 1985. The annual Korean
science and engineering Ph.D. recipients from U.S. institutions also increased from 131
students in 1980 to 323 students in 1985. The domestic media reported as the
reemergence of the brain drain problem yet it should be noted that it was the result of
delicate decision and choice. It was partly affected by the Korean government’s de -
regulation on studying abroad from the 1980s. In this context, between conflicting
visions over the kind of ability that KAIS would nurture, KAIS’s educational direction was
shaped in a way to emphasize accelerated studies supplementing further studies as a post-
doc. While, in the 1970s, at the KAIS’s emergence stage, the Korean government focused
on capturing the brain abroad, and locking up them within South Korea, from the 1980s, it
gradually unlatched the gate and let the brain flow though controlling its flow. It partly
stemmed from the confidence that South Korea in the 1980s would be able to control the
brain flow properly as it had been achieving remarkable economic growth: 7.25% GDP
growth in 1981, 13.38% in 1983; remind the [Figure 8-1] which showed higher brain drain
rate of lower-middle income countries than low-income countries.
[Figure 8-7] Korean Students and Ph.D. Recipients in America and Korea, 1960-93

Source: Song (1997)

===== PDF_PAGE 284 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
272
In the late 1980s, with the June Democratic Struggle, South Korea laid the foundation of
democratic society. Along with the continuing economic growth – 11.99% GDP growth
in 1988, 7.97% in 1989, 9.88% in 1990, and 10.78% in 199168 – the political stability came
to South Korea. In addition, with the fall of the Berlin Wall in 1989, the geopolitical
tension between North and South Korea was also relieved. On September 1990, the
South-North ministerial-level talk was held for the first time. Against its backdrop, in the
early 1990s, there was a few reports concerning the brain drain problem in South Korea.
Though the number of the brain who went abroad kept growing, so did the number of
the brain who returned to the country. Not just the economic development but also the
rise of democratic society as well as the relieved geopolitical tension in South Korea
played a role in bringing the brain back to the country. In 1993, the international science
exposition “Daejeon Expo” was also held. In 1994, Samsung released the world’s first
256M-DRAM chip. In the 1990s, both economically and culturally, the brain found South
Korea attractive than in 1970s. In 1997, South Korea joined the Organization for
Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) as its 29th member.
However, the harsh economic crisis hit South Korea in the late 1990s. In the middle of
the Asian financial crisis started from Thailand, Indonesia, Philippines with the rapid
outflow of foreign capital, the foreign exchange reserves of South Korea got to be
drained. In 1997, the Korean government received the International Monetary Fund
(IMF)’s bail-out package. In this context, a lot of companies including big companies like
Daewoo Group, SsangYong Group, and Haetae Group went bankrupt. Among top 30
companies in South Korea in the 1990s, 17 companies went bankrupt or dissolved. The
remaining companies also suffered greatly. It brought a huge confusion and shock in
Korean society.69 As South Korea had just entered to the OECD which symbolized the
rapid development of the nation, the receive of IMF’s bail -out package was not just an
economic but also cultural and social shock to South Korea. In this context, the brain
drain and the concern over it were raised again. In 1998, the domestic media reported

that “the advanced brain who went abroad this year exceeds 3000” which was “10 times
more than last year.”70 Another media also reported that “the brain which was attracted
to South Korea made a U-turn” and went back to their country.71
In this changing situation, KAIST adjusted itself in terms of its educational direction and
institutional strategy. In 1997, KAIST released a new report called “KAIST Re-Engineering
Report.” By addressing the term “re -engineering,” KAIST showed its willingn ess to
change its component, mechanism, and system. The “KAIST Re -Engineering Report”
stressed that “this is the time that the change and the repair are inevitable in order to
leap toward world’s top-10 university by accommodating rapidly changing domestic and
global environments.”72 It emphasized that, now, KAIST “could not settle for its 25 years
achievements.” From the late 1990s, KAIST sought for different strategies and
mechanisms in raising and circulating the brain in South Korea. In other words, from the
late 1990s, KAIST ente red to the stage of experimentation. The scope of KAIST’s re -
engineering project was wide. From education and research to welfare as shown in
[Figure 8-8], KAIST conducted an experiment.

In relation to the brain drain, the interesting impetus that KAIST pushed forward was
the consideration of the kind of the brain that KAIST would attract. So far, when KAIST
as well as the Korean government concerned on reversing the brain from outside the
country, the major target was the Korean brain, i.e. well -educated Koreans who leave
outside the country. There was less concern on the foreign brain per se, i.e. non-Korean
yet who is very talented. In 2001, however, KAIST decided to open its entrance to
foreigners. 73 More specifically, before 2001, it was possible for foreigners who
graduated from foreign universities could apply to KAIST. So, KAIST allowed foreigners
to apply for its graduate programs, but not for its undergraduate program. However, in
2001, KAIST revised its rule so that for those who graduated from domestic and foreign
high schools yet foreigners could also apply for KAIST. It meant the opening of KAIST’s
undergraduate entrance to foreigners. As a result, the first foreign undergraduate
student, Yan Long, who was a Chinese-Korean, entered to KAIST in 2003.74 The number
of foreign students in KAIST’s graduate program also got rapidly increased in 2000s.

While 77 foreigners entered to KAIST’s graduate program in 2003, there were 142
foreigners in 2004.75
In this context, in 2004, KAIST decided to elect a foreigner as its president. Robert
Laughlin, who was the professor of physics and applied physics at Stanford University
and won the Nobel Prize in physics in 1998 with Horst L. Störmer and Daniel C. Tsui ,
became the KAIST’s president in 2004. It was the first moment in which non -Korean
international scholar led KAIST as its president. By doing so, KAIST attempted to make it
as the center of knowledge not just within South Korea but also in a global world. With
this change, KAIST aspired to attract not only Korean brain but also diverse international
brain.
In the context of the government’s emphasis on the internationalization of the higher
education system in overall in 2000s, KAIST was in the forefront of its reformation.
Laughlin tried to make KAIST global by, for example, increasing the number of
international faculty, encouraging class in English, the diversification of funding of KAIST.
He also conceived to enlarge the number of students enrolled annually from 700 to 2000.
By doing so, he thought to establish not only science and engineering department but
also the department of law, art, pre -med, humanities et ac. His ultimate goal was to
transform KAIST from science oriented specialized school to full -fledged university. He
thought that, to attract diverse international brain, KAIST should offer divers e
intellectual stimulus by enlarging the kind of department and courses while expanding
the kind of students enrolled to KAIST. To this end, he sought to diversify the KAIST’s
finance in addition to the Korean government’s support. Laughlin took the Califo rnia
State University as a model for KAIST.76
However, Laughlin’s plan faced harsh opposition from KAIST’s professors. First of all, his
idea of diversifying the course of KAIST – including pre-law or pre -med courses – had
raised concern. For KAIST’s professors, the identity of KAIST was science and technology

75 Yo-Han Choi, “Foreign Students, Coming to Daeduk Research Park,” The Science Times, 28 April, 2005,
https://url.kr/vibpnj.
76 Ho-Jae Jung, “Honest Comments from Robert Laughlin, the President of KAIST,” Dong -Ah Il-bo, 25 October, 2005,
https://shindonga.donga.com/3/all/13/104912/1.

===== PDF_PAGE 288 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
276
oriented specialized university. Once KAIST expanded its area toward art, law,
humanities, medicine, etc, KAIST’s identity would get to be blurred; difficult to
differentiate from other universities. In addition, it would be difficult for KAIST to focus
on nurturing scientists and engineers as some of students would be distracted toward
other majors. However, Laughlin thought that the government or the institute should
not intervene the students’ education and career per se. Instead, the university should
just offer a wide range of excellent courses, let students explore, and find a few students
who want to major in science and technology. In Laughlin’s view, that’s the way the U.S.
has done for nurturing scientists and engineers in universities. Instead of concentration,
the diversification of course, students, finance, and professors were what Laughlin want
at KAIST.
In addition to the conflict over the vision of KAIST, Laughlin were in conflict with some
of KAIST’s professors due to his communication and management style. In the process
of drafting KAIST’s vision, including the expansion of enrolled students, he seldom made
a place for discussing his ideas with KAIST’s members. He made his vision and plan in his
office and just released it publicly. Thus, KAIST’s members criticized his communication
style as dogmatic and undemocratic. Yet, regarding this criticism, Lau ghlin often just
stated that it was inevitable as the reformation would be always followed by criticism.
It should be remembered that, as a foreigner, Laughlin was unfamiliar with Korean
culture and system. Then, it was needed for Laughlin to put effort in to learning local
culture and environment. Without this effort, his vision – regardless of how much good
it was – was difficult to be realized. Some of KAIST professors who even sympathized
with Laughlin’s vision on KAIST criticized his communication style and leadership.77
In the end, after a lot of controversies and conflicts, Laughlin failed to serve a second
term and his reformation was stranded. Laughlin’s rise and fall showed, on the one hand,
the attempt of KAIST to take the experimentation and reformation in the changing social
context, but, on the other hand, their confusion over how to make it with what by whom.
Laughlin’s vision emphasized t he importance of diversity in terms of education and

77 Yo-Sep Kim, “Laughlin, What on Earth Did He Wrong,” HelloDD, 6 April 2006,

students in order to make KAIST as a global university. While KAIST has been emerged
and evolved as science and technology-oriented school, in the experimentation stage, it
got challenged to be a full -fledged university; diversification instead of concentr ation.
What would it take for KAIST to attract not only Korean brain but also diverse
international brain? This was the question that KAIST dealt with in the stage of
experimentation. In addition, who would be able to lead this KAIST’s experimentation?
By foreigners? In the early 2000s, KAIST thought so and invited Laughlin, a Nobel -Prize
winner, as its president, but it turned out to be very contentious.
Laughlin left the KAIST in 2006 but the KAIST’s experimentation did not stop. In 2006,
the new president, Nam -Pyo Suh, came to KAIST. Nam -Pyo Suh was born in Korea in
1936 but he attained the citizenship in the U.S. in 1963. So, Suh was a Korean-American.
KAIST elected the foreigner again as its president, but, this time, searched for someone
who would be familiar with Korean culture and system. Before leaving the country in
1954, he went to middle and high -schools in South Korea. Then, he entered to the
Massachusetts Institute of Technology receiving his bachelor degree in mechanical
engineering; he did not experience Korean university system. After receiving Ph.D. in
Carnegie-Mellon University in 1964, he spent four years as a professor at the University
of South Carolina, then, in 1970, moved to Massachusetts Institute of Technology’s
department of mechanical engineering. There, Suh established his reputation as a
talented engineer serving, for example, the head of the MIT’s department of mechanical
engineering for ten years. As the foreigner born in Korea having achieved well -
established scientific career, Suh was chosen as the right person to carry out the
experimentation of KAIST.
After becoming the KAIST’s professor, Suh released his five -year development plan of
KAIST. Like Laughlin, Suh also emphasized to make KAIST as a global university. To this
end, Suh, on the one hand, strengthen the evaluation system of students and professors
and, on the other hand, broadened the qualification requirement to enter to the KAIST;
diversify KAIST’s students and professors while evaluating them with tough criteria. For
professors, Suh strengthened their promotion evaluation system including ten ure
examination. In 2007, among 38 professors who underwent tenure examination, 15
professors could not pass the examination which was a big shock to the Korean

===== PDF_PAGE 290 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
278
academic society.78 For students who had received the government’s scholarship – so,
tuition exemption with the support of the cost of living – Suh obligated them to receive
the certain GPA in minimum. Those who did not receive the minimum GPA had to pay a
certain amount of tuition. It contributed to spurring competition among students at
KAIST.79
In addition, he pushed forward making courses at KAIST in English. First, Suh obligated
professors to offer KAIST’s major courses in English, then made all the courses including
the liberal art classes in English. Again, it was an attempt to attract not only the Korean
brain but also the international brain. Yet, it raised a huge tension among KAIST
members. As English is not the first language for Koreans, both professors and students
found difficult to interact in class in English. Some of KAIST’s profess ors opposed Suh’s
initiative by saying that “100% English teaching in KAIST is a crazy thing.”80 They stressed
that “there are students who could not follow the class because of English [not because
of the class’s contents per se].” Students also found confusing. Some students sent a
letter or posted a hand-written poster urging for Korean class at KAIST.81 However, Suh
adhered to the opinion that 100% English class is necessary to make KAIST global, and
continued his initiative.
However, Suh’s experiment could not last long. In 2011, a series of students (as well as
a professor) killed themselves in a row. Though they might have different reasons, it
invoked a huge concern on the KAIST’s ongoing experiments on students. To make KAIST
global, Suh carried out several experiments like strengthening evaluation systems,
diversifying KAIST’s entrance exam, and encouraging 100% English class. Yet, it was
criticized as, first, spurring competitive environment instead of collaborative

environment at KAIST, second, unifying and standardizing KAIST’s classes despite the
diversification of entrance exam, and, third, enforcing impractical 100% English class
despite the opposition from KAIST’s members. Suh also struggled with communication
problem. For example, right after a series of tragic incidents in 2011, there was an
emergency meeting that students requested for Suh yet Suh not only came late but also
hardly communicated with students. It represented the way Suh communicated with
KAIST stakeholders.
In the end, in 2013, Suh left KAIST before his official term ended. It was the second time
that the foreigner president could not serve its term at KAIST. It indicated how difficult
it was for foreigner presidents to make a good rapport with KAIST members. To realize
some vision – regardless of how good it was – it was neces sary for those who carried
out experimentation to have a close communication and interaction with its members.
Without it, experimentation would be difficult to be achieved. To make KAIS T global –
in other words, to attract not only Korean brain but also international brain as the center
of knowledge in the world (not just in South Korea) – KAIST members in the 2000s used
to think that bring ing the leader outside the country would be important. Yet, after
undergoing Laughlin and Suh, they realized that KAIST’s experimentation should be
derived from inside KAIST; not from outside of the country. After Laughlin and Suh, no
foreigners were further elected as the president of KAIST. Neverthele ss, KAIST’s
experimentations to make it as the global center of knowledge have been still kept going
on.

280

5. Discussion & Conclusion

So far, this paper traced the way KAIST dealt with the brain drain problem in South Korea
from the 1970s to 2010s. To date many people remembered that KAIST ‘solved’ the
brain drain problem in the 1970s by offering unprecedented support and attracting the
brain from overseas. Yet, as this paper shows, the brain drain problem was not ‘solved,’
though it could be temporarily relieved in the 1970s. Instead, the brain drain problem in
South Korea kept rising over and over in the 1980s, in the 1990s, and even in the 2000s.
KAIST, on the one hand, contributed to outflowing the brain while also putting effort in
stimulating the inflow of the brain. In the changing sociopolitical contexts over the flows
of the brain in South Korea, KAIST has kept concerning and shap ing the way the brain
flows in/out of South Korea. This paper regarded KAIST as a medium to examine the
evolving ways of addressing the brain drain problem in South Korea. It divided KAIST’s
development into three stages: (1) emergence, (2) evolution, and (3) experimentation
stage.
At the emergence stage, KAIST focused on attracting the brain in abroad by offering
material incentive with the support of the USAID. In the context of changing domestic
as well as foreign context over the foreign aids, the USAID provided $6 million to
establish KAIST though the Korean government also provided the matching fund. It
made KAIST possible to offer unprecedented material support for those who would
come to KAIST. It should be noted, however, material incentive did not naturally draw
the brain in overseas. Continuous persuasion and negotiation processes were needed
which for example Chung played a role as a Korean yet a professor in the U.S. KAIST
emerged as the result of ‘new wine in new bottle strategy:’ a new institute in a non -
traditional ministry. By establishing KAIST under the supervision of Ministry of Science
and Technology - not the Ministry of Education which supervised most of the higher
education system in South Korea - the government enabled KAIST to use unprecedented
material incenti ves including salary, housing subsidies, and research support in
attracting the brain from overseas.

===== PDF_PAGE 293 =====
Chapter 8. STI Brain Drain and S&T University:
A Study on the Struggle of KAIST
281
Yet, the strategy of ‘a new wine in a new bottle’ had limitations. First, offering unusual
material incentive had a sustainability issue. The USAID’s support was planned to be
ended within five years. T he number of students and professors kept growing. How
would KAIST sustain its material incentive in this situation? In addition, partly due to
KAIST, there were increasing demands for increasing material support for traditional
universities as well. It should be remembered that, if using material incentive f or some
institute, it could raise a social tension among stakeholders as this paper showed, and it
would raise sustainability issue. From the late 1970s while addressing this problem in
the context of the changing political regime, different visions were shaped and collided
in deciding the way KAIST would evolve in the 1980s. Though, in South Korea, in the
context of rapidly growing economy in the 1970s and the 1980s, KAIST could sustain its
material support to both students/professors to certain degree, it would not always
happen to other countries.
The second problem KAIST faced in the evolution stage was the generation problem.
Though KAIST attracted talented brain from overseas in the emergence stage, the next
question remained: how they were gonna raise and nurture the next generation? Unlike
advanced countries, like U.K., where the term, brain drain, originally came from, South
Korea lacked the experience and culture in nurturing science and scientists. Thus, South
Korea could not but learning the brain while handling the brain drain problem. What
kind of the brain South Korea needed? What those brain needed in order to do brain
activities? What would be the role of KAIST and the Korean government? These were
the example questions that KAIST should have solved in its evolution stage. As this paper
revealed, amid handling these questions in the 1980s, the new flow of the brain was
shaped around KAIST.
As shown in [Figure 8-9], while at the emergence stage, KAIST focused on attracting the
brain from outside, at the evolution stage, KAIST intentionally made some outflow of
the brain by promoting postdoc education in abroad. With the launch of the gifted
education in the conflicting vision over it – technologist vs Ph.D. in 20s – KAIST began to
offer an early and accelerated education based on the belief that the brain’s creativity
would be maximized in their 20s; which was not quite true if seeing the history of science
more closely. In order to complement the accelerated education, KAIST encouraged

===== PDF_PAGE 294 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
282
students to conduct postdoc study in abroad along with the government’s de-regulation
on studying abroad in the 1980s. Thanks to the formation of the Korean Science and
Engineering Foundation’s Post -Doctoral Overseas Training Fellowship, which Lee who
was the professor of KAIST as well as the minister of science and technology played a
role, the controlled circulation of the brain was shaped at KAIST in the 1980s. Concerns
on the brain drain were raised in the 1980s yet, this time, it was more of the result of
intention and deliberation with the confidence of controlling it.
[Figure 8-9] The Emergence and Evolution Stage of KAIST

Source: Author

There was a downside of this strategy, though. As KAIST outsourced the postdoc study
in abroad, the domestic system for postdoc education lost the opportunity to grow. In
the 2000s – even currently – criticisms have pointed toward the poor postdoc system in
South Korea; see for example the report of “the size and characteristics of post -doc
researchers in South Korea” published in 2020.82
Though the aspiration of controlling the brain flow could work to certain degree in the
1990s at which South Korea could attain not only economic but also political and cultural

283
development – i.e., democratization movement, the host of Daejeon Expo, the invention
of 256M-DRAM chip by Samsung – it began to dismantle in the wake of IMF crisis from
the late 1990s. Again, the concern on the brain drain was highly reported in domestic
media. It alarmed the rapidly outflowing of the brain from South Korea to abroad. In this
situation, KAIST also readjusted itself. While at the emergence and evolution stage,
KAIST mostly concerned about the Korean brain, from the 2000s, not only the Korea n
but also various international brain began to be actively attracted. For example, by
revising the rule of its entrance, KAIST broadened the scope of students and the first
foreign undergraduate had entered to KAIST in 2003. Also, by inviting the foreigne r as
the president of KAIST, it carried out several experiments to make KAIST as the global
center of knowledge.
As shown in [Figure 8-10], KAIST’s experimentation stage was the stage at which not
only the quantity but also the diversity of the brain in the flow of the brain in South
Korea. While at the emergence and evolution stage, most of concerns over the brain
drain focused upon the number of the brain that went in or outside South Korea, at the
experimentation stage in the 2000s, KAIST took experiments to diversify the kind of the
brain including international brain in the campus. However, KAIST struggled with
figuring out, in what way, and, by whom, they should carry out experiments. It was also
related to the lack of experience and culture in nurturing the brain and its flow within
South Korea as the newly industrialized country. KAIST attempted to make it glob al by
bringing some global figures like Robert Laughlin, a Nobel prize winner in physics, and
Nam-Pyo Suh, a mechanical engineer from MIT. Yet, a series of struggles and failures of
those two presidents in completing their term showed the importance of dri ving
experiments from inside KAIST; instead from outside of the country. KAIST’s
experimentation is still going on nowadays.

===== PDF_PAGE 296 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
284
[Figure 8-10] The Experimentation Stage of KAIST

Source: Author

As shown in this paper, the brain drain problem was not what could be easily ‘solved’ in
the developing country. Despite the launch of the KAIST which offered privileged
support to attract the brain, the brain drain problem was not eliminated; though
temporarily resolved. In South Korea, the brain drain problem came over and over in
different forms. In this sense, it should be noted that, instead of trying to ‘solve’ the
brain drain problem, the better approach to this problem is to put effort in ‘caring’ the
brain flow. To this end, it is needed to (1) have a prepared mindset to keep watching the
brain flow since this problem would take long time (2) keep an eye on examining
changing local and global contexts that affected the circulation of the brain, and (3) keep
monitoring and caring the quantity as well as diversity of the brain flow. This would be
the first step in approaching the brain drain in developing country.
In this process, as emphasized in this paper, it is important to build the developing
country’s own experience and viewpoint in nurturing the brain and its flow. Unlike U.K.,
where the term, brain drain, came from, developing countries mostly lacked experience,
system, and culture in nurturing the brain per se. So, before talking about the problem
of brain drain, developing countries should take time in realizing more about the brain;
for example, who is the brain, what the brain wants, what kind of the bra in they are

===== PDF_PAGE 297 =====
Chapter 8. STI Brain Drain and S&T University:
A Study on the Struggle of KAIST
285
talking about, how to nurture the brain, what is the role of the society, and how they
are gonna answer those questions in what ways. These are the questions that
developing countries should grasp while dealing with the brain drain problem,
simultaneously. In addition, it should be underlined that these are not the questions
whose answers could be found in textbook or scholarly articles. Rather, answers to those
questions should be continuously found and shaped amid the specific local and global
context. So, the issue is whether the developing country is ready for ‘caring’ the flow of
the brain instead of ‘solving’ it.

1. Background

Research and Development (R&D) is a key factor to improve the national economy,
particularly R&D on science and technology (S&T). Policymakers have taken R&D and
S&T for innovation to strengthen commercial productivity. It aims to transform
economic structure to industrialization and modernization in achieving the sustainable
development goals (SDGs) by 2030.
In order to carry out the National Socio -Economic Development Plan for the 9th year
(2021-2025), in the past, the government has planned and expanded the science,
technology and innovation infrastructure of the country from the center down to the
local area so that people can access and use science, technology and innovation in life,
business and socio -economic development. Therefore, there is a need to establishing
and improve legal system and policies on science, technology and innovation to suit the
growth of the socio -economy particularly formulate National Master Plan for STI and
application in Lao PDR as one of the most important drivers of the 9th NSEDP
achievements.
In recent years, science and technology development has advanced rapidly. Therefore,
Lao PDR should seize this opportunity for socio -economic development, especially the
application of science and technology in production, services, and human resource

Chapter 9. Summary of the National
Master Plan Draft for Science,
Technology and Innovation R&D
and Application in Lao PDR

===== PDF_PAGE 302 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
290
development to achieve higher quality growth and to be able to compete at the regional
and international levels.
In Lao PDR, it is essential to develop the National Master Plan for Science, Technology
and Innovation Development and Application as well as the National Policy on Science
and Technology Research. The plans should focus on survey and study development
indicator of relevant sectors, human resource, science and technology research
innovation networking and infrastructure development. It is specially to increase
competitiveness of trade, capability of research, and industrial development to be more
innovative. Recently, there are several areas that are important for developing of
science and technology such as technology commercialization, small and medium-sized
enterprises (SME) innovation, international science, technology and innovation (STI)
Collaboration, etc.

 International environment
They are constrained for STI activities due to a lack of financing and a shortage of skilled
labor
With respect to resources, the government invests in human resource management by
establishing higher education institutes, vocational institutes, and skill development
institutes over the past decades. They have been increased in number, but limited
qualification
With respect to institutions, R&D implementation are at the early stage of STI
development, which focus on technology utilization rather than invention or creation.
There are numbers of research and education institutes, but applied research to
improve products and services is poor. Research based course is insufficient to build
research base and human capital. Research fund is limited
Despite these, in terms of R&D, policies lack of a term development, particularly in
science and technology research, and instrument are not clear and complex.
Implementation periods of R&D and S&T projects are not appropriate to support a
continuation of researches, which obstruct to accumulate knowledge and human capital.

===== PDF_PAGE 303 =====
Chapter 9. Summary of the National Master Plan Draft for Science,
Technology and Innovation R&D and Application in Lao PDR
291
Therefore, it is essential to modify research term. For example, short -term, mid-term,
and long -term projects could be determined as 1 year, 2 -3 years, and 4 -5 years,
respectively.

 Domestic environment
Despite many achievements in STI, it is still seen that the research, management and
use of research results in development is not yet as wide as it should be due to research
and development sectors are not deepening their responsibility for R&D.
The Lao government established the Ministry of Science and Technology (MOST) in 2011,
in regard with the decree number 309/PM, officially issued on September 28, 2011. It
indicated the government’s interest in fostering R&D . However, Lao PDR sought to
reduce the overlap of R&D policies. In February 2021, the government settled the MOST
and transferred its roles and responsibilities to Ministry of Education and Sports (MOES),
Ministry of Post and Telecommunications (MPT), and other relevant line ministries. In
October 2021, the government established the Ministry of Technology and
Communications (MTC) under the Decree on Organization and Activities of Ministry of
Technology and Communications, No.625/PM, officially issued on October 22, 2021. It
is responsible mostly for MOST’s responsibilities and roles in terms of governance and
management of technology, innovation, telecommunication, internet, post, and cyber
security.
Lao PDR’s 7th Socio -Economic Development Plan (2011 -2015) included the
development technology and promotion of technology transfer was one of the
significant elements in such development plan. The technology and technology
development for SMEs was supported under the Development Strategy of Science and
Technology (2013-2020) of the Ministry of Science and Technology (MOST).
A legal and policy framework supporting development of technology in universities, R&D
labs and support centers on IP rights exist has also been established by the Government
(ERIA and OECD, 2014).

===== PDF_PAGE 304 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
292
There is limitation on the financial support schemes funded by the government, donors,
and other organizations. The pilot public funds supporting R&D activities are also
provided with a commercial orientation and limited allocation.

country in 2020 (5 year NSEDP, period VIII, (2016-2022)
• Transforming Lao PDR to a Sustainable Lower Income Economy by 2030 to promote
R&D and application of STI into all sectors.

===== PDF_PAGE 309 =====
Chapter 10. Conclusion

297

Inkyoung SUN, STEPI

The K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR (2020-2022) was launched with the
title of the “National Master Plan for STI R&D and Application in Lao PDR” in the spring
of 2020. A couple of unexpected events hindered the project teams -STEPI and Ministry
of Science and Technology (MOST) in Lao PDR -of both countries from lively
communicating and operating the project, such as the COVID-19 pandemic and Laotian
government restructuring followed by the dissolution of the project counterpart MOST
in early 2021. Nevertheless, the project was successfully resumed with the Ministry of
Technology Communications (MTC) in the midst of the second year of the project 2021
thanks to the great support both from the Laotian Embassy in Korea and the Korean
Embassy in Lao PDR. Following the hybrid High-Level Meeting in September 2021, STEPI
conducted the STI Survey targeting civil servants studying in Korea and STI experts of
Lao PDR in order to identify top priorities and key STI challenges facing the Laotian
government that can be referenced for bilateral collaboration with Korea. According to
46 respondents, the Laotian government officials and STI experts stressed that the lack
of human resources and financial support and the limited international network are
critical challenges in STI development.
During a series of workshops and meetings conducted throughout the year 2022, our
bilateral research team explores several key questions on STI master planning to develop
the framework of the master plan; why is the STI master plan necessary in Lao PDR?
How will the master plan be different from existing policies and plans? What would be
the urgent and long-term issues to be solved by MTC t achieve the 9th National Socio -
economic Development Plan? How does MTC mobilize the financial and human

===== PDF_PAGE 310 =====
2022 K-Innovation Partnership Program with Lao PDR
298
resources to solve current challenges and implement the master plan? How will MTC
monitor and evaluate the implementation of the master plan?
In the course of examination of the national innovation system of Lao PDR and current
challenges facing MTC officials in developing STI policy, the initial suggestions were
made to align the development of the national STI master plan with DTI 5 -year
development plan and MTC policies and development plan (2021 -2025) under the
umbrella of the 9th National Socio -Economics Development Plan (2021 -2025). The
suggestions particularly apply to the vision and objectives sections of the master plan.
They also emphasize a clear direction and objectives of the plan and identification of key
players and available resources for actionable and effective implementation of the plan
in the future.
The suggestions from the Korean experts have been echoed in Chapter 1. Starting with
the concept and critical components of a national STI plan and its importance in the
context of national development, Chapter 1 provides the background on why and how
the Korean government switched the national development plan to the Science and
Technology Framework Plan. It covers the whole dynamics of the national STI
masterplan of Korea in rich from the development of rationales of the master planning,
organization of planning processes, structure of contents, and to implementation and
ex-post evaluation. The chapter may work as a key reference for the policy community
to re-visit and gain insights into STI policy planning and implementation.
Three strategic areas of the Master Plan were selected based on the analysis of the
national innovation system of Lao PDR, which was delivered in the 2021 project report,
with the consideration of the main tasks and roles of MTC as well as the result of the
survey: technology commercialization, SME innovation, and international S TI
collaboration. From Chapter 2 to Chapter 7, each chapter explores the international and
domestic environments of the designated strategic area and identifies challenges and
opportunities in the area by examining statistical data, governance, stakeholders and
required resources, and current policy issues of both countries.

===== PDF_PAGE 311 =====
Chapter 10. Conclusion

299
The proposed Master Plan presents its vision for 2030, “transforming Lao PDR to a
sustainable lower-income economy by 2030 through promoting R&D and application of
STI into all sectors.” Under the vision, three strategies are highlighted; technology
commercialization to promote the linkage of the supply and demand for technologies,
MSME innovation to develop MSMEs to generate demands for technologies, and
international STI cooperation to take advantage of the strengthening of S&T capacity.
Each strategy also delineates several objectives and actions of each strategic area in the
proposed Plan to achieve the vision. In terms of ‘learning by doing,’ this project is
especially meaningful for all participants to dedicate themselves to drafting the Master
Plan. It has been a great journey for all experts to learn mutually and deliberate on STI
policy challenges and solutions in the unique circumstance of Lao PDR.
While there are needs to be continuously updated and revised until the implementation
of the Plan, the Department of Technology Innovation (DTI) in MTC plans to use this
National Master Plan as a guide to creating a 5 -year development plan of DTI and to
vision a more active role of STI in the next 10th National Socio -Economic Development
Plan (2026-2030). In addition, the DTI is going to pursue the dissemination of the Plan
to relevant STI ministries, public and private institutions, and universities in ord er to
build a cooperation mechanism for STI development in Lao PDR. Despite the termination
of the K-Innovation program with Lao PDR this time, STEPI will continue supporting the
dissemination and implementation of the National STI Master Plan of Lao PDR a nd
further developing this valuable bilateral partnership between Korea and Lao PDR
